## Lesson 6 — Bodin's two surviving limitations

Progress: 6 / 38 | Stage: Core | Part II — the classical line, Bodin to Austin

━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
Book context: Core owner §6's Bodin entry, which records that the sovereign is above law but **not above duty and moral responsibility**, with the Advanced companion §5's note on fundamental-law and property restraints, read against the closing paragraph of the Bodin section at PDF p.181 (printed 164), which supplies the Salic law and the tax-consent limitations.
CA search: "Bodin fundamental laws Salic law taxation without consent sovereignty limits scholarship 2025 2026"
CA found: ⚠️ nothing returned that bears on the chapter's two named limitations. ❌ No linkage is carried into this lesson.
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

---

### 1. The sentence that makes Bodin interesting rather than merely early

⚠️ If Bodin had only asserted that the sovereign is above law, he would be a historical curiosity. ✅ What makes him a live figure in examination answers is the next sentence:

> Bodin treated the sovereign above law but not above duty and moral responsibility.
>
> — Gauba, Ch.7, PDF p.181 (printed 164)

⚠️ And then, unusually for a theorist of absolutism, he names two **concrete** limitations rather than leaving duty as a pious gesture.

### 2. The two limitations, and what kind of limitation each is

| # | ✅ Limitation as the chapter states it | ⚠️ Its source | ⚠️ What it protects |
|---|---|---|---|
| (a) | There are some **fundamental laws** — such as the **Salic law of France, which excluded females from dynastic succession** — and **the sovereign could not lawfully abrogate such laws** | The constitution of the realm itself | The continuity and identity of the sovereign office |
| (b) | **Private property, being granted by the law of nature, was inviolable**; therefore **the sovereign could not tax his subjects without their consent** | The **law of nature** | The subject's holdings against the sovereign's revenue needs |

🔑 Limitation (a) is **constitutional**: it concerns the rules by which the sovereign office is transmitted, and a sovereign who could alter those rules could destroy the very office he holds. 🔑 Limitation (b) is **natural-law based** and reaches into the sovereign's most practical activity — raising money.

⚠️ Taken together they produce the striking result that Bodin's absolute sovereign cannot change the succession and cannot levy a tax without consent. ⚠️ Those are not small matters. They are, historically, the two issues over which European monarchies most often collided with their estates.

### 3. The visual: where the limitations sit

```text
                          ABOVE
                            |
                 DUTY and MORAL RESPONSIBILITY
                            |
            FUNDAMENTAL LAWS  (e.g. Salic law of France)
             -- cannot be lawfully abrogated --
                            |
          LAW OF NATURE -> private property inviolable
             -- no taxation without consent --
                            |
     ================= THE SOVEREIGN =================
                            |
                 source of all ordinary law
                            |
                    ORDINARY POSITIVE LAW
                            |
                 citizens, subjects, associations
```

*Caption: Bodin's sovereign is above everything below the double line and below everything above it. ⚠️ Austin will later remove the upper tier entirely as a matter of **legal** theory, while conceding that it survives as a matter of moral practice.*

### 4. The tension inside Bodin, stated honestly

⚠️ There is a genuine difficulty here, and strong answers name it rather than hiding it.

```text
   CLAIM A : the sovereign is the SOURCE of law and is therefore
             above law      (the whole derivation of Lesson 5)

   CLAIM B : there are FUNDAMENTAL LAWS the sovereign CANNOT
             LAWFULLY ABROGATE

   If A is true, what makes the fundamental laws LAW?
   If B is true, in what sense is the sovereign the source of ALL law?
```

⚠️ Bodin's implicit answer, as the chapter's wording suggests, is that fundamental laws are not ordinary enactments but the conditions of the sovereign's own existence — constitutive rather than regulative. ⚠️ The sovereign is the source of the law he administers; he is not the source of the rule that made him sovereign. ⚠️ But this is close to reintroducing the medieval structure he had just abolished, with the Salic law in the role that the universal and eternal order used to occupy.

🔑 That unresolved tension is why the chapter can say, two pages later, that Austin **perfected** the theory. ⚠️ What Austin removes is precisely this residue.

### 5. Example, and the limit of the example

⚠️ Suppose a sovereign purports to alter the rule of succession so that his own line is excluded. ⚠️ On Bodin's account the act is not merely unwise but **unlawful**, because fundamental laws cannot be lawfully abrogated. ⚠️ Yet on his own premises, who could say so? There is no superior human authority to pronounce the act void.

❌ **Where the example stops.** ⚠️ It shows that Bodin's limitation is real as a *norm* and unenforceable as a *sanction* — exactly the structure of the six restraints in Lesson 3. ⚠️ Bodin's limitations and the chapter's later erosion clause are the same species of restraint: binding in conscience and in the long run, void of machinery in the short run.

### 6. Comparison: how four thinkers handle the limits on a legally supreme sovereign

| ✅ Thinker | ✅ Is the sovereign legally limited? | ✅ What restrains him instead |
|---|---|---|
| **Bodin** | ⚠️ Partly — **fundamental laws** and the natural-law protection of property | **Duty and moral responsibility** |
| **Hobbes** | ❌ No — the surrender is **complete, final and irrevocable** | ⚠️ Only the fear of returning to **anarchy**, which argues *against* challenge rather than for restraint |
| **Bentham** | ❌ No — sovereignty is **not limited by law** | ✅ **Moral limitations**, the **possibility of resistance**, and the demand for **useful legislation** promoting the **greatest happiness of the greatest numbers** |
| **Austin** | ❌ No — no **like superior** exists | ✅ The sovereign **habitually observes fundamental moral principles**, but is **not legally bound by any external authority** |

⚠️ Read along the second column and you watch legal limitation disappear from the theory between the sixteenth and the nineteenth centuries. ⚠️ Read along the third and you watch moral limitation survive in every one of them. ⚠️ That is the strongest single refutation of the claim that classical sovereignty theory is indifferent to morality — and the chapter makes it explicitly for Austin at PDF p.184.

### 7. Criticism and reply

⚠️ **Objection.** Bodin's limitations are self-serving. The Salic law protects dynastic continuity, which serves the monarch's family; the tax-consent rule protects property, which serves the propertied classes from whom Bodin came. A theory whose only two exceptions happen to protect the interests of the theorist's own order is ideology wearing the costume of logic.

✅ **Reply.** The genealogy does not touch the logic. ✅ The fundamental-law limitation follows from the structure of the office — a power that can abolish the rule constituting it is not perpetual, and perpetuity is one of the two adjectives in Bodin's own definition. ⚠️ The property limitation similarly follows from a premise the chapter attributes to him, that property is **granted by the law of nature** and therefore not the sovereign's to take; whether that premise is correct is a separate question from whether the inference is valid.

⚠️ **Residual.** The objection retains a sting the reply cannot draw. ⚠️ If exceptions can be generated whenever something is declared "fundamental" or "natural", the doctrine's absoluteness depends entirely on how short that list is kept, and Bodin offers no principle for keeping it short. ⚠️ Austin's solution — delete the list and relocate the restraint to morality, which makes no legal claim — is tidier, at the price of leaving the subject with **no legal right against the state**.

### 8. Using this in an answer

- ⚠️ **Syllabus relevance.** Examined stems ask whether Bodin's conception is **compatible with the social and political ideals of equality, justice and liberty**. The two limitations are the only textual material in this chapter that permits a nuanced answer rather than a flat denial.
- ⚠️ **Probable framing.** A compatibility stem should concede the two limitations, then state their weakness — no enforcement machinery, and a succession rule that is itself discriminatory.
- ❌ **Trap.** Presenting Bodin as a constitutionalist. ✅ He limits the sovereign in two places and leaves him legally unrestrained everywhere else.
- ✅ **Answer use.** Name the **Salic law** by name. A concrete instance converts a generic claim about "fundamental laws" into evidence.

### Revision notes

1. ✅ Bodin treated the sovereign **above law but not above duty and moral responsibility**. (PDF p.181)
2. ✅ Limitation (a): some **fundamental laws** — e.g. the **Salic law of France, which excluded females from dynastic succession** — which the sovereign **could not lawfully abrogate**.
3. ✅ Limitation (b): **private property, being granted by the law of nature, was inviolable**; therefore **the sovereign could not tax his subjects without their consent**.
4. 🔑 (a) is constitutional and protects the office; (b) is natural-law based and protects the subject.
5. ⚠️ Together they bar the two things early-modern monarchs most wanted: altering the succession and taxing at will.
6. ⚠️ The internal tension: if the sovereign is the source of *all* law, what makes the fundamental laws law?
7. ⚠️ The implied answer is that fundamental laws are **constitutive** of the office rather than enacted by it — a residue of the medieval structure Bodin had abolished.
8. ⚠️ Austin **perfected** the theory partly by deleting this residue.
9. ✅ Hobbes removes legal limitation entirely: surrender is **complete, final and irrevocable**.
10. ✅ Bentham keeps **moral limitations** plus the **possibility of resistance**.
11. ✅ Austin keeps moral practice — the sovereign **habitually observes fundamental moral principles** — without any legal limit.
12. ⚠️ Across four thinkers, legal limitation vanishes and moral limitation survives in every one.
13. ⚠️ Residual: with no principle restricting what counts as "fundamental", absoluteness depends on keeping the list short, and Bodin supplies no such principle.

### Concept check

**Question:** Identify the internal tension created by Bodin's fundamental-law limitation, state the best reconciliation available on the chapter's own wording, and say what the reconciliation costs.

**Model answer:** ⚠️ The tension is that Bodin makes the sovereign **the source of all law** and simultaneously holds that there are **fundamental laws** he **could not lawfully abrogate** — which asks us to accept laws the law-source did not make and cannot unmake. ✅ The best reconciliation available on the chapter's wording is that such laws are **constitutive** rather than enacted: the Salic law governs the transmission of the sovereign office itself, so a sovereign empowered to abolish it could terminate his own perpetual authority, contradicting the **perpetual** half of his own definition. ⚠️ The cost is that this reinstates, in miniature, the very structure Bodin abolished — a law standing above the ruler which he finds rather than makes, with the Salic law occupying the place the universal and eternal order used to hold. ✅ That residue is one reason the chapter treats Austin, who removes the exception altogether and relocates restraint to morality, as having **perfected** the legal theory.

**Misconception to avoid:** ❌ Do not resolve the tension by saying the limitations are "merely moral". ⚠️ The chapter's word is **lawfully** — the sovereign **could not lawfully abrogate** fundamental laws — so the limitation is stated in legal and not merely ethical terms, which is precisely what makes it awkward for the doctrine.

### Lesson Mains practice

**Mains question [10]:** "Bodin's sovereign is absolute everywhere except where it matters most." Examine this claim. *(Examine — answer in about 150 words.)*

**Mains model:** ⚠️ The claim is half right and instructive where it is wrong. ✅ Bodin's two exceptions do strike at practical essentials: **fundamental laws** such as the **Salic law of France** cannot be **lawfully abrogated**, and since **private property, being granted by the law of nature, was inviolable**, the sovereign **could not tax his subjects without their consent** — succession and taxation being the two issues over which monarchs most often collided with their estates. ⚠️ But the claim overstates, because neither exception has machinery. ⚠️ There is no superior human authority to declare an abrogation void, so the limitation binds in duty and **moral responsibility**, which is exactly where the chapter locates it. ⚠️ Everywhere else the sovereign remains **unrestrained by law**, is the **source of law** and may command **whether residing in one person or several**. 🔑 Absolute in machinery, limited in conscience.

**Rubric [10]:** both limitations stated with the Salic law named **3** · the natural-law ground of the property limitation **2** · the *could not lawfully abrogate* wording preserved **1** · the enforcement gap identified as the reason the claim overstates **2** · the *above law but not above duty and moral responsibility* formulation **1** · a verdict that neither simply accepts nor simply rejects the quoted claim **1**.

---

## Lesson 7 — Grotius: the external face

Progress: 7 / 38 | Stage: Core | Part II — the classical line, Bodin to Austin

━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
Book context: Core owner §6's Grotius entry — the fullest single entry in that section — and §5's internal/external distinction, with the Advanced companion §5's second thinker entry, read against the **Hugo Grotius (1583-1645)** section spanning PDF pp.181-182 (printed 164-165), which supplies the two foundations of international law and the completion claim.
CA search: "Grotius father of international law voluntary law of nations consent sovereignty 2025 2026"
CA found: ⚠️ nothing returned that bears on the chapter's seventeenth-century material, and the external-sovereignty developments that do surface belong to Chapter 9. ➡️ Routed to **Topic 12**. ❌ No linkage is carried into this lesson.
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

---

### 1. The problem Grotius inherits

⚠️ Bodin has produced a sovereign who is the source of law and unrestrained by law. ⚠️ Now ask the obvious follow-up question: if every state's sovereign is unrestrained by law, how can there be any law *between* states? ⚠️ Either international law binds sovereigns — in which case they are not sovereign — or it does not bind them, in which case it is not law.

🔑 That dilemma is the whole of Lesson 7. ✅ Grotius's achievement, as the chapter presents it, is to dissolve it rather than to pick a side.

### 2. Who he is, and when

✅ The chapter's placement is precise:

> After about half a century of Bodin's enunciation of the theory of sovereignty, Grotius, a Dutch jurist, widely known as the father of international law, made an important contribution to the concept of sovereignty. He brought out the implications of sovereignty of the state in the international sphere, i.e. independence of the sovereign state from foreign control.
>
> — Gauba, Ch.7, PDF p.181 (printed 164)

| ✅ Fact | ✅ Detail |
|---|---|
| Dates | **1583-1645** |
| Nationality and profession | **Dutch jurist** |
| Standing | **widely known as the father of international law** |
| Distance from Bodin | **about half a century** after Bodin's enunciation |
| Contribution | the implications of sovereignty **in the international sphere** — **independence of the sovereign state from foreign control** |

### 3. The two foundations, and why there must be two

✅ Grotius builds international law on **two distinct foundations**, and the chapter letters them:

```text
  (a) NATURAL LAW / "the dictate of right reason"
      Nations are subject to it IN THE SAME MANNER AS CITIZENS,
      because the society of nations is formed on the SAME
      FUNDAMENTAL MORAL PRINCIPLES which guide the behaviour of
      men in society.
                    |
                    |  supplies CONTENT: there are standards
                    |  that hold whether or not anyone agreed
                    v
  (b) A "VOLUNTARY" LAW OF NATIONS based upon their FREE CONSENT
          explicit  -> treaties and conventions
          implicit  -> usages and customs
                    |
                    |  supplies OBLIGATION without subordination:
                    |  the bond is self-assumed
                    v
      INTERNATIONAL LAW that binds sovereigns without
      creating a superior over them
```

⚠️ Each foundation alone would fail. ⚠️ Natural law alone would make states subject to a standard they did not choose, which looks like the superior authority sovereignty denies. ⚠️ Consent alone would make international law a bundle of revocable promises with no moral content. ⚠️ Together they give a law that has content *and* does not subordinate.

### 4. The decisive reframing

✅ The chapter states the consequence in a sentence worth memorising verbatim in substance:

> He made it clear that nations respected international law not because it curtailed their sovereignty, but because they voluntarily chose to do so, of their free consent.
>
> — Gauba, Ch.7, PDF p.182 (printed 165)

🔑 This is the **consent manoeuvre**, and it reappears throughout the topic. ⚠️ It is the same move the chapter uses at PDF p.185 for diplomatic immunity — a state grants ex-territoriality **because of its free consent, not because of any external obligation or restriction**, which makes immunity **a matter of international courtesy, not of compulsion**. ⚠️ And it is Garner's **except by its own will** applied to the external face.

⚠️ The manoeuvre is powerful and has a known cost. ⚠️ If every international obligation is self-assumed, then in principle every one is withdrawable, and the binding quality of international law rests on the continuing will of the bound party. ⚠️ Laski's external front — **the scientific fact of world-interdependence** — is an attack on exactly this, and Chapter 9 takes the attack further. ➡️ That continuation belongs to **Topic 12**.

### 5. What Grotius shares with Bodin, and what he adds

✅ The chapter's summary sentence does both jobs at once:

> Thus, Grotius, like Bodin, attributed moral responsibility to sovereignty, but he extended it to the external sphere also.
>
> — Gauba, Ch.7, PDF p.182 (printed 165)

| ⚠️ Element | ✅ Bodin | ✅ Grotius |
|---|---|---|
| Moral responsibility attached to sovereignty | ✅ Yes — above law, **not above duty and moral responsibility** | ✅ Yes — and **extended to the external sphere** |
| Sphere addressed | Internal: the sovereign and his subjects | External: the state and other states |
| Source of the restraint | Fundamental laws; the law of nature protecting property | Natural law as **the dictate of right reason**; the voluntary law of nations |
| Mechanism of obligation | The sovereign's own duty | The state's **free consent**, explicit or implicit |

✅ And the completion claim, which is the sentence to carry into an answer: **In this way, Grotius introduced the idea of external sovereignty to the existing idea of internal sovereignty, and thus gave it a fuller shape.**

### 6. Example, and the limit of the example

⚠️ Take a state that ratifies a treaty and then performs it faithfully for decades. ⚠️ On Grotius's account its compliance is not evidence that its sovereignty has been curtailed; it is evidence of sovereignty exercised — the state bound itself, and keeping a self-assumed bond is an act of the same will that assumed it. ⚠️ The same applies to unwritten practice: the chapter's **implicit** consent, **as expressed in usages and customs**.

❌ **Where the example stops.** ⚠️ It cannot distinguish a state that complies because it consented from a state that complies because non-compliance would be ruinous. ⚠️ From the outside the two look identical, and the Grotian account classifies both as free consent. ⚠️ That indistinguishability is precisely what Laski exploits when he says that **legally unlimited power turns out to be power exercised under conditions fairly well known to each generation**.

### 7. Criticism and reply

⚠️ **Objection.** The consent foundation makes international law parasitic on the sovereign's will, so it cannot explain the cases that matter — a state that repudiates a treaty, or that was never a party to a custom. If obligation ends where consent ends, international law is a description of current agreement, not a law at all.

✅ **Reply.** The second foundation answers it. ✅ Grotius does not rest on consent alone: nations are subject to **natural law or 'the dictate of right reason' in the same manner as citizens**, because the society of nations is formed on **the same fundamental moral principles** that guide men in society. ⚠️ So repudiation may be legally effective within the voluntary layer while remaining wrong at the natural-law layer, exactly as an individual may exercise a legal power immorally.

⚠️ **Residual.** The reply secures content at the price of enforcement. ⚠️ Natural law, on the chapter's own boxed definition at PDF p.190, is **discovered through moral intuition and by the application of human faculty of reasoning** — which supplies no determinate authority to adjudicate between two states each invoking right reason against the other. ⚠️ The chapter notes the standing of natural law and does not claim to solve the adjudication problem.

### 8. Using this in an answer

- ⚠️ **Syllabus relevance.** Grotius is the only point inside this topic's range where external sovereignty is given a positive theory rather than merely named. Any stem touching the international dimension that must stay inside Chapters 7-8 lands here.
- ⚠️ **Probable framing.** A stem comparing the internal and external faces, or asking how sovereign states can be bound by international law, is answered by the two foundations plus the consent manoeuvre.
- ❌ **Trap.** Treating Grotius as a critic of sovereignty. ✅ He **extends** it; the chapter's verb is **gave it a fuller shape**.
- ✅ **Answer use.** Pair Grotius's consent manoeuvre with the later immunity example at p.185 to show that one device does duty in two places — it reads as pattern recognition rather than recall.

### Revision notes

1. ✅ **Grotius (1583-1645)**, **Dutch jurist**, **widely known as the father of international law**, writing **about half a century** after Bodin.
2. ✅ His contribution: the implications of sovereignty **in the international sphere** — **independence of the sovereign state from foreign control**.
3. ✅ Foundation (a): nations are subject to **natural law or 'the dictate of right reason' in the same manner as citizens**, because the **society of nations is formed on the same fundamental moral principles** guiding men in society.
4. ✅ Foundation (b): a **'voluntary' law of nations based upon their free consent** — **explicit as expressed in treaties and conventions**, or **implicit as expressed in usages and customs**.
5. 🔑 The consent manoeuvre: nations respect international law **not because it curtailed their sovereignty, but because they voluntarily chose to do so, of their free consent**.
6. ⚠️ Natural law supplies content; consent supplies obligation without subordination. Neither works alone.
7. ✅ **Grotius, like Bodin, attributed moral responsibility to sovereignty, but he extended it to the external sphere also.**
8. ✅ The completion claim: he **introduced the idea of external sovereignty to the existing idea of internal sovereignty, and thus gave it a fuller shape**.
9. ⚠️ The same manoeuvre recurs at p.185: diplomatic immunity is granted **because of its free consent**, a matter of **international courtesy, not of compulsion**.
10. ⚠️ Cost of the manoeuvre: a self-assumed obligation is in principle withdrawable.
11. ⚠️ The account cannot distinguish compliance from consent and compliance from necessity — Laski's opening.
12. ⚠️ Natural law is **discovered through moral intuition and by the application of human faculty of reasoning** (boxed definition, p.190), which leaves adjudication unresolved.
13. ❌ Grotius is not a critic of sovereignty; ✅ he is the theorist who completes it.

### Concept check

**Question:** A state signs a treaty limiting what it may do within its own territory. Does it thereby lose sovereignty? Answer using Grotius as the chapter presents him, and state the weakness of that answer.

**Model answer:** ✅ On the chapter's Grotius, no. ✅ Nations respect international law **not because it curtailed their sovereignty, but because they voluntarily chose to do so, of their free consent**, consent being **explicit as expressed in treaties and conventions**. ⚠️ A self-assumed obligation is an exercise of the sovereign will, not a subtraction from it, which is Garner's **except by its own will** applied externally; the chapter uses the identical device for diplomatic immunity, which it calls a matter of **international courtesy, not of compulsion**. ✅ The commitment is not therefore empty, because the second foundation holds nations to **natural law or 'the dictate of right reason'** independently of agreement. ⚠️ The weakness is that the account cannot distinguish genuine consent from compliance under conditions that make refusal ruinous, and provides no authority to adjudicate when two states each invoke right reason. ⚠️ Laski presses exactly this when he writes that **legally unlimited power turns out to be power exercised under conditions fairly well known to each generation**.

**Misconception to avoid:** ❌ Do not treat the two foundations as alternatives, choosing one. ⚠️ The chapter presents them as jointly necessary: consent alone leaves international law without content, natural law alone subordinates the sovereign to a standard it did not choose, and only the pair yields obligation without a superior.

### Lesson Mains practice

**Mains question [10]:** How does Grotius make international obligation compatible with state sovereignty? *(Explain — answer in about 150 words.)*

**Mains model:** ✅ By splitting the foundation of international law in two. ✅ First, nations are subject to **natural law or 'the dictate of right reason' in the same manner as citizens**, because the **society of nations is formed on the same fundamental moral principles** that guide men in society — this supplies standards independent of agreement. ✅ Second, he recognises a **'voluntary' law of nations based upon their free consent**, whether **explicit as expressed in treaties and conventions** or **implicit as expressed in usages and customs** — this supplies obligation without a superior. ✅ The result is the reframing that matters: nations respect international law **not because it curtailed their sovereignty, but because they voluntarily chose to do so**. ⚠️ The device is the external twin of Garner's **except by its own will**, and the chapter reuses it for diplomatic immunity. ⚠️ Its weakness is that consent and necessity look identical from outside. 🔑 Bound, but by oneself.

**Rubric [10]:** both foundations stated with their lettered content **4** · the explicit/implicit sub-division of consent **1** · the not-curtailed-but-chosen reframing **2** · the link to the *except by its own will* structure or to the immunity parallel **2** · the consent/necessity weakness named **1**.

## Lesson 8 — Hobbes: origin and function

Progress: 8 / 38 | Stage: Core | Part II — the classical line, Bodin to Austin

━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
Book context: Core owner §6's Hobbes entry and §4's spine, with the Advanced companion §5's third thinker entry and §8's comparative matrix row for Hobbes, read against the **Thomas Hobbes (1588-1679)** section at PDF p.182 (printed 165) and the inalienability discussion at PDF p.186 (printed 169), which together supply the contract, the surrender and the two-part contribution.
CA search: "Hobbes Leviathan sovereign order peace security contemporary debate 2025 2026"
CA found: ⚠️ nothing returned that bears on the chapter's presentation of Hobbes. ❌ No linkage is carried into this lesson.
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

---

### 1. What Hobbes adds that Bodin could not

⚠️ Bodin gives sovereignty a *definition* and a *source of law*. ⚠️ What he does not give is an account of why anyone should accept it. ⚠️ A sovereign who is above law because he is the source of law has been described; he has not been justified.

🔑 Hobbes's contribution, on the chapter's reading, is **justification by two independent routes** — and the chapter is unusually explicit that there are two:

> Hobbes's chief contribution to the theory of sovereignty consists in adding an element of legitimacy to authority of the sovereign, because he held: (a) that the sovereign is the product of the will of the people; and (b) that the sovereign enjoys his supreme authority for its functional value, that is by virtue of providing order, peace and security in the place of anarchy and oppression.
>
> — Gauba, Ch.7, PDF p.182 (printed 165)

| ⚠️ Route | ✅ Hobbes's ground | ⚠️ What kind of justification it is |
|---|---|---|
| (a) | The sovereign is **the product of the will of the people** | **Genetic** — legitimate because of how it came to be |
| (b) | The sovereign holds authority **for its functional value**, by **providing order, peace and security in the place of anarchy and oppression** | **Consequentialist** — legitimate because of what it delivers |

⚠️ A theory that offers both is sturdier than one that offers either, because each covers the other's failure case: a sovereign whose origin is murky may still justify itself by performance, and a sovereign performing badly may still claim the authority its origin conferred. ⚠️ It is also, for the same reason, harder to dislodge — which is the pluralists' complaint.

### 2. The contract, stated as the chapter states it

✅ The chapter's sequence is compact and should be learned in order:

```text
  STATE OF NATURE
  "characterized by law of the jungle"
  the right of the stronger to oppress the weaker
            |
            |  men EMERGE from it to attain
            v
  ORDER, PEACE and SECURITY
            |
            |  to this end they
            v
  CREATE A SOVEREIGN
            |
            |  surrendering ALL their natural rights
            v
  SURRENDER IS "COMPLETE, FINAL AND IRREVOCABLE"
            |
            |  because defying the sovereign would mean
            v
  REVERSION TO THE STATE OF NATURE
  => authority of the sovereign is placed BEYOND CHALLENGE
```

✅ Two of the chapter's own formulations deserve exact retention. ⚠️ First, the natural rights surrendered are glossed as **the right of the stronger to oppress the weaker** — which makes the surrender far less costly than it sounds, since what is given up is a licence rather than a protection. ⚠️ Second, the rhetorical question the chapter attributes to the argument: **How can they ask for restoration of their natural rights once they have become civilized men!**

### 3. The structure of the argument, and its single point of failure

```text
  P1  Without a sovereign, life is governed by the law of the jungle.
  P2  Men create a sovereign in order to obtain order, peace and security.
  P3  Any challenge to the sovereign risks dissolving the sovereign.
  P4  Dissolving the sovereign returns men to the law of the jungle.
  C   Therefore no challenge to the sovereign is rational,
      and his authority is beyond challenge.
```

⚠️ The chain is valid. Its vulnerability is **P3**, and it is a factual premise dressed as a logical one. ⚠️ Whether *any* challenge risks dissolution — as opposed to a particular challenge risking a particular change of government — is an empirical claim, and the whole constitutional tradition is built on denying it. ✅ The chapter effectively denies it too, at PDF p.189, when it insists on **institutional mechanisms** and **inbuilt checks and safeguards** so that **no organ of the government is able to become omnipotent**.

🔑 Laski makes the same denial the centre of his pluralism at PDF p.202, where the value of the state/government distinction is said to be **the possibility it offers of creating institutional mechanisms for changing the agents of the state... when the latter shows itself inadequate to its responsibilities**. ⚠️ Changing the government without dissolving the state is precisely the possibility Hobbes's P3 excludes.

### 4. Hobbes on inalienability — a second appearance

⚠️ Hobbes returns at PDF p.186 in the discussion of inalienability, and the point is subtle enough to be worth isolating.

✅ The chapter's argument there is that the creation of the Hobbesian sovereign is **not a case of sovereignty being transferred from people to the sovereign, because in the state of nature sovereignty did not exist at all**.

⚠️ This is an elegant rescue. ⚠️ If the people had held sovereignty and handed it over, sovereignty would be alienable, and inalienability — one of the five characteristics — would fail at its first historical instance. ⚠️ By insisting that the state of nature contains no sovereignty, the chapter makes the contract an act of **creation** rather than **conveyance**.

⚠️ The cost is that it widens the distance between Hobbes and Rousseau. ⚠️ For Rousseau, as the chapter says on the same page, **sovereignty is throughout held by the general will, although powers may be delegated to the government** — so there the people never part with anything either, but for the opposite reason: they had it and kept it, rather than never having had it.

### 5. Example, and the limit of the example

⚠️ Consider a community emerging from prolonged disorder that accepts a strong authority it would not have tolerated before. ⚠️ Hobbes explains the acceptance exactly: the authority's legitimacy comes both from having been willed and from what it supplies — **order, peace and security in the place of anarchy and oppression**.

❌ **Where the example stops.** ⚠️ It makes the Hobbesian case at its strongest moment, the moment of emergency, and says nothing about the ordinary case where disorder is remote and the question is whether *this* government's commands deserve obedience. ⚠️ Chapter 8 is built on precisely the ordinary case: the dictates are issued by **human beings who may be wise or foolish, benevolent or selfish, virtuous or vicious — but in no case perfect, divine and infallible**.

### 6. Comparison: the two contract theorists on the same page

| ⚠️ Axis | ✅ **Hobbes** | ✅ **Locke** |
|---|---|---|
| What the people surrender | **All their natural rights** — glossed as the right of the stronger to oppress the weaker | **Some** of their natural rights, on condition that **life, liberty and property** are protected |
| Finality of the surrender | **Complete, final and irrevocable** | ⚠️ Conditional — the people **remain supreme throughout** |
| Who ends up supreme | The created sovereign | The **people**, who **came into existence before the formation of the state** |
| Status of the ruler | Sovereign, beyond challenge | **Ruler or government could not possess supreme power** |
| Purpose of the state | **Order, peace and security** | **Protection of these vital rights** |

⚠️ The two are sometimes taught as a single "social contract" position. ⚠️ On the chapter's account they reach opposite conclusions from a shared method, which is what makes the comparison examinable.

### 7. Criticism and reply

⚠️ **Objection.** A surrender that is **complete, final and irrevocable** cannot be grounded in the will of the people, because a will that can bind itself for all time has effectively been abolished at the moment it acted. Hobbes's route (a) therefore destroys itself: consent is invoked once and then permanently disabled.

✅ **Reply.** The chapter's Hobbes can answer that the disabling is the whole point and is itself consented to. ⚠️ Men consent not merely to an authority but to an *irrevocable* authority, because a revocable one would leave the threat of the state of nature standing and so fail route (b) — it would not actually supply **order, peace and security**. ⚠️ The irrevocability is thus not an accident of the contract but its delivered good.

⚠️ **Residual.** The reply is coherent and still troubling, and the chapter itself says why. ⚠️ At PDF p.189 it observes that in the real world sovereignty must be **vested in human beings who, being mortals, are as imperfect as any other human beings**, and that absolute, perpetual, unaccountable authority leads to **the most cruel and degenerate forms of autocracy, tyranny and authoritarianism**. ⚠️ If irrevocability is the delivered good, the deliverable includes the risk of tyranny, and Hobbes offers no way to price that risk.

### 8. Using this in an answer

- ⚠️ **Syllabus relevance.** Hobbes supplies the strongest available justification of absolute authority and therefore the best foil for any stem on the compatibility of sovereignty with liberty or democracy.
- ⚠️ **Probable framing.** A stem on the social contract and sovereignty is answered by the two-route legitimacy structure, not by a narrative of the state of nature.
- ❌ **Trap.** Writing that Hobbes's sovereign is imposed on the people. ✅ The chapter is explicit that **the state was a product of the will of the people**.
- ✅ **Answer use.** Route (b) — functional value — is the most transferable sentence in the lesson; it converts an argument about origins into an argument about performance, which every evaluative stem rewards.

### Revision notes

1. ✅ **Hobbes (1588-1679)**, English political thinker, **further developed** the concept of sovereignty in the seventeenth century. (PDF p.182)
2. ✅ As an exponent of the **social contract**, he argued that **the state was a product of the will of the people**; sovereignty is an attribute of the state, its character determined by the terms of the contract.
3. ✅ The state of nature is **characterized by law of the jungle**; men form a state to attain **order, peace and security**.
4. ✅ They **surrender all their natural rights** — glossed as **the right of the stronger to oppress the weaker**.
5. ✅ The surrender is **complete, final and irrevocable**.
6. ✅ Defying the sovereign **would mean reversion to the state of nature**, so his authority is placed **beyond challenge**.
7. 🔑 Two-route legitimacy: (a) the sovereign is **the product of the will of the people**; (b) he enjoys authority **for its functional value**, providing **order, peace and security in the place of anarchy and oppression**.
8. ⚠️ (a) is genetic, (b) is consequentialist; each covers the other's failure case.
9. ⚠️ The weak premise is that **any** challenge risks dissolution — an empirical claim doing logical work.
10. ✅ At p.186 the chapter denies that the contract transfers sovereignty: **in the state of nature sovereignty did not exist at all**, so this is creation, not conveyance — which saves **inalienability**.
11. ⚠️ Rousseau reaches the same no-transfer result by the opposite route: sovereignty is **throughout held by the general will**.
12. ⚠️ Chapter 7's own appraisal at p.189 contradicts Hobbes's conclusion by demanding **inbuilt checks and safeguards** so that **no organ of the government is able to become omnipotent**.
13. ⚠️ Laski's state/government distinction turns on the very possibility Hobbes excludes — changing the government without dissolving the state.

### Concept check

**Question:** The chapter credits Hobbes with "adding an element of legitimacy" to sovereign authority. Set out the two grounds of that legitimacy, explain why offering two is stronger than offering one, and identify which ground Chapter 8 attacks.

**Model answer:** ✅ The grounds are (a) that **the sovereign is the product of the will of the people**, and (b) that he **enjoys his supreme authority for its functional value, that is by virtue of providing order, peace and security in the place of anarchy and oppression**. ⚠️ (a) is genetic and (b) consequentialist, and offering both is stronger because each covers the other's failure case: an authority whose origin is contested may still be justified by what it delivers, and one performing badly may still invoke its origin. ✅ Chapter 8 attacks ground (a). ⚠️ Laski's second prompting factor is that the state's claim to pre-eminence **always means, in fact, the sovereignty of a government composed of fallible men whose intentions alone are not a sufficient justification for so vast a claim** — that is, the will that created the sovereign does not transfer its authority to whichever body of officials currently speaks in its name. ⚠️ Ground (b) survives the attack in weakened form, since pluralists concede the state the functions of internal order, external defence and contract enforcement.

**Misconception to avoid:** ❌ Do not say Hobbes rests sovereignty on force. ⚠️ The chapter's claim is the reverse: his specific addition to the theory is **legitimacy**, and the force in his account is what the sovereign replaces, not what the sovereign is.

### Lesson Mains practice

**Mains question [10]:** "For Hobbes the irrevocability of the surrender is not a flaw in the contract but its product." Examine. *(Examine — answer in about 150 words.)*

**Mains model:** ⚠️ The statement is defensible on the chapter's own account. ✅ Men leave a state of nature **characterized by law of the jungle** to obtain **order, peace and security**, and to that end **create a sovereign**, surrendering **all their natural rights** — the **right of the stronger to oppress the weaker** — in a surrender that is **complete, final and irrevocable**. ⚠️ A revocable surrender would leave the threat of reversion permanently available, so it would not supply the security that was the object; irrevocability is therefore what is bought, not an overcharge. ✅ This also secures Hobbes's second ground of legitimacy, **functional value**. ⚠️ The cost is severe and the chapter states it: sovereignty must be vested in **mortals** who are **as imperfect as any other human beings**, and unaccountable authority invites **autocracy, tyranny and authoritarianism**. 🔑 The contract buys security by disabling the buyer.

**Rubric [10]:** the state of nature and the three ends stated in the chapter's terms **2** · the content of the surrender, with the stronger/weaker gloss **2** · the three-adjective irrevocability formula **1** · the argument that irrevocability is the delivered good, not a defect **3** · the mortal-sovereign cost drawn from the chapter's appraisal **1** · a verdict rather than a summary **1**.

---

## Lesson 9 — Locke: the people's retained supremacy

Progress: 9 / 38 | Stage: Core | Part II — the classical line, Bodin to Austin

━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
Book context: Core owner §6's Locke entry and §5's state/government distinction, with the Advanced companion §5's fourth thinker entry and §8's matrix row for Locke, read against the **John Locke (1632-1704)** paragraph at PDF p.182 (printed 165) and the inalienability treatment at PDF p.186 (printed 169).
CA search: "Locke natural rights life liberty property supreme power of the people political theory 2025 2026"
CA found: ⚠️ nothing returned that bears on the chapter's paragraph on Locke. ❌ No linkage is carried into this lesson.
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

---

### 1. A short paragraph with a long consequence

⚠️ Locke receives one paragraph in this chapter — markedly less than Bodin, Grotius, Hobbes, Rousseau or Austin. ⚠️ Do not read the brevity as unimportance. The paragraph contains the first outright **denial** in the chapter that the state possesses absolute sovereignty, and everything the pluralists later say about the limits of state authority runs through its logic.

✅ The chapter's whole statement:

> John Locke (1632-1704), another English exponent of the theory of the social contract, did not concede absolute sovereignty of the state. He thought that the supreme power in society was held by the people which came into existence before the formation of the state. The natural rights of life, liberty and property were retained by the people and the state was created for the protection of these vital rights. Ruler or government could not possess supreme power.
>
> — Gauba, Ch.7, PDF p.182 (printed 165)

### 2. Four claims, and the order they depend on

```text
  [1]  THE PEOPLE "came into existence BEFORE the formation of the state"
                              |
                              |  therefore
                              v
  [2]  SUPREME POWER IN SOCIETY is held by the people
                              |
                              |  and since the people retained
                              v
  [3]  NATURAL RIGHTS of LIFE, LIBERTY and PROPERTY
                              |
                              |  and the state exists only
                              v
  [4]  FOR THE PROTECTION of these vital rights
                              |
                              v
       => "RULER or GOVERNMENT could not possess SUPREME POWER"
```

🔑 Claim [1] does all the work, and it is a claim about **temporal and logical priority**. ⚠️ If a people exists as a people before there is a state, then whatever authority the state has is derivative; and a derivative authority cannot be supreme in the sense the classical theory requires. ⚠️ Compare Hobbes, for whom there is no organised people before the sovereign — only a multitude in the state of nature — which is exactly why his sovereign *can* be supreme.

⚠️ Claim [4] converts the state into an **instrument**. An instrument is assessed by whether it performs its function, which is the seed of every later demand that the state **justify its claim to authority** — the heading the chapter itself uses at PDF p.197.

### 3. The chapter's own use of Locke at the inalienability stage

⚠️ Locke returns at PDF p.186, and the return is not decorative; it closes a hole in the doctrine.

✅ The chapter argues that when Locke **postulates surrender of some of the natural rights of the people to the state on the condition that their fundamental natural rights to 'life, liberty, and property' shall be protected, it is not a question of transfer of sovereignty, because the people themselves remain supreme throughout, in spite of the formation of the state.**

⚠️ Set this beside the parallel Hobbes and Rousseau arguments on the same page and a pattern emerges:

| ✅ Thinker | ⚠️ Why the contract does not alienate sovereignty |
|---|---|
| **Hobbes** | Sovereignty **did not exist at all** in the state of nature; the contract **creates** rather than transfers |
| **Locke** | The people **remain supreme throughout**; only **some** rights are surrendered, and conditionally |
| **Rousseau** | Sovereignty is **throughout held by the general will**, **although powers may be delegated to the government** |

🔑 Three different metaphysics, one shared conclusion: **sovereignty is inalienable**. ⚠️ The chapter is using the three contract theorists to defend a characteristic none of them set out to defend, which is an argumentative move worth naming in an answer.

### 4. The distinction Locke forces into the open

⚠️ Locke's final sentence — **ruler or government could not possess supreme power** — is the first appearance in the chapter of the distinction that eventually governs the whole topic.

```text
        THE STATE                    |          THE GOVERNMENT
   abstract, continuing              |   human, changeable, fallible
   the bearer of sovereignty         |   the holder of assigned powers
                                     |
   Locke p.182: people supreme,      |   "Ruler or government could not
   state created to protect rights   |    possess supreme power"
                                     |
   Ch.7 p.185: "sovereignty belongs  |   "governments may be formed and
   to the state"                     |    dissolved according to
                                     |    established procedure"
                                     |
   Ch.7 p.189: sovereignty an        |   "only assigned definite powers,
   attribute of the state            |    to be exercised through an
   "conceived as an abstract idea"   |    established procedure with
                                     |    inbuilt checks and safeguards"
                                     |
   Ch.8 p.202 (Laski): the state's   |   "government is but the agent of
   purpose                           |    the state"
```

⚠️ Notice that the distinction appears four times in two chapters, in four different argumentative roles: with Locke it limits the ruler; at p.185 it explains **permanence**; at p.189 it is the remedy for the mortal-sovereign problem; at p.202 it is Laski's instrument for making government accountable. 🔑 A candidate who can trace one idea through four functions is demonstrating exactly the understanding the Mains format rewards.

### 5. Example, and the limit of the example

⚠️ Suppose a government enacts a measure that destroys the security of property for a class of holders. ⚠️ On Locke's premises this is not merely bad policy; it is the instrument acting against the purpose for which it was constituted, and the supreme power — the people — has not thereby lost its supremacy, because it never parted with it.

❌ **Where the example stops.** ⚠️ Locke's framework tells you that such a government has failed; it does not tell you what follows procedurally. ⚠️ The chapter does not develop Locke's account of the remedy. ➡️ Resistance, political obligation and the right to disobey belong to a different owner, and this file does not reconstruct them. ⚠️ What the chapter *does* supply is Bentham's version — sovereignty **limited by the possibility of resistance**, with **conditions under which resistance was morally justified** — which is taken up at Lesson 11.

### 6. Comparison: where supremacy sits, across the first five thinkers

| ✅ Thinker | ✅ Locus of supremacy | ✅ Status of the ruler/government |
|---|---|---|
| **Bodin** | The sovereign law-maker, **one person or several** | ⚠️ Above law; bound only by duty, fundamental laws and the natural-law protection of property |
| **Grotius** | The state, in both spheres | ⚠️ Morally responsible, externally independent by consent |
| **Hobbes** | The **created sovereign** | ✅ Beyond challenge; the surrender is **complete, final and irrevocable** |
| **Locke** | The **people**, who pre-exist the state | ✅ **Could not possess supreme power** |
| **Rousseau** | The **people in their corporate capacity**, through the **general will** | ✅ **Government is but an agent of the general will**; it **cannot logically claim to hold sovereignty** |

⚠️ Read the column of loci downward and the chapter's narrative becomes visible: supremacy migrates from the law-maker to the created sovereign, and then back to the people by two different routes. ⚠️ Austin will later move it again — not to a person but to a **determinate human superior** defined by a behavioural test.

### 7. Criticism and reply

⚠️ **Objection.** Locke's position is not a theory of sovereignty at all but a refusal of one. If the people are supreme and the government is not, and if the people are not organised into a determinate body that can command, then there is no identifiable authority from which law proceeds — which is the very gap Bodin's argument was designed to close.

✅ **Reply.** The chapter's framework can absorb the objection through the later legal/political distinction. ⚠️ Locke is denying that the *ruler* is sovereign, not denying that the legal order has a final law-making authority; Dicey's formulation at p.189 gives both a place — **behind the sovereign which the lawyer recognizes there is another sovereign to whom the legal sovereign must bow**. ⚠️ On that reading Locke is an early statement of the political sovereign.

⚠️ **Residual.** The reply is a reconciliation rather than a refutation, and it imports a distinction Locke did not draw. ⚠️ MacIver later makes the hard version of the objection in his own name: even in a democratic state **it is very difficult to locate the ultimate sovereign**, because **there is no homogeneous will of the people** and majority and minority opinions **exist simultaneously**. ⚠️ If that is right, the locus Locke relies on is not merely unorganised but indeterminate.

### 8. Using this in an answer

- ⚠️ **Syllabus relevance.** Locke supplies the cleanest textual basis within this chapter for the claim that sovereignty and limited government are compatible.
- ⚠️ **Probable framing.** Any stem on sovereignty and rights, or on sovereignty and democracy, should use Locke's priority claim — the people **came into existence before the formation of the state** — as the pivot.
- ❌ **Trap.** Writing that Locke rejected sovereignty. ✅ He rejected **absolute sovereignty of the state** and denied supreme power to **ruler or government**; the chapter's words are precise and so should yours be.
- ✅ **Answer use.** Use the three-column table in §3. Showing that the chapter deploys Hobbes, Locke and Rousseau jointly to establish inalienability demonstrates reading of the argument, not of the summary.

### Revision notes

1. ✅ **Locke (1632-1704)**, **another English exponent of the theory of the social contract**, **did not concede absolute sovereignty of the state**. (PDF p.182)
2. ✅ The **supreme power in society was held by the people which came into existence before the formation of the state**.
3. ✅ The natural rights of **life, liberty and property** were **retained by the people**.
4. ✅ **The state was created for the protection of these vital rights.**
5. ✅ **Ruler or government could not possess supreme power.**
6. 🔑 Priority of the people is the load-bearing claim: a derivative authority cannot be supreme in the classical sense.
7. ⚠️ Purpose-built authority is instrumental authority, and instruments are assessed by performance — the seed of **the state must justify its claim to authority** (p.197 heading).
8. ✅ At p.186 Locke's contract is said not to transfer sovereignty **because the people themselves remain supreme throughout, in spite of the formation of the state**.
9. ⚠️ Hobbes, Locke and Rousseau are used jointly to establish **inalienability** by three different routes.
10. ⚠️ Locke is the first appearance of the **state/government** distinction, which recurs at pp.185, 189 and 202 in four different roles.
11. ⚠️ Contrast with Hobbes: no organised people precedes the Hobbesian sovereign, which is why that sovereign *can* be supreme.
12. ⚠️ Objection: an unorganised people is not a determinate law-source — the gap Bodin closed.
13. ⚠️ MacIver's hard version at p.204: even in a democracy **it is very difficult to locate the ultimate sovereign**, since **there is no homogeneous will of the people**.

### Concept check

**Question:** Explain how a single claim in Locke's paragraph makes the denial of absolute state sovereignty unavoidable, and show how the same claim is used elsewhere in the chapter for a purpose Locke did not intend.

**Model answer:** ✅ The claim is that **the supreme power in society was held by the people which came into existence before the formation of the state**. ⚠️ If the people pre-exist the state, the state's authority is derivative, and a derivative authority cannot satisfy a definition requiring that it **cannot be legally bound except by its own will or limited by any other power than itself**; hence the conclusion that **ruler or government could not possess supreme power** follows directly rather than as a separate value judgment. ✅ The chapter then reuses the same priority claim for a different purpose at p.186, in the defence of **inalienability**: Locke's surrender of **some** rights is held not to transfer sovereignty, **because the people themselves remain supreme throughout, in spite of the formation of the state**. ⚠️ Locke's point was to limit rulers; the chapter's use is to protect a characteristic of the classical doctrine — and it pairs Locke with Hobbes and Rousseau to do so.

**Misconception to avoid:** ❌ Do not summarise Locke as holding that the people surrendered their rights to the state. ⚠️ The chapter is careful on both quantity and condition: **some** of the natural rights are surrendered, **on the condition** that life, liberty and property are protected, and the fundamental rights are **retained**.

### Lesson Mains practice

**Mains question [10]:** How does Locke's account of the origin of the state limit the sovereignty of the government? *(Discuss — answer in about 150 words.)*

**Mains model:** ✅ By making the people prior. ✅ Locke held that **the supreme power in society was held by the people which came into existence before the formation of the state**, that **the natural rights of life, liberty and property were retained by the people**, and that **the state was created for the protection of these vital rights**. ⚠️ Two limits follow. ⚠️ First, an authority created by a pre-existing people is derivative, so it cannot be legally unlimited in the sense the classical definition requires; the chapter accordingly records that Locke **did not concede absolute sovereignty of the state**. ⚠️ Second, an authority created *for a purpose* is instrumental and is measurable against that purpose — the ground on which pluralists later require the state to justify its special powers. ✅ Hence **ruler or government could not possess supreme power**. ⚠️ The limit is conceptual: no remedy is developed. 🔑 Prior people, derivative state, limited government.

**Rubric [10]:** the priority claim stated exactly **2** · retained rights named as life, liberty and property **2** · the protective purpose of the state **2** · the derivative-authority inference **2** · the instrumental-authority inference, linked forward to the justification demand **1** · the honest note that the chapter supplies no remedy **1**.

## Lesson 10 — Rousseau: the people in corporate capacity

Progress: 10 / 38 | Stage: Core | Part II — the classical line, Bodin to Austin

━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
Book context: Core owner §6's Rousseau entry and §3's definition of popular sovereignty, with the Advanced companion §5's fifth thinker entry, read against the **Jean Jacques Rousseau (1712-78)** section spanning PDF pp.182-183 (printed 165-166) and the boxed definition of **General Will** at PDF p.183, which supply the two-capacity contract, the agent-government claim and the forced-to-be-free formula.
CA search: "Rousseau general will popular sovereignty government as agent contemporary political theory 2025 2026"
CA found: ⚠️ nothing returned that bears on the chapter's eighteenth-century material. ❌ No linkage is carried into this lesson.
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

---

### 1. The move nobody else in the chapter makes

⚠️ Bodin relocated law's source. Hobbes justified the sovereign. Locke denied the ruler supremacy. ⚠️ Rousseau does something structurally different: he keeps the full apparatus of absolute sovereignty and **changes who holds it**.

✅ The chapter marks the contrast exactly:

> While another chief exponent of the social contract theory, viz. Hobbes had created a sovereign apart from the people, Rousseau made the people themselves sovereign.
>
> — Gauba, Ch.7, PDF p.182 (printed 165)

🔑 This is the single most examinable sentence on Rousseau in the topic, because it explains why popular sovereignty is **not** a weakening of sovereignty. ⚠️ Rousseau's sovereign is, if anything, more absolute than Hobbes's: it is **absolute and unlimited** because the **supreme direction and control of the general will is accepted by the people unconditionally in the interests of their freedom**.

### 2. The two-capacity contract

✅ The device that makes this possible is a contract with an unusual pair of parties:

```text
        THE SAME HUMAN BEINGS, TWICE OVER
                        |
       +----------------+-----------------+
       |                                  |
  as INDIVIDUALS                     as a CORPORATE BODY
  "the people in their               "the people in their
   individual capacity"               corporate capacity"
       |                                  |
   bearers of the                     bearer of the
   PARTICULAR WILL                    GENERAL WILL
       |                                  |
       +----------------+-----------------+
                        |
      the social contract is concluded BETWEEN THESE TWO
                        |
        => sovereignty is RETAINED by the people in their
           CORPORATE capacity, represented by the GENERAL WILL
        => their ACTUAL will is SUBORDINATED to their REAL will
```

⚠️ The contract is therefore not a transaction between ruler and ruled, nor between individuals and a third party. ⚠️ It is a people contracting with itself under two descriptions. ⚠️ That is why nothing is handed over and nothing can be reclaimed: there is no second party to hold the surrendered thing.

### 3. The three consequences the chapter draws

✅ The chapter draws out three implications in quick succession, and each is separately examinable:

| # | ✅ Consequence | ⚠️ Why it matters |
|---|---|---|
| 1 | **Sovereignty belongs to the people**; it **could be exercised only in an assembly of the whole people** | Sets up the scale problem the chapter raises later: this is **direct democracy**, workable only in a small, uniform community |
| 2 | **Government is but an agent of the general will which reigns supreme**; **a government cannot logically claim to hold sovereignty** | The sharpest statement in the chapter of the state/government distinction, and the standing correction to the belief that an elected government is the sovereign |
| 3 | Sovereignty is **absolute and unlimited**, because the general will's **supreme direction and control... is accepted by the people unconditionally in the interests of their freedom** | Popular sovereignty inherits absoluteness rather than diluting it |

🔑 Consequence 2 is the one examiners most often test by implication. ⚠️ Whenever a stem suggests that "in a democracy the elected government is sovereign", the answer begins here: the chapter says a government **cannot logically claim** sovereignty, and the word **logically** matters — this is not a recommendation about good practice but a claim about what the concept permits.

### 4. The boxed definition, read closely

✅ The chapter supplies a boxed definition of the key term:

> **General Will.** According to J.J. Rousseau (1712-78), a French philosopher, the common denominator of the real will of the people which embodies not only their common interest but also everybody's real and long-term interest. Man realizes his freedom by submitting his particular will to the direction of the general will.
>
> — Gauba, Ch.7, boxed definition, PDF p.183 (printed 166)

⚠️ Four elements, each doing separate work:

1. ⚠️ **Common denominator** — not a sum, not a majority, but what is shared when particular differences are factored out. ⚠️ This is why the general will is not the will of all.
2. ⚠️ **Of the real will** — the general will is built from a *refined* version of individual willing, not from raw preference.
3. ⚠️ **Not only common interest but also everybody's real and long-term interest** — the doctrine denies that there is a conflict between the individual's genuine good and the community's.
4. ⚠️ **Man realizes his freedom by submitting** — freedom is redefined as the condition of acting on one's real will, which makes submission a route to liberty rather than a cost of it.

⚠️ Element 4 is where the famous slogan comes from, and the chapter reports it at p.183 in exactly this context: **Rousseau went to the extent of saying that the 'people can be forced to be free'.**

### 5. Example, and the limit of the example

⚠️ The chapter supplies its own worked example at PDF pp.191-192 and it is deliberately mundane — a driver who wants to jump a red light at a crossing. ⚠️ Taken in isolation, the driver's momentary interest is to go; taken as a member of a community of drivers, his genuine interest is a rule he also obeys, because **when all are self-disciplined, there will be a smooth flow of traffic**, which **will ensure safety of one and all**.

⚠️ The example is well chosen because the gain from the rule is visible, universal and uncontroversial, and because the restraint is light.

❌ **Where the example stops, and it stops early.** ⚠️ Traffic is a coordination problem in which everyone's interest genuinely converges once a rule exists. ⚠️ Most political questions are **distributive**, not coordinative: they allocate burdens and benefits between groups whose interests do not converge. ⚠️ In those cases the claim that a dissenter is being returned to his own real will is far harder to sustain — and the chapter itself notes the danger at p.193, warning that confusing the two grounds of popular sovereignty has **opened the floodgates of absolutism and totalitarianism**.

### 6. Comparison: Rousseau against Hobbes, on four axes

| ⚠️ Axis | ✅ **Hobbes** | ✅ **Rousseau** |
|---|---|---|
| Where the sovereign comes from | Created **apart from the people** | Created **out of the people themselves** |
| What sovereignty serves | **Self-interest of the individual** | The **common interest** |
| Ground of justification | Order, peace, security — functional | **Moral grounds** |
| Status of government | Sovereign authority dominates the structure | **Agent** only; **cannot logically claim to hold sovereignty** |

✅ The first three rows are the chapter's own contrast at PDF p.191: **Hobbes made sovereignty an instrument of serving self-interest of the individual; Rousseau made it an instrument of serving the common interest. Rousseau justified his doctrine of popular sovereignty on moral grounds.**

⚠️ Note what the two share. Both keep sovereignty **absolute**, both make it **inalienable**, and both derive it from an act of the people. ⚠️ The disagreement is about the *holder* and the *purpose*, not about the *attributes*.

### 7. Criticism and reply

⚠️ **Objection.** "Forced to be free" is the point at which the theory becomes dangerous. If a person's expressed preference can be overridden on the ground that it is not his *real* will, then any coercion can be redescribed as liberation, and the redescription is made by whoever currently controls the state.

✅ **Reply.** The chapter's Rousseau has a defence with two parts. ⚠️ First, the general will is defined as the **common denominator of the real will** of all members — not as the view of a ruler, a party or a majority — so a government imposing its own programme is not applying the general will but counterfeiting it. ⚠️ Second, Rousseau's own institutional requirement blocks the abuse: sovereignty **could be exercised only in an assembly of the whole people**, and **government is but an agent**. ⚠️ A doctrine that denies the government any share in sovereignty cannot easily be used by a government.

⚠️ **Residual.** The chapter does not let Rousseau off, and the residual is its own. ⚠️ At p.193 it observes that Rousseau **has not drawn a clear distinction between 'will' and 'reason'**: reason concerns **right and wrong** and is **subject to prolonged debate**, whereas will **is supposed to be neutral** and **can be ascertained empirically**. ⚠️ Once the general will is treated as a *superior* will, the doctrine slides from the empirical question *what do they will?* into the evaluative question *what should they will?*, and so **enters the realm of idealist theory which is often invoked in support of absolutism**. ⚠️ Hence the chapter's verdict: **Rousseau's philosophy must be taken with a note of caution.**

### 8. Using this in an answer

- ⚠️ **Syllabus relevance.** Rousseau is the bridge between the classical theory and popular sovereignty, and the source of the most frequently tested correction in the topic — that an elected government is not the sovereign.
- ⚠️ **Probable framing.** Stems on whether a given theory is compatible with democracy will use Rousseau as the test case; stems on popular sovereignty will require the general will's definition verbatim in substance.
- ❌ **Trap.** Equating the general will with the will of the majority. ✅ The chapter's term is **common denominator of the real will**, and MacIver's criticism at p.204 depends on noticing that the two are different.
- ✅ **Answer use.** The sentence **a government cannot logically claim to hold sovereignty** is worth deploying in any answer touching democracy, federalism or executive power.

### Revision notes

1. ✅ **Rousseau (1712-78)**, French exponent of the social contract, introduced the **doctrine of popular sovereignty** in the eighteenth century. (PDF p.182)
2. ✅ **Hobbes had created a sovereign apart from the people; Rousseau made the people themselves sovereign.**
3. ✅ The contract is concluded **between the people in their individual capacity on the one hand, and the people in their corporate capacity on the other**.
4. ✅ Sovereignty is **retained by the people themselves in their corporate capacity, represented by the general will**, while their **actual will is subordinated to their real will**.
5. ✅ Sovereignty **could be exercised only in an assembly of the whole people**.
6. 🔑 **Government is but an agent of the general will which reigns supreme**; a government **cannot logically claim to hold sovereignty**.
7. ✅ Sovereignty remains **absolute and unlimited** because the general will's supreme direction is **accepted by the people unconditionally in the interests of their freedom**.
8. ✅ **Rousseau went to the extent of saying that the 'people can be forced to be free'.**
9. ✅ Boxed definition: the general will is **the common denominator of the real will of the people**, embodying **not only their common interest but also everybody's real and long-term interest**; **man realizes his freedom by submitting his particular will to the direction of the general will**.
10. ✅ At p.191: **Hobbes made sovereignty an instrument of serving self-interest of the individual; Rousseau made it an instrument of serving the common interest**, justified **on moral grounds**.
11. ⚠️ Rousseau and Hobbes agree on absoluteness and inalienability and disagree on holder and purpose.
12. ⚠️ The traffic example works because traffic is a coordination problem; distributive questions do not converge in the same way.
13. ⚠️ The chapter's own residual: **will** and **reason** are not clearly distinguished, so the doctrine **enters the realm of idealist theory which is often invoked in support of absolutism** — hence **a note of caution**.

### Concept check

**Question:** Why does the chapter say that a government "cannot logically claim to hold sovereignty" on Rousseau's premises? Identify the premise that makes the denial logical rather than merely normative.

**Model answer:** ✅ The premise is the two-capacity contract: it is concluded **between the people in their individual capacity on the one hand, and the people in their corporate capacity on the other**, so that sovereignty is **retained by the people themselves in their corporate capacity, represented by the general will**. ⚠️ Because both parties to the contract are the same people under two descriptions, nothing is transferred to any third body; there is no moment at which a government could acquire what it would need in order to be sovereign. ✅ The government's status is accordingly fixed by definition — it is **but an agent of the general will which reigns supreme**. ⚠️ The denial is therefore logical and not merely a recommendation about good practice: on these premises a sovereign government is not undesirable but unconstructable. ⚠️ The chapter's own corollary at p.186 is that this is also why sovereignty is **inalienable** for Rousseau — **powers may be delegated to the government**, but sovereignty is not among them.

**Misconception to avoid:** ❌ Do not treat popular sovereignty as a *limitation* on sovereignty. ⚠️ Rousseau keeps it **absolute and unlimited**; he changes the holder, not the attributes, which is why the chapter can present him inside the classical line rather than against it.

### Lesson Mains practice

**Mains question [10]:** "Rousseau did not weaken sovereignty; he relocated it." Examine. *(Examine — answer in about 150 words.)*

**Mains model:** ✅ The relocation is explicit: **Hobbes had created a sovereign apart from the people; Rousseau made the people themselves sovereign**, through a contract concluded between the people **in their individual capacity** and the people **in their corporate capacity**. ✅ The attributes survive the move. ⚠️ Sovereignty remains **absolute and unlimited**, because the general will's supreme direction is **accepted by the people unconditionally in the interests of their freedom**; it remains **inalienable**, since **powers may be delegated to the government** while sovereignty is not; and it acquires a new rigour in the claim that a government **cannot logically claim to hold sovereignty**. ⚠️ What changes is holder and purpose: **Hobbes made sovereignty an instrument of serving self-interest of the individual; Rousseau made it an instrument of serving the common interest**, justified **on moral grounds**. ⚠️ The relocation carries its own risk, which the chapter names as a **note of caution**. 🔑 Same powers, new hands.

**Rubric [10]:** the Hobbes/Rousseau relocation sentence **2** · the two-capacity contract **2** · at least two retained attributes, with the unconditional-acceptance ground for absoluteness **3** · the agent-government consequence **2** · the chapter's own caution acknowledged **1**.
