## Lesson 11 — Bentham: utility, moral limits and resistance

Progress: 11 / 38 | Stage: Core | Part II — the classical line, Bodin to Austin

━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
Book context: Core owner §6's Bentham entry, with the Advanced companion §5's sixth thinker entry and §8's matrix row recording **legal sovereign, utility-tested**, read against the **Jeremy Bentham (1748-1832)** paragraph at PDF p.183 (printed 166), which supplies the law/morals split, the resistance clause and the useful-legislation demand.
CA search: "Bentham utilitarianism sovereignty moral limits right of resistance political theory 2025 2026"
CA found: ⚠️ nothing returned that bears on the chapter's paragraph on Bentham. ❌ No linkage is carried into this lesson.
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

---

### 1. A paragraph that is usually skipped, and should not be

⚠️ Bentham gets three sentences in this chapter. ⚠️ They contain the only explicit statement in the classical line that **resistance can be morally justified**, and the only explicit standard by which a sovereign's legislation is to be judged. ⚠️ Skipping Bentham costs a candidate the ability to answer "is absolute sovereignty compatible with X?" with anything other than the pluralists.

✅ The chapter's whole statement:

> Bentham, the famous English Utilitarian, argued that sovereignty was not limited by law, but was subject to moral limitations. Bentham maintained that sovereignty was limited by the possibility of resistance, and there were conditions under which resistance was morally justified. He, therefore, insisted that the sovereign should justify his authority by useful legislation with the object of promoting the greatest happiness of the greatest numbers.
>
> — Gauba, Ch.7, PDF p.183 (printed 166)

### 2. Three claims, three different kinds of limit

```text
   CLAIM 1   "not limited by LAW, but subject to MORAL limitations"
                     |
                     +--> a limit of STANDARD
                          there is a measure the sovereign can fail

   CLAIM 2   "limited by the POSSIBILITY OF RESISTANCE,
              and there were CONDITIONS under which resistance
              was MORALLY JUSTIFIED"
                     |
                     +--> a limit of CONSEQUENCE  (it may happen)
                          AND a limit of JUSTIFICATION (it may be right)

   CLAIM 3   "should JUSTIFY his authority by USEFUL LEGISLATION
              with the object of promoting the GREATEST HAPPINESS
              OF THE GREATEST NUMBERS"
                     |
                     +--> a limit of PURPOSE
                          authority is held on terms, and the terms
                          are performance terms
```

🔑 Claim 2 is doing two jobs at once and most summaries notice only the first. ⚠️ "Possibility of resistance" is a *prudential* limit — a sovereign who provokes revolt may lose power. ⚠️ "Conditions under which resistance was morally justified" is a *normative* limit — the revolt may be right. ⚠️ Bentham is asserting both, and only the second is philosophically interesting.

### 3. Where this sits between Bodin and Austin

| ⚠️ Question | ✅ **Bodin** | ✅ **Bentham** | ✅ **Austin** |
|---|---|---|---|
| Legal limits on the sovereign? | ⚠️ Two — fundamental laws; property and consent to taxation | ❌ None — **not limited by law** | ❌ None — no **like superior** |
| Moral limits? | ✅ **Duty and moral responsibility** | ✅ **Moral limitations** | ✅ Habitually observes **fundamental moral principles** |
| A standard of good exercise? | ⚠️ Reason, inside the definition of the state | ✅ **The greatest happiness of the greatest numbers** | ❌ None supplied — the theory is formal |
| Resistance? | ❌ Not addressed in this chapter | ✅ **Morally justified** under conditions | ❌ Not addressed as a right |

🔑 Read across the Bentham column and you have the most *demanding* of the three positions on substance and the most *permissive* on form. ⚠️ He concedes everything Austin concedes about legal unlimitedness, and then imposes a performance test Austin never states.

⚠️ This is why the chapter places him immediately before Austin. ⚠️ The sequence is not chronological padding: Bentham shows what a legally unlimited sovereignty looks like when it is still held to an external standard, and Austin shows what it looks like when the standard is removed from the legal theory altogether.

### 4. The internal difficulty, stated plainly

⚠️ There is a strain in Bentham's position that strong answers name.

```text
   If sovereignty is NOT LIMITED BY LAW ...
        ... then the sovereign's enactment is valid whatever it contains.

   If resistance is sometimes MORALLY JUSTIFIED ...
        ... then there are enactments that ought not to be obeyed.

   => A citizen may be under a VALID legal obligation to do
      what he is MORALLY JUSTIFIED in refusing to do.
```

⚠️ Bentham, as the chapter presents him, accepts this result rather than evading it, and the acceptance is the point: the separation of legal validity from moral merit is a *feature* of utilitarian jurisprudence, not an oversight. ⚠️ It is also the exact structure Austin will formalise when he says that if a positive law conflicts with natural law or religious commandments, **the former must prevail** — a statement about legal validity that leaves the moral question untouched.

### 5. Example, and the limit of the example

⚠️ Suppose a sovereign enacts a measure that produces widespread and avoidable misery with no compensating gain. ⚠️ On Bentham's account three things are simultaneously true: the measure is law; the sovereign has failed the test of **useful legislation**; and resistance, if the conditions are met, is morally justified.

❌ **Where the example stops.** ⚠️ The chapter does not report what Bentham's conditions for justified resistance are, and this file does not supply them. ⚠️ Without the conditions, the doctrine states that a threshold exists without locating it. ⚠️ That is an honest gap in the evidence available here, and it is recorded in the Source Ledger rather than filled.

### 6. Comparison: two ways of limiting a legally unlimited sovereign

| ⚠️ Device | ✅ Who uses it | ⚠️ How it bites | ⚠️ Its weakness |
|---|---|---|---|
| **Erosion of legitimacy** | The chapter itself at p.180 — neglect of reason, conscience, justice, custom, values and the common interest leaves authority **rapidly eroded** and sovereignty liable to be **eventually lost** | Gradually, through decay of obedience | Slow, diffuse, unattributable |
| **Possibility of resistance** | **Bentham** | Abruptly, through refusal or revolt | Costly, risky, and the threshold is unstated |
| **Moral justification of resistance** | **Bentham** | By denying that obedience is owed | Requires a standard, which Bentham supplies as utility and Austin does not supply at all |

⚠️ The three are complements rather than rivals: erosion explains the slow case, resistance the sudden case, and moral justification tells you which refusals are defensible.

### 7. Criticism and reply

⚠️ **Objection.** Utility is too malleable a standard to constrain a sovereign. Every government claims that its measures promote the greatest happiness; if the sovereign both acts and assesses, the test collapses into whatever the sovereign says it is. A limit of purpose with no independent assessor is not a limit.

✅ **Reply.** Bentham's test is not self-certifying in the way the objection assumes, because the measure of **useful legislation** is the aggregate effect on the governed, which the governed are positioned to judge. ⚠️ That is exactly why he couples the standard to the **possibility of resistance**: the assessor of last resort is the population whose happiness is at stake. ⚠️ The chapter's own erosion clause makes the same structural point — a sovereign that neglects the **common interest or public interest** finds its **legitimacy and authority rapidly eroded**.

⚠️ **Residual.** The reply makes the standard enforceable only through the very disruption it is meant to prevent, which is an uncomfortable result. ⚠️ It is also the ancestor of Laski's demand at p.203 that the state justify itself as a **'public service corporation'** whose **claim to the allegiance of individuals will rest on the efficient performance of its functions** — a demand that faces the identical objection and gives the identical answer.

### 8. Using this in an answer

- ⚠️ **Syllabus relevance.** Bentham supplies the only performance standard inside the classical line, which makes him indispensable for compatibility stems about justice, liberty or democracy.
- ⚠️ **Probable framing.** Use Bentham to show that the classical tradition itself contains the resources for criticism — a stronger answer than treating all criticism as external.
- ❌ **Trap.** Describing Bentham as a critic of sovereignty. ✅ He accepts that it is **not limited by law**; his limits are moral and prudential.
- ✅ **Answer use.** The triad — moral limitation, possibility of resistance, useful legislation — is three named items in one sentence, which is efficient under time pressure.

### Revision notes

1. ✅ **Bentham (1748-1832)**, **the famous English Utilitarian**. (PDF p.183)
2. ✅ Sovereignty was **not limited by law, but was subject to moral limitations**.
3. ✅ Sovereignty was **limited by the possibility of resistance**.
4. 🔑 There were **conditions under which resistance was morally justified** — a normative claim, not merely a prudential one.
5. ✅ The sovereign **should justify his authority by useful legislation** with the object of promoting the **greatest happiness of the greatest numbers**.
6. ⚠️ Three kinds of limit: of standard, of consequence-and-justification, of purpose.
7. ⚠️ Bentham is the most demanding on substance and the most permissive on form among Bodin, Bentham and Austin.
8. ⚠️ The structural result he accepts: a valid legal obligation may coexist with a moral justification for refusing it.
9. ⚠️ Austin formalises the same separation when he holds that positive law **must prevail** over natural law or religious commandments.
10. ⚠️ The chapter does not report Bentham's conditions for justified resistance; the gap is disclosed, not filled.
11. ⚠️ Complementary devices: erosion of legitimacy (p.180), possibility of resistance, moral justification of resistance.
12. ⚠️ Objection — utility is self-certifying; reply — the assessor is the governed, which is why resistance is coupled to the standard.
13. ⚠️ Laski's **public service corporation** demand at p.203 is the same structure in pluralist dress.

### Concept check

**Question:** Bentham accepts that sovereignty is not limited by law and simultaneously holds that resistance may be morally justified. Show that these are consistent, and state what the consistency costs.

**Model answer:** ✅ They are consistent because they answer different questions. ✅ "Not limited by law" is a claim about **legal validity** — no superior human authority can invalidate the sovereign's enactment. ✅ "Morally justified resistance" is a claim about **what ought to be done** — whether obedience is owed, which the chapter grounds in **moral limitations** and the test of **useful legislation** promoting the **greatest happiness of the greatest numbers**. ⚠️ A single act can therefore be legally valid and morally indefensible at once, and a citizen can be under a valid obligation he is justified in refusing. ⚠️ The cost is that the only remedy is extra-legal: the standard is enforceable at last resort through the **possibility of resistance**, which is the disruption the legal order exists to prevent. ⚠️ This is the structure Laski later reproduces when he rests the state's claim to allegiance on **the efficient performance of its functions**.

**Misconception to avoid:** ❌ Do not say that Bentham placed legal limits on the sovereign. ⚠️ The chapter's wording is exact — **not limited by law, but subject to moral limitations** — and reading the moral limits as legal ones destroys the very separation that makes his position distinctive.

### Lesson Mains practice

**Mains question [10]:** Assess Bentham's contribution to the theory of sovereignty. *(Assess — answer in about 150 words.)*

**Mains model:** ✅ Bentham's contribution is to hold legal unlimitedness and moral accountability together. ✅ He argued that sovereignty was **not limited by law, but was subject to moral limitations**; that it was **limited by the possibility of resistance**, with **conditions under which resistance was morally justified**; and that the sovereign **should justify his authority by useful legislation with the object of promoting the greatest happiness of the greatest numbers**. ⚠️ The value of this is that it supplies the classical line with an internal standard of criticism, so that the doctrine need not be attacked only from outside. ⚠️ Its weakness is twofold: the standard has no institutional assessor other than the governed, so the remedy is extra-legal; and the chapter does not report what the conditions of justified resistance are, leaving a threshold asserted but unlocated. 🔑 A legally unlimited sovereign on performance terms.

**Rubric [10]:** the law/morals separation in Bentham's own terms **2** · the possibility of resistance **1** · the moral justification of resistance recognised as a separate claim **2** · the useful-legislation standard with the greatest-happiness formula **2** · the value identified as internal criticism of the classical line **2** · at least one honest weakness **1**.

## Lesson 12 — Austin: the determinate superior and the command theory

Progress: 12 / 38 | Stage: Core | Part II — the classical line, Bodin to Austin

━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
Book context: Core owner §6's Austin entry and §3's definition of legal sovereignty, with the Advanced companion §5's seventh thinker entry and §8's **pure legal monism** matrix row, read against the **John Austin (1790-1859)** section spanning PDF pp.183-184 (printed 166-167), which supplies the 1832 definition, the polemical context, the three characteristics of law and the monistic conclusion.
CA search: "Austin command theory of law determinate human superior habitual obedience jurisprudence 2025 2026"
CA found: ⚠️ nothing returned that bears on the chapter's nineteenth-century material. ❌ No linkage is carried into this lesson.
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

---

### 1. Why Austin is the pivot of both chapters

✅ The chapter's placement of Austin could not be more emphatic:

> In the nineteenth century, the theory of sovereignty as a legal concept was perfected by Austin, an English jurist. He is regarded as the greatest exponent of the monistic theory of sovereignty.
>
> — Gauba, Ch.7, PDF p.183 (printed 166)

⚠️ Two words carry weight. **Perfected** means the doctrine reaches its finished form here, not merely its most famous one. **Monistic** is the label Chapter 8 will use as its target — the chapter opens by describing the whole line **from Jean Bodin to John Austin** as **the monistic theory of sovereignty**.

🔑 Austin is therefore simultaneously the high point of Chapter 7 and the opponent of Chapter 8. ⚠️ Every pluralist criticism is aimed at this formulation, so a candidate who states it imprecisely cannot state the criticisms precisely either.

### 2. The definition, in Austin's own words as the chapter gives them

> If a determinate human superior, not in the habit of obedience to a like superior, receives habitual obedience from the bulk of a given society, that determinate superior is sovereign in that society, and that society (including the superior) is a society political and independent.
>
> — Austin, *Province of Jurisprudence Determined* (**1832**), quoted at Gauba, Ch.7, PDF p.183 (printed 166)

⚠️ The definition has four conditions and one consequence. Pull them apart:

| # | ✅ Condition | ⚠️ What it rules out |
|---|---|---|
| 1 | **Determinate** human superior | Rules out "the people", "custom", "the general will" — anything whose membership cannot be specified |
| 2 | **Human** superior | Rules out God, natural law, divine commandment as the sovereign |
| 3 | **Not in the habit of obedience to a like superior** | Rules out a dependent or federated authority; this is independence, stated behaviourally |
| 4 | Receives **habitual obedience from the bulk of a given society** | Rules out an obeyed-once usurper and a universally obeyed authority alike; *habitual* and *bulk* are both hedges |
| → | Consequence: that society, **including the superior**, is **a society political and independent** | Statehood is defined *by* sovereignty, not the other way round |

🔑 Condition 4's two hedges are heavily examinable. ⚠️ **Habitual** allows for disobedience by individuals without destroying sovereignty; **the bulk of** allows for a dissenting minority. ⚠️ Austin's test is statistical and behavioural, not moral — and that is both its strength and the thing MacIver will later turn against it.

⚠️ Note also the bracket in the consequence — **including the superior**. ⚠️ The sovereign is a member of the society he rules, not an external force acting upon it. ⚠️ This blocks the reading of Austinian sovereignty as conquest.

### 3. The polemical context, which the chapter supplies and most summaries drop

⚠️ Austin's theory is a reply, and knowing what it replies to turns recitation into argument.

```text
   THE EARLY UTILITARIANS' PROGRAMME
   remove the anomalies of COMMON LAW
            by subordinating it to a SUPERIOR LAW
            consisting in the UNIVERSAL AND PERMANENT
            DICTATES OF NATURAL REASON
            which "the state could neither ignore nor abrogate"
                            |
                            |  AUSTIN REPUDIATES THIS
                            v
   THEORY OF POSITIVE LAW
   law expresses the will of the LEGAL SOVEREIGN of the state
   and is hence NOT BOUND by the dictates of natural law
            or any other superior law
```

⚠️ Two consequences follow immediately, and both are in the chapter. ⚠️ First, **natural law, or for that matter any superior law, is not law in the proper sense of the term**. ⚠️ Second, **in case a positive law enforced by the state comes into conflict with the natural law or religious commandments, etc., the former must prevail**.

⚠️ Read the second carefully. It is a statement about which norm governs *legally*. ⚠️ It is not a statement that the moral or religious norm is worthless, and Lesson 13 shows that Austin says so himself.

### 4. Law as command: the three characteristics

✅ Austin defines law as **the command of the sovereign, obliging the subject to do or refrain from doing, certain acts, failure to obey the law being visited by penalty**, and the chapter extracts three characteristics:

```text
   (a) It must emanate from a DETERMINATE SOURCE,
       that is the sovereign, TO BE CLEARLY LOCATED IN THE STATE
                 |
   (b) It must be the EXPRESSION OF THE COMMAND of the sovereign
                 |
   (c) It must be BACKED BY SANCTIONS
       -- "disobedience to law must be punishable"
                 |
                 v
   "These characteristics are PECULIAR TO THE POSITIVE LAW OF THE STATE,
    not to be found in NATURAL LAW, CUSTOM or RELIGIOUS COMMANDMENTS."
```

⚠️ The last line is the engine of the whole theory. ⚠️ Custom fails (a) and (b) because no determinate body commanded it; natural law fails all three; religious commandment fails (a) and (c) as the state administers them. ⚠️ By making these three the *marks* of law, Austin has defined his rivals out of the category rather than argued with them — which is efficient and is precisely what Duguit, Krabbe and MacIver will later refuse to accept.

### 5. The hardest consequence: rights as concessions

✅ The chapter does not soften what follows:

> He maintained that any rights of the citizens, including the right to property, were nothing but concessions granted by the sovereign. The subjects could have no legal right against the state.
>
> — Gauba, Ch.7, PDF p.184 (printed 167)

⚠️ This is the moment where Austin breaks decisively with Bodin, who had held private property **inviolable** because it was **granted by the law of nature**. ⚠️ On Austin's premises there is no law of nature to grant anything, so every right is a creature of the sovereign's command and can be withdrawn by it.

🔑 For examination purposes, this is the sharpest answer to any stem asking whether Austinian sovereignty is compatible with rights, liberty or democracy. ⚠️ It is also the exact claim that makes the pluralist reply necessary: if subjects have **no legal right against the state**, then the limits on the state must come from somewhere other than law, which is why pluralists relocate them to associations, conscience and function.

### 6. Comparison: three definitions of sovereignty, side by side

| ⚠️ Element | ✅ **Bodin** | ✅ **Rousseau** | ✅ **Austin** |
|---|---|---|---|
| Who is sovereign | The commanding authority, **one person or several** | The **people in their corporate capacity** | A **determinate human superior** |
| Test applied | Is he the source of law? | Does the general will reign? | Is he habitually obeyed, and not habitually obedient to a like superior? |
| Nature of the test | Jurisprudential-genetic | Moral-constitutive | **Behavioural and observational** |
| What law is | What the sovereign establishes | The expression of the general will | **Command + determinate source + sanction** |
| Rights | Property inviolable by natural law | Secured by the general will's own object | **Concessions**; no legal right against the state |

⚠️ Austin's column is the only one whose test could in principle be applied by an observer without any view about morality or legitimacy. ⚠️ That is what makes it a *jurisprudential* theory rather than a political one — and what makes Chapter 8's complaint that it fails in the political sphere both fair and, in a sense, beside the point.

### 7. Criticism and reply

⚠️ **Objection.** The determinacy requirement cannot be met by any modern state. In a constitutional democracy, law issues from legislatures elected under rules, limited by a constitution, interpreted by courts and constrained by an electorate; there is no single body that is both determinate and not habitually obedient to anything.

✅ **Reply.** Austin's defender can say that determinacy is satisfied by the *composite* law-making authority, however complex — the chapter's own illustration at p.189 is the legal sovereign in an absolute monarchy, but nothing in the definition forbids a body of persons. ⚠️ The chapter also notes that Austin's theory was **influenced by the then prevailing conditions in England**, which is a historical explanation rather than a refutation.

⚠️ **Residual.** The reply holds the definition together and concedes the political point. ⚠️ Laski presses it to breaking at p.201: **everyone knows that to regard the King-in-Parliament as a sovereign body in the Austinian sense is absurd. No Parliament would dare to disfranchise the Roman Catholics or to prohibit the existence of trade unions. If it made the attempt, it would cease to be a Parliament.** ⚠️ The chapter prints the charge without rebutting it, and the honest position is that determinacy survives as a legal construct while losing descriptive grip.

### 8. Using this in an answer

- ⚠️ **Syllabus relevance.** Austin is examined directly and is the presupposition of every pluralist stem; two of the nine primary anchors turn on him.
- ⚠️ **Probable framing.** "Is Austin's theory compatible with democracy?" is answered by the four conditions, the rights-as-concessions consequence and then Dicey's political sovereign — in that order.
- ❌ **Trap.** Quoting the definition without the clause **not in the habit of obedience to a like superior**. ✅ That clause is what makes it a test of independence and not merely of obedience.
- ✅ **Answer use.** Set out the four conditions as conditions. An answer that shows what each rules out reads as analysis; an answer that recites the sentence reads as memory.

### Revision notes

1. ✅ **Austin (1790-1859)**, **an English jurist**, **perfected** the theory of sovereignty as a legal concept; **the greatest exponent of the monistic theory**. (PDF p.183)
2. ✅ The definition is from **Province of Jurisprudence Determined (1832)**.
3. 🔑 Four conditions: **determinate**; **human**; **not in the habit of obedience to a like superior**; **receives habitual obedience from the bulk of a given society**.
4. ✅ Consequence: that society, **including the superior**, is **a society political and independent**.
5. ⚠️ **Habitual** and **the bulk of** are deliberate hedges; the test is behavioural, not moral.
6. ✅ Context: the early Utilitarians sought to subordinate **common law** to a **superior law** consisting in the **universal and permanent dictates of natural reason**, which **the state could neither ignore nor abrogate**; Austin **repudiated** this.
7. ✅ His **theory of positive law** expresses **the will of the legal sovereign of the state** and is **not bound by the dictates of natural law or any other superior law**.
8. ✅ Law = **the command of the sovereign**, obliging the subject to act or refrain, **failure to obey being visited by penalty**.
9. ✅ Three characteristics of law: **(a)** determinate source, the sovereign, **clearly located in the state**; **(b)** expression of the **command**; **(c)** **backed by sanctions**.
10. ✅ These are **peculiar to the positive law of the state**, not found in **natural law, custom or religious commandments**; so superior law is **not law in the proper sense of the term**.
11. ✅ On conflict, **the former must prevail** — positive law over natural law or religious commandments.
12. ✅ Rights, **including the right to property**, are **nothing but concessions granted by the sovereign**; subjects **could have no legal right against the state**.
13. ✅ By establishing a **single source of all positive law**, Austin put forward a **monistic view of law, state and sovereignty**.
14. ⚠️ Laski's attack on determinacy: regarding **the King-in-Parliament** as sovereign in the Austinian sense is **absurd** (p.201).

### Concept check

**Question:** Austin's definition contains a clause that is about independence rather than obedience. Identify it, explain what it excludes, and show why removing it would make the definition useless.

**Model answer:** ✅ The clause is **not in the habit of obedience to a like superior**. ⚠️ It excludes any authority that, however widely obeyed within its own sphere, is itself habitually obedient to another authority of the same kind — a dependency, a province, a subordinate legislature. ✅ Without it the definition would read: any determinate human superior receiving habitual obedience from the bulk of a society is sovereign. ⚠️ That version would make a provincial government sovereign over its province, a colonial administration sovereign over its colony, and arguably a dominant association sovereign over its members, since each receives habitual obedience from the bulk of its own society. ⚠️ Sovereignty would multiply and cease to mark anything. ✅ The clause therefore supplies externally what condition one supplies internally: determinacy fixes *who*, and non-obedience-to-a-like-superior fixes *that there is nobody above*. 🔑 Together they reproduce Garner's **cannot be legally bound except by its own will** in behavioural language.

**Misconception to avoid:** ❌ Do not treat "habitual obedience from the bulk of a given society" as a requirement of universal or willing obedience. ⚠️ Both hedges are deliberate: **habitual** tolerates individual disobedience and **the bulk of** tolerates a dissenting minority, so neither crime nor protest defeats sovereignty on Austin's test.

### Lesson Mains practice

**Mains question [10]:** Why is Austin described as having "perfected" the legal theory of sovereignty? *(Explain — answer in about 150 words.)*

**Mains model:** ✅ Because he removed the residues that kept earlier versions impure. ✅ Bodin had left **fundamental laws** the sovereign **could not lawfully abrogate** and a natural-law protection of property; Austin repudiated the Utilitarian project of subordinating common law to **the universal and permanent dictates of natural reason**, holding that **natural law, or for that matter any superior law, is not law in the proper sense of the term**. ✅ He then supplied a single operational test — a **determinate human superior, not in the habit of obedience to a like superior**, receiving **habitual obedience from the bulk of a given society** — and a matching account of law as **command** from a **determinate source** and **backed by sanctions**. ✅ The result is a **monistic view of law, state and sovereignty**, with rights reduced to **concessions granted by the sovereign**. ⚠️ Perfection here means internal consistency, not descriptive accuracy. 🔑 The purest form, and therefore the easiest to attack.

**Rubric [10]:** the residues Austin removes, with at least one Bodin exception named **2** · the repudiation of superior law, with the *not law in the proper sense* clause **2** · the definition's four conditions, at least three stated **3** · the three characteristics of law, at least two stated **2** · the qualification that perfection is consistency rather than accuracy **1**.

---

## Lesson 13 — Austin's firewall

Progress: 13 / 38 | Stage: Core | Part II — the classical line, Bodin to Austin

━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
Book context: Core owner §9's first listed trap — the correction that Austin's theory **dwells on legal form and does not deny moral or social restraints in practice** — with the Advanced companion §12's first factual-risk check, read against the closing paragraph of the Austin section at PDF p.184 (printed 167), which supplies the four disclaimers that form the firewall.
CA search: "Austin sovereignty moral limitations misreading legal positivism corrective scholarship 2025 2026"
CA found: ⚠️ nothing returned that bears on the chapter's closing paragraph on Austin. ❌ No linkage is carried into this lesson.
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

---

### 1. The most profitable paragraph in the topic

⚠️ Ask most candidates what is wrong with Austin and you will hear: *he ignores morality*. ✅ The chapter says, in terms, that he does not. ⚠️ Being able to show this is worth more than any criticism of Austin a candidate could offer, because it demonstrates that the criticism being offered is the *right* one.

✅ The paragraph in full:

> It is significant that Austin's theory solely dwells on the legal character of sovereignty; it does not repudiate moral or social limitations on the power of the state. He does not declare the state as a 'perfect embodiment of reason' as the idealist theory had maintained. Austin is an exponent of absolute and unlimited sovereignty purely from the legal or formal point of view. He admits that sovereign authority habitually observes fundamental moral principles though not legally bound by any external authority. He does not envisage an irresponsible sovereign, but holds that the sovereign cannot be formally made responsible to any authority similar to himself; his authority is legally superior to all individuals and groups within his jurisdiction.
>
> — Gauba, Ch.7, PDF p.184 (printed 167)

### 2. Four disclaimers, and what each one blocks

```text
   DISCLAIMER 1
   "does not repudiate MORAL OR SOCIAL LIMITATIONS on the power
    of the state"
        -> blocks: "Austin says morality is irrelevant to the state"

   DISCLAIMER 2
   "does not declare the state a 'PERFECT EMBODIMENT OF REASON'
    as the IDEALIST theory had maintained"
        -> blocks: "Austin glorifies the state"
        -> and marks the distance from idealism: Austin's sovereign
           is supreme, not wise

   DISCLAIMER 3
   "admits that sovereign authority HABITUALLY OBSERVES FUNDAMENTAL
    MORAL PRINCIPLES though NOT LEGALLY BOUND by any external authority"
        -> blocks: "Austin's sovereign is amoral in practice"
        -> note the symmetry with condition 4 of the definition:
           habitual obedience from below, habitual observance from above

   DISCLAIMER 4
   "does not envisage an IRRESPONSIBLE SOVEREIGN, but holds that the
    sovereign cannot be FORMALLY made responsible to any authority
    SIMILAR TO HIMSELF"
        -> blocks: "Austin denies responsibility"
        -> the denial is only of FORMAL responsibility to a LIKE authority
```

🔑 Disclaimer 3 contains a symmetry worth pointing out in an answer. ⚠️ Austin's definition requires **habitual obedience** from the bulk of society; his firewall asserts **habitual observance** of moral principles by the sovereign. ⚠️ The theory is held together at both ends by regularities of behaviour rather than by rules — which is consistent, and which also explains why it cannot be refuted by producing a badly behaved sovereign.

### 3. What the firewall does to the standard criticisms

| ❌ Common criticism | ✅ What the firewall answers | ⚠️ What survives the firewall |
|---|---|---|
| "Austin makes the state a moral absolute." | ❌ He expressly refuses the idealist claim that the state is a **perfect embodiment of reason** | ⚠️ Nothing of this criticism survives in this form |
| "Austin's sovereign is unaccountable." | ❌ He **does not envisage an irresponsible sovereign** | ✅ But: accountability has no **formal** mechanism, and that is the pluralist complaint |
| "Austin ignores custom." | ❌ Not as a social fact; he denies custom the *status of law* | ✅ Maine's historical criticism, accepted by Laski, that custom **substantially limits the exercise of sovereign power** |
| "Austin leaves subjects defenceless." | ⚠️ Not answered by the firewall | ✅ Survives in full: **the subjects could have no legal right against the state** |
| "Austin's sovereign cannot be located." | ⚠️ Not answered by the firewall | ✅ Survives in full: Laski at p.202 and MacIver at p.204 |

🔑 Read the right-hand column. ⚠️ Those three surviving criticisms are the serious ones, and all three are *structural* — they concern where authority is, what recourse the subject has, and whether the theory describes anything. ⚠️ None of them is the easy claim that Austin was indifferent to morals.

### 4. The firewall and the domain lock are the same device

⚠️ Return to Lesson 1. ✅ The chapter said that **all objections about sovereignty arise when this concept is shifted from the legal and juristic domain to other spheres**. ✅ Austin's firewall is that sentence applied by Austin to himself: his theory **solely dwells on the legal character of sovereignty**, and he is an exponent of absolute sovereignty **purely from the legal or formal point of view**.

```text
     DOMAIN LOCK (chapter, p.179)        AUSTIN'S FIREWALL (p.184)
     --------------------------          -------------------------
     objections arise when the     <-->  the theory dwells SOLELY on
     concept is shifted out of           the legal character
     the legal domain
                        \            /
                         \          /
                          v        v
         => Austin is not refuted by showing that his sovereign
            behaves badly, or that morality matters.
         => He IS vulnerable to showing that the LEGAL claim
            itself fails -- which is exactly what Laski's second
            front and MacIver's supremacy of law attempt.
```

⚠️ This is the structural insight that organises the whole of Chapter 8 for a reader: the pluralists who matter most are not those who say the state should be moral, but those who say the *legal* picture is wrong.

### 5. Example, and the limit of the example

⚠️ Consider a sovereign legislature that could, as a matter of legal competence, abolish an entire category of civil protections overnight, but has never done so and would face catastrophe if it tried. ⚠️ Austin's account says: the competence exists, and its non-exercise is explained by **habitual observance of fundamental moral principles**, not by any legal bar.

❌ **Where the example stops.** ⚠️ It cannot distinguish habitual moral observance from habitual political calculation. ⚠️ Laski's version of the same scenario is explicitly political: **No Parliament would dare to disfranchise the Roman Catholics or to prohibit the existence of trade unions. If it made the attempt, it would cease to be a Parliament.** ⚠️ Where Austin sees moral habit, Laski sees a power **exercised under conditions fairly well known to each generation**. ⚠️ Both are accounts of the same restraint; only the second treats the restraint as damaging to the theory.

### 6. Comparison: Austin against the idealist theory

| ⚠️ Axis | ✅ **Austin** | ✅ **Idealist theory**, as the chapter characterises it |
|---|---|---|
| What the state is | A determinate legal superior | A **perfect embodiment of reason** |
| Basis of obedience | Habitual obedience; command backed by sanction | ⚠️ The state's rational and moral character |
| Role of morality | Real, observed, but **not legally binding** | ⚠️ Constitutive of the state's claim |
| Risk the chapter identifies | ⚠️ Subjects left with **no legal right against the state** | ⚠️ Glorification; ✅ the organic theory at p.195 **glorified the state as the fountain of all moral and social values** |

⚠️ The comparison matters because candidates often attribute idealist glorification to Austin. ❌ The chapter separates them explicitly, and the separation is one of its sharpest corrections.

### 7. Criticism and reply

⚠️ **Objection.** The firewall is a retreat disguised as a clarification. By confining the theory to legal form, Austin makes it unfalsifiable: no political fact can embarrass it, because every political fact is outside its scope. A doctrine immune to evidence is not a strength but an evasion.

✅ **Reply.** A theory of legal validity is *supposed* to be insensitive to political fact, in the same way that a theory of grammatical correctness is insensitive to whether a sentence is true. ⚠️ Austin's claim is about what makes a norm a law of the state, and it answers that question with a determinate source, a command and a sanction. ⚠️ The chapter endorses this much when it says the concept of sovereignty is **an essential element of the legal character of the state** and that the state **cannot perform its function of regulating the life of its citizens unless it is equipped with supreme authority**.

⚠️ **Residual.** The reply works only while the theory is kept inside its domain, and the chapter's own appraisal is that it does not stay there. ⚠️ At p.194 Chapter 8 states the consequence: once **the theory of absolute and unlimited sovereignty is accepted, the distinction between the state and government is soon forgotten or ignored and absolute powers are claimed by a government in the name of the state, paving the way for autocracy and tyranny**. ⚠️ If a legal doctrine reliably migrates into political practice, confining it by stipulation is not enough.

### 8. Using this in an answer

- ⚠️ **Syllabus relevance.** The firewall is the difference between a mid-band and an upper-band answer on any Austin stem, because it shows which criticisms are worth making.
- ⚠️ **Probable framing.** In a compatibility stem — Austin and democracy, Austin and liberty — concede the firewall first, then press the surviving structural objections.
- ❌ **Trap.** Writing that Austin denied moral limits. ✅ He **does not repudiate moral or social limitations**; he denies them **legal** force.
- ✅ **Answer use.** One sentence of concession buys credibility for three sentences of criticism. Use **he does not envisage an irresponsible sovereign** as that sentence.

### Revision notes

1. ✅ Austin's theory **solely dwells on the legal character of sovereignty**. (PDF p.184)
2. ✅ It **does not repudiate moral or social limitations on the power of the state**.
3. ✅ He **does not declare the state as a 'perfect embodiment of reason' as the idealist theory had maintained**.
4. ✅ He is an exponent of absolute and unlimited sovereignty **purely from the legal or formal point of view**.
5. 🔑 He **admits that sovereign authority habitually observes fundamental moral principles though not legally bound by any external authority**.
6. ✅ He **does not envisage an irresponsible sovereign**, but holds that the sovereign **cannot be formally made responsible to any authority similar to himself**.
7. ✅ His authority is **legally superior to all individuals and groups within his jurisdiction**.
8. ⚠️ Symmetry: **habitual obedience** from below, **habitual observance** from above — the theory rests on regularities of behaviour at both ends.
9. ⚠️ The firewall kills the easy criticisms and leaves three serious ones: no formal accountability; **no legal right against the state**; and the sovereign cannot be located.
10. ⚠️ The firewall is Austin applying the chapter's own domain lock to himself.
11. ⚠️ Consequence for study: the pluralists who matter are those attacking the **legal** picture, not those asserting that the state should be moral.
12. ⚠️ Laski's political reading of the same restraint: power **exercised under conditions fairly well known to each generation**.
13. ⚠️ Chapter 8's residual at p.194: once absolute sovereignty is accepted, the state/government distinction **is soon forgotten or ignored**, **paving the way for autocracy and tyranny**.

### Concept check

**Question:** "The pluralist attack succeeds because Austin ignored morality." Show that this diagnosis is wrong on the chapter's own evidence, and state what the correct diagnosis is.

**Model answer:** ✅ The diagnosis is wrong because the chapter records four express disclaimers: Austin's theory **does not repudiate moral or social limitations on the power of the state**; he **does not declare the state as a 'perfect embodiment of reason'**; he **admits that sovereign authority habitually observes fundamental moral principles**; and he **does not envisage an irresponsible sovereign**. ⚠️ A critic who argues that morality matters is therefore attacking a position Austin never held. ✅ The correct diagnosis is structural and has three parts, all of which survive the disclaimers. ⚠️ First, responsibility has no **formal** mechanism, since the sovereign cannot be made responsible to any authority **similar to himself**. ⚠️ Second, the subject is left exposed, because rights are **concessions granted by the sovereign** and **the subjects could have no legal right against the state**. ⚠️ Third, the determinate sovereign cannot be located in modern political organisation — Laski on the **King-in-Parliament**, MacIver on the absence of a **homogeneous will of the people**.

**Misconception to avoid:** ❌ Do not conflate Austin with the idealist or organic theories of the state. ⚠️ The chapter separates them in the same paragraph, and the organic theory it describes at p.195 — which **glorified the state as the fountain of all moral and social values** — is the position Austin expressly declines.

### Lesson Mains practice

**Mains question [10]:** "Austin's firewall changes which criticisms of him are worth making." Discuss. *(Discuss — answer in about 150 words.)*

**Mains model:** ✅ The firewall consists of four disclaimers: the theory **solely dwells on the legal character of sovereignty**; it **does not repudiate moral or social limitations**; Austin **does not declare the state as a 'perfect embodiment of reason'**; and he **does not envisage an irresponsible sovereign**, admitting that sovereign authority **habitually observes fundamental moral principles**. ⚠️ Three familiar criticisms therefore miss — that Austin is indifferent to morality, that he glorifies the state, and that he denies responsibility outright. ⚠️ What remains are structural objections the disclaimers cannot touch: responsibility is not **formal**, since no authority **similar to himself** can enforce it; the subject has **no legal right against the state**, rights being **concessions granted by the sovereign**; and the determinate sovereign is unlocatable in modern political organisation. ✅ These are the objections Laski and MacIver actually press. 🔑 Concede the morals, attack the structure.

**Rubric [10]:** at least three of the four disclaimers quoted or closely reported **3** · the identification of criticisms that the disclaimers defeat **2** · the no-formal-responsibility survival **1** · the no-legal-right-against-the-state survival **2** · the unlocatability survival, attributed to Laski or MacIver **1** · a closing instruction that distinguishes concession from attack **1**.

## Lesson 14 — Absoluteness

Progress: 14 / 38 | Stage: Core | Part III — the five characteristics

━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
Book context: Core owner §3's consolidated five-characteristic entry and §5's distinctions, with the Advanced companion §4's definition of **absoluteness** as legal non-subordination, read against the **II. CHARACTERISTICS OF SOVEREIGNTY** heading and the **ABSOLUTENESS** section at PDF pp.184-185 (printed 167-168).
CA search: "absolute sovereignty international organisation consent political theory doctrine 2025 2026"
CA found: ⚠️ nothing returned that bears on the characteristic as the chapter defines it; the file's single dated anchor is already used at Lesson 2. ❌ No linkage is carried into this lesson.
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

---

### 1. Where the five characteristics come from, and what licenses them

⚠️ Before the first characteristic, notice the sentence that introduces all five, because it is a licensing condition and candidates routinely drop it:

> Viewed as a purely legal concept, it is customary to describe certain characteristics of sovereignty. They are: absoluteness, permanence, universality, inalienability and indivisibility.
>
> — Gauba, Ch.7, PDF p.184 (printed 167)

🔑 **Viewed as a purely legal concept.** ⚠️ The five are not free-standing truths about political life; they are the properties sovereignty must have *if* the legal account is taken strictly. ⚠️ Any answer that lists the five without this clause has stated the doctrine at its most vulnerable and thrown away the defence in the same breath.

> 🔑 Mnemonic: **A-PUI-I** — **A**bsoluteness, **P**ermanence, **U**niversality, **I**nalienability, **I**ndivisibility. Say it as *"a pooey eye"* if that helps; what matters is that the order follows the chapter's own, because later sentences recite the five in exactly this sequence.

⚠️ Note also that the five are recited again twice in the chapters, in the same order and with one adjective changed: at p.187 as **absolute, perpetual, all-pervasive, inalienable and indivisible**, and at p.188 as **absolute, permanent, universal, inalienable and indivisible**. ⚠️ Chapter 8 opens by attributing the same list to the whole line from Bodin to Austin: **absolute, perpetual, universal, inalienable and indivisible**. ⚠️ The variation between *permanent* and *perpetual*, and between *universal* and *all-pervasive*, is the author's own and is not a doctrinal difference.

### 2. Absoluteness defined

✅ The chapter opens with Bodin, as it should:

> Jean Bodin, the first exponent of sovereignty, said: 'Sovereignty is the absolute and perpetual power ... of commanding in a state'. Sovereignty is regarded as absolute because it cannot be limited or restricted by any superior power or authority.
>
> — Gauba, Ch.7, PDF p.184 (printed 167)

⚠️ Note the logical form: absoluteness is defined by a **negation about superiors**, not by a positive grant of scope. ⚠️ It says nothing about how much the sovereign may do and everything about whether anyone may stop him.

### 3. The two concessions built into the characteristic

✅ The chapter immediately attaches two qualifications, and both are stated as things the sovereign does **of his own will**:

| ⚠️ Concession | ✅ The chapter's wording | ⚠️ What makes it compatible with absoluteness |
|---|---|---|
| Internal | The sovereign **may, of his own will, pay due regard to moral principles, customs and traditions or public opinion**, but **he is not responsible to any authority — individual or group — similar or superior to himself** | The regard is self-directed; no external authority compels it |
| External | The sovereign **may pay due regard to international law by his free consent, not because he is obliged to obey the commands of any superior state or international organization** | This is Grotius's consent manoeuvre reappearing as a property of sovereignty |

🔑 The external concession is the same device seen at Lesson 7 and reappearing at Lesson 16 for diplomatic immunity. ⚠️ A candidate who spots that one manoeuvre — *compliance by free consent is an exercise of sovereignty, not a subtraction from it* — does duty in three separate places has understood the architecture of the chapter.

### 4. The sentence that gives the game away

✅ The characteristic closes with a self-limitation that most summaries omit and every good answer uses:

> Absoluteness of sovereignty is the logical outcome of its legal character, which may not hold good in the political sphere, nor would it govern the non-legal actions of the individuals and groups within the state.
>
> — Gauba, Ch.7, PDF p.185 (printed 168)

⚠️ Three claims are packed in here:

```text
   [1] absoluteness is a LOGICAL OUTCOME of the legal character
            -> it is derived, not assumed; remove the legal framing
               and the property goes with it

   [2] it "MAY NOT HOLD GOOD IN THE POLITICAL SPHERE"
            -> the chapter concedes, before Chapter 8 opens, that the
               characteristic may be false of politics

   [3] "nor would it govern the NON-LEGAL ACTIONS of the individuals
       and GROUPS within the state"
            -> and here is the pluralist opening, written by the author
               of the monist exposition
```

🔑 Claim [3] is remarkable. ⚠️ The chapter is saying that even within the territory, the non-legal conduct of individuals and groups is not governed by sovereign absoluteness. ⚠️ Chapter 8's entire thesis — that associations **embody important social values prior to and independent of their authorization or approval by the state** — is a development of that single clause.

### 5. Example, and the limit of the example

⚠️ A state signs a treaty that constrains its domestic environmental regulation and complies for twenty years. ⚠️ On the chapter's account this is absoluteness intact: the regard paid to international law is **by his free consent**, not obedience to **any superior state or international organization**.

❌ **Where the example stops.** ⚠️ Replace "signs a treaty" with "is admitted to a body whose membership is in practice indispensable and whose rules are a condition of membership" and the account strains. ⚠️ Consent remains formally present, but the alternative to consenting has become unaffordable. ⚠️ The chapter does not pursue this; ➡️ the pursuit is Chapter 9's and belongs to **Topic 12**. ⚠️ Inside this topic, the strongest available statement of the difficulty is Laski's: absolute sovereignty is incompatible with **the scientific fact of world-interdependence**.

### 6. Comparison: absoluteness in three registers

| ⚠️ Register | ⚠️ Does absoluteness hold? | ✅ Evidence in the chapters |
|---|---|---|
| **Legal** | ✅ Yes, by derivation | **Absoluteness... is the logical outcome of its legal character** (p.185) |
| **Political** | ⚠️ Possibly not, by the chapter's own admission | **May not hold good in the political sphere** (p.185); **extremely difficult to discover the seat or real character of sovereignty** in democratic, federal, pluralist and constitutional government (p.187) |
| **Social / non-legal conduct of groups** | ❌ No, by the chapter's own admission | **Nor would it govern the non-legal actions of the individuals and groups within the state** (p.185) |

⚠️ This table is the most efficient single piece of material in the topic for a critically-framed stem, because every entry in it is the author's own concession rather than a critic's charge.

### 7. Criticism and reply

⚠️ **Objection.** A property that holds only in the legal register, is admitted possibly not to hold in the political register, and is admitted not to hold over non-legal social conduct, is not a characteristic of sovereignty in any useful sense. It is a restatement of the definition in adjectival form.

✅ **Reply.** The restatement is doing real work, because it rules out an entire class of positions. ⚠️ If absoluteness holds legally, then: there is no appellate authority above the state; no association within it may claim legal immunity as of right; no foreign power may lawfully command it; and no rule of natural or divine law can invalidate its enactment. ⚠️ Each of those was seriously maintained before the modern period and each is denied by this single adjective.

⚠️ **Residual.** The reply does not meet the deeper complaint, which is about explanatory value rather than content. ⚠️ If the interesting questions about authority are political and social, and absoluteness is confessedly silent on both, then the characteristic marks the boundary of the theory rather than its achievement. ⚠️ The chapter's own appraisal at p.189 concedes as much by moving from the legal description straight to a demand for **constitutional government**.

### 8. Using this in an answer

- ⚠️ **Syllabus relevance.** Absoluteness is named in examined stems directly — one 20-mark anchor asks for the arguments that sovereignty must be **absolute, perpetual and undivided**.
- ⚠️ **Probable framing.** A compatibility stem is best answered by granting absoluteness its legal sense and then quoting the chapter's own **may not hold good in the political sphere**.
- ❌ **Trap.** Listing the five characteristics without the governing clause **viewed as a purely legal concept**. ✅ That clause is the first mark on the page.
- ✅ **Answer use.** Use the three-register table of §6. An answer built from the author's own concessions is very difficult for an examiner to mark down.

### Revision notes

1. ✅ The five are introduced **viewed as a purely legal concept**: **absoluteness, permanence, universality, inalienability and indivisibility**. (PDF p.184)
2. 🔑 Mnemonic **A-PUI-I**, in the chapter's own order.
3. ⚠️ The list is recited again at p.187 as **absolute, perpetual, all-pervasive, inalienable and indivisible** and at p.188 as **absolute, permanent, universal, inalienable and indivisible**; the variation is stylistic.
4. ✅ Chapter 8 attributes the same five to the whole line **from Jean Bodin to John Austin** and names it **the monistic theory of sovereignty** (p.194).
5. ✅ Bodin: **'Sovereignty is the absolute and perpetual power... of commanding in a state'**.
6. ✅ Absolute **because it cannot be limited or restricted by any superior power or authority**; the will of the sovereign **reigns supreme in the state**.
7. ✅ He **may, of his own will, pay due regard to moral principles, customs and traditions or public opinion**, but is **not responsible to any authority — individual or group — similar or superior to himself**.
8. ✅ He may regard international law **by his free consent**, not because **obliged to obey the commands of any superior state or international organization**.
9. 🔑 **Absoluteness of sovereignty is the logical outcome of its legal character, which may not hold good in the political sphere, nor would it govern the non-legal actions of the individuals and groups within the state.**
10. ⚠️ That final clause is the pluralist opening, conceded by the chapter before Chapter 8 begins.
11. ⚠️ Absoluteness is a negation about superiors, not a grant of scope.
12. ⚠️ What the adjective rules out: appellate authority above the state; associational legal immunity as of right; lawful foreign command; invalidation by natural or divine law.
13. ⚠️ Residual: a property silent on politics and on non-legal social conduct marks the theory's boundary rather than its achievement.

### Concept check

**Question:** Using only sentences supplied by the chapter in its exposition of absoluteness, construct the strongest case that the classical doctrine concedes the pluralist point in advance.

**Model answer:** ✅ Three concessions suffice, all from pp.184-185. ⚠️ First, the characteristics are offered only **viewed as a purely legal concept**, so no claim about political or social reality is being made by listing them. ⚠️ Second, absoluteness **is the logical outcome of its legal character, which may not hold good in the political sphere** — an explicit admission that the property may be false of politics. ⚠️ Third, and decisively, it **would not govern the non-legal actions of the individuals and groups within the state**: the author of the monist exposition concedes that sovereign absoluteness does not reach the non-legal conduct of associations. ✅ Chapter 8's definition of political pluralism — that certain groups **embody important social values prior to and independent of their authorization or approval by the state** — is a development of that third concession rather than a contradiction of it. ⚠️ The honest conclusion is that monism and pluralism are not rival answers to one question but answers to two, and that the chapter marks the join itself.

**Misconception to avoid:** ❌ Do not infer from these concessions that the chapter abandons absoluteness. ⚠️ It affirms the property within its register and in the same paragraph insists that the sovereign is **not responsible to any authority — individual or group — similar or superior to himself**; the concessions limit the claim's domain, not its truth inside that domain.

### Lesson Mains practice

**Mains question [10]:** "Absoluteness is a consequence, not a premise." Examine this reading of the first characteristic of sovereignty. *(Examine — answer in about 150 words.)*

**Mains model:** ✅ The chapter states the reading directly: **absoluteness of sovereignty is the logical outcome of its legal character**. ✅ Once sovereignty is taken as the quality by which the state **cannot be legally bound except by its own will**, it follows that it **cannot be limited or restricted by any superior power or authority**, since any such limitation would require precisely the superior the definition excludes. ⚠️ Two corollaries confirm the derivation. ⚠️ The sovereign's regard for **moral principles, customs and traditions or public opinion** is **of his own will**, and his regard for international law is **by his free consent** — neither is an external constraint, so neither falsifies the property. ⚠️ The cost of deriving it this way is that the property inherits the premise's limits: the chapter concedes it **may not hold good in the political sphere**. 🔑 A conclusion, and only as strong as its domain.

**Rubric [10]:** the *logical outcome of its legal character* formulation **3** · the derivation from the definition's exclusion of a superior **2** · both self-willed concessions, internal and external **2** · the inheritance of the premise's limits, with the political-sphere caveat **2** · a closing line that does not merely restate **1**.

## Lesson 15 — Permanence, and the distinction it forces

Progress: 15 / 38 | Stage: Core | Part III — the five characteristics

━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
Book context: Core owner §5's **state vs government** distinction, with the Advanced companion §4's entry on **permanence** and §4's state/government entry, read against the **PERMANENCE** section at PDF p.185 (printed 168), which supplies Bodin's perpetuity quotation, the state/government distinction and the suzerainty exception.
CA search: "sovereignty permanence state continuity succession of governments political theory 2025 2026"
CA found: ⚠️ nothing returned that bears on the characteristic as the chapter defines it. ❌ No linkage is carried into this lesson.
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

---

### 1. Permanence is a corollary, and the chapter says so

✅ The opening sentence fixes the logical relation:

> Permanence of sovereignty is the corollary of its absoluteness.
>
> — Gauba, Ch.7, PDF p.185 (printed 168)

⚠️ The derivation runs through Bodin's definition of a sovereign as one who holds power not merely greatly but **perpetually**:

> Bodin describes sovereignty as perpetual power because, 'if power be held only for a certain time (it does not matter how long a time), it is not sovereign power, and he who holds it for that time is not a sovereign.'
>
> — Bodin, quoted at Gauba, Ch.7, PDF p.185 (printed 168)

🔑 The parenthesis is the argument. ⚠️ **It does not matter how long a time** — a hundred-year grant is still a grant, and a grant implies a grantor, and a grantor is a superior. ⚠️ Permanence is therefore absoluteness extended along the time axis: no superior in space, no superior in time.

### 2. The move that makes permanence survivable

⚠️ There is an obvious objection staring at this characteristic: governments fall constantly. ✅ The chapter answers by introducing, at this exact point, the distinction that will dominate the rest of the topic:

> In order to understand the permanence of sovereignty, it is necessary to distinguish between the state and government. Theoretically, sovereignty belongs to the state. Governments may be formed and dissolved according to established procedure, but the continuity of the state will not be affected by such changes. As such, sovereignty endures as long as the state maintains its independence.
>
> — Gauba, Ch.7, PDF p.185 (printed 168)

```text
        TIME ------------------------------------------------>

   GOVERNMENTS   [ G1 ]--[ G2 ]--[ G3 ]--[ G4 ]--[ G5 ]--[ G6 ]
                   formed and dissolved "according to
                   established procedure"

   THE STATE     [======================================]
                   continuous; sovereignty endures as long
                   as the state "maintains its independence"
```

⚠️ Notice the qualifier **according to established procedure**. ⚠️ It matters, because it excludes the revolutionary case — and the revolutionary case is precisely what the de jure/de facto distinction at Lesson 21 exists to handle.

### 3. Permanence is not eternity — the chapter's own limit

✅ The characteristic is given an explicit terminating condition, and candidates who can state it score:

> The quality of permanence should not be interpreted to mean that sovereignty is eternal. On the other hand, if a state loses its independence and is placed under the suzerainty of another state, its sovereignty is also lost.
>
> — Gauba, Ch.7, PDF p.185 (printed 168)

| ⚠️ Event | ✅ Does sovereignty survive? | ⚠️ Why |
|---|---|---|
| Government falls, successor formed by established procedure | ✅ Yes | Sovereignty belongs to the **state**, not the government |
| Constitution amended, organs reorganised | ✅ Yes | Powers are redistributed; sovereignty is not |
| Revolution; new authority not yet legally established | ⚠️ Temporarily split | De jure and de facto sovereigns coexist — Lesson 21 |
| State placed under the **suzerainty of another state** | ❌ No | **Its sovereignty is also lost** |
| Territory ceded to another state | ⚠️ Replaced, not transferred | From the ceded portion's viewpoint **the original state no longer exists** — Lesson 17 |

🔑 The suzerainty row is the single most precise statement in the chapter of what *ends* sovereignty, and it is also the cleanest demonstration that permanence is conditional on **independence**, which is the external face from Lesson 2.

### 4. Why this characteristic is the quiet turning point of the topic

⚠️ Permanence looks like the least interesting of the five. ⚠️ In fact it is the hinge, because it forces the chapter to introduce the state/government distinction two pages before the appraisal that will rest everything on it.

```text
   PERMANENCE (p.185)
     needs a bearer that outlives governments
          -> STATE / GOVERNMENT distinction introduced
                    |
                    v
   INDIVISIBILITY (pp.186-187)
     needs a holder distinct from the organs exercising powers
          -> SOVEREIGNTY / POWER distinction, built on the same split
                    |
                    v
   APPRAISAL (p.189)
     needs a remedy for the mortal, fallible sovereign
          -> sovereignty attributed to the STATE as "an abstract idea";
             GOVERNMENT given only "definite powers... with inbuilt
             checks and safeguards" => CONSTITUTIONAL GOVERNMENT
                    |
                    v
   CHAPTER 8 (p.202, Laski)
     needs a way to change rulers without dissolving the order
          -> "government is but the agent of the state"; the value of
             the distinction is "the possibility it offers of creating
             institutional mechanisms for changing the agents of the
             state"
```

⚠️ One distinction, introduced to rescue a minor characteristic, ends by carrying the chapter's constitutional conclusion and the pluralists' practical programme. 🔑 Trace it in an answer and you are demonstrating the structure of the argument rather than its contents.

### 5. Example, and the limit of the example

⚠️ A state changes its government six times in a decade through ordinary procedures. ⚠️ On the chapter's account nothing whatever has happened to its sovereignty: each government held **assigned powers**, the state retained the sovereignty, and the continuity **will not be affected by such changes**.

❌ **Where the example stops.** ⚠️ It handles instability of *personnel* and says nothing about instability of *authority*. ⚠️ If six successive governments are each unable to secure habitual obedience, Austin's test is in difficulty even though the chapter's permanence claim is untouched — because permanence is a property of the state's legal continuity, not a guarantee that anyone is obeying. ⚠️ The chapter does not address this gap directly; the nearest it comes is the erosion clause at p.180.

### 6. Comparison: permanence against the other four

| ⚠️ Characteristic | ⚠️ Is it a property of the state or of the sovereign body? | ⚠️ What ends it |
|---|---|---|
| **Absoluteness** | Of the sovereign's legal position | ⚠️ Subordination to a superior authority |
| **Permanence** | ✅ Expressly of the **state** | ❌ Loss of independence; **suzerainty of another state** |
| **Universality** | Of the state's jurisdiction | ⚠️ Nothing short of loss of territory |
| **Inalienability** | Of sovereignty as such | ⚠️ Transfer would destroy **the state itself** |
| **Indivisibility** | Of final legal authority | ⚠️ Division would make commands **inconsistent, conflicting and ambiguous** |

⚠️ Permanence is the only one of the five whose bearer the chapter names explicitly, and that naming is why it carries the state/government distinction.

### 7. Criticism and reply

⚠️ **Objection.** The state/government distinction saves permanence by making sovereignty the property of an abstraction. But an abstraction cannot declare law, issue commands, take decisions or command physical force — the four things Lesson 1 said a sovereign does. So the characteristic is preserved only by relocating sovereignty to something that cannot exercise it.

✅ **Reply.** The chapter accepts the relocation and treats it as a benefit rather than a cost. ✅ At p.189 it says that sovereignty should be **regarded as an attribute of the state — conceived as an abstract idea — while the government consisting of human beings... is only assigned definite powers**. ⚠️ Separating the bearer from the exerciser is exactly what permits **inbuilt checks and safeguards** and prevents any organ becoming **omnipotent**; a sovereignty that could only be held by whoever currently exercises it would make every check a diminution of sovereignty itself.

⚠️ **Residual.** The reply is strong and leaves one question open, which MacIver presses at p.204. ⚠️ If sovereignty belongs to an abstract state and the government merely holds powers, then in any actual dispute about who may do what, sovereignty does no work — and it becomes tempting to say, with MacIver, that **it is not the office but the instrument, not sovereignty but the law and the constitution that wear the legitimate armour of might**. ⚠️ The chapter does not answer this; it records both positions.

### 8. Using this in an answer

- ⚠️ **Syllabus relevance.** The state/government distinction is the single most transferable idea in the topic, and permanence is where the chapter introduces it.
- ⚠️ **Probable framing.** Any stem on sovereignty in a democracy requires this distinction in the first three lines; so does any stem on Laski.
- ❌ **Trap.** Writing that sovereignty is eternal. ✅ The chapter expressly denies it and names the terminating condition.
- ✅ **Answer use.** The four-stage trace in §4 is a ready-made analytical spine for a 15- or 20-mark answer on the state/government distinction.

### Revision notes

1. ✅ **Permanence of sovereignty is the corollary of its absoluteness.** (PDF p.185)
2. ✅ Bodin: **if power be held only for a certain time (it does not matter how long a time), it is not sovereign power, and he who holds it for that time is not a sovereign**.
3. 🔑 The parenthesis is the argument — any term, however long, implies a grantor, and a grantor is a superior.
4. ✅ **Theoretically, sovereignty belongs to the state.**
5. ✅ **Governments may be formed and dissolved according to established procedure, but the continuity of the state will not be affected by such changes.**
6. ⚠️ The qualifier **according to established procedure** excludes the revolutionary case, which is handled separately by de jure/de facto.
7. ✅ **Sovereignty endures as long as the state maintains its independence.**
8. ✅ Permanence **should not be interpreted to mean that sovereignty is eternal**.
9. 🔑 Terminating condition: if a state **loses its independence and is placed under the suzerainty of another state, its sovereignty is also lost**.
10. ⚠️ Permanence is the only characteristic whose bearer the chapter names expressly — the **state**.
11. ⚠️ The distinction introduced here carries four later loads: permanence, the sovereignty/power resolution, the constitutional-government appraisal, and Laski's accountability mechanism.
12. ⚠️ Objection: sovereignty is preserved by relocating it to an abstraction that cannot exercise it; reply: that separation is what permits **inbuilt checks and safeguards**.
13. ⚠️ Residual: MacIver's claim that **not sovereignty but the law and the constitution** carry legitimate might.

### Concept check

**Question:** Permanence is said to be a corollary of absoluteness. Reconstruct the derivation, then show why the characteristic cannot be stated at all without a distinction the chapter had not yet introduced.

**Model answer:** ✅ The derivation runs through Bodin: **if power be held only for a certain time (it does not matter how long a time), it is not sovereign power**. ⚠️ A term-limited power is held on someone's grant; a grant implies a grantor; a grantor is a superior; and absoluteness denies any superior. ⚠️ So absoluteness in space entails absoluteness in time, which is perpetuity. ✅ The characteristic then meets an obvious fact — governments change constantly — and the chapter answers only by distinguishing **state** from **government**: **theoretically, sovereignty belongs to the state**, while **governments may be formed and dissolved according to established procedure** without affecting **the continuity of the state**. ⚠️ Without that distinction, permanence would be plainly false of every political system; with it, permanence becomes a claim about the state's legal continuity alone. ✅ The chapter also fixes the limit: sovereignty is not **eternal**, and is **lost** if the state is placed under the **suzerainty of another state**.

**Misconception to avoid:** ❌ Do not treat permanence as a claim that sovereign *bodies* last. ⚠️ The chapter's claim is about the state; the sovereign body may change by procedure, by amendment or by revolution without permanence being disturbed — what disturbs it is loss of independence.

### Lesson Mains practice

**Mains question [10]:** Why does the chapter introduce the distinction between state and government at the point of permanence rather than later? *(Examine — answer in about 150 words.)*

**Mains model:** ✅ Because permanence is unstatable without it. ⚠️ The characteristic asserts that sovereignty is perpetual, following Bodin's rule that power held **only for a certain time** is **not sovereign power**; but governments visibly change, so without a bearer that outlasts them the claim is refuted by ordinary observation. ✅ The chapter therefore says that **theoretically, sovereignty belongs to the state**, that **governments may be formed and dissolved according to established procedure**, and that **the continuity of the state will not be affected by such changes**. ⚠️ The distinction, introduced here to rescue a minor characteristic, then carries three further loads: the sovereignty/power resolution of the federal paradox, the appraisal's demand that government hold only **definite powers... with inbuilt checks and safeguards**, and Laski's claim that **government is but the agent of the state**. 🔑 A local repair that becomes the chapter's structural spine.

**Rubric [10]:** Bodin's perpetuity rule with the *does not matter how long a time* clause **2** · the refutation-by-observation problem stated **2** · the chapter's three-part answer — belongs to the state, governments change by procedure, continuity unaffected **3** · at least two later uses of the distinction **2** · a closing line identifying it as structural rather than local **1**.
