## Lesson 21 — De jure and de facto, and Austin's refusal

Progress: 21 / 38 | Stage: Core | Part IV — aspects, and the first appraisal

━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
Book context: Core owner §5's **de jure vs de facto** entry and §7's second illustrative example, which names Cromwell, Napoleon and the Bolsheviks as historical illustrations, with the Advanced companion §4's entry and §12's warning against converting those illustrations into present-tense examples, read against the **DE JURE AND DE FACTO SOVEREIGNTY** section spanning PDF pp.187-188 (printed 170-171).
CA search: "de facto sovereignty recognition coup legitimacy doctrine political theory 2025 2026"
CA found: ⚠️ nothing returned that bears on the chapter's conceptual treatment, and no contemporary instance is named in this file by design. ❌ No linkage is carried into this lesson.
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

---

### 1. The condition that produces the distinction

⚠️ This aspect has a precise trigger, and the trigger is part of the definition:

✅ The chapter defines a revolution as **a successful overthrow of the existing regime in a state through unconstitutional means — that is not by the established procedure of replacing the existing government**.

🔑 Compare Lesson 15. ⚠️ There, governments change **according to established procedure** and sovereignty is untouched. ⚠️ Here the procedure is bypassed, and the chapter's own permanence argument no longer covers the case. ⚠️ The de jure/de facto distinction exists to fill exactly that gap.

```text
     ORDINARY CHANGE                    REVOLUTIONARY CHANGE
  "according to established          "through unconstitutional means
   procedure"                         -- that is NOT by the established
         |                             procedure"
         v                                      |
  one legal sovereign throughout                v
  (Lesson 15, permanence)            TWO SOVEREIGNS, TEMPORARILY
                                              |
                               +--------------+--------------+
                               |                             |
                        DE JURE SOVEREIGN            DE FACTO SOVEREIGN
                        "legal or formal"            "the actual sovereign"
                        HAS LOST his real            NOT YET legally
                        powers                       established
```

### 2. The two sovereigns, defined

✅ The chapter's formulation is exact:

> Till such time as the new sovereign is legally established and recognized, there may exist two sovereigns — one in the legal sense who has lost his real powers; the other in the factual sense who has not yet been legally established. Under such circumstances, the legal or formal sovereign is said to retain de jure sovereignty while the actual sovereign is characterized by de facto sovereignty.
>
> — Gauba, Ch.7, PDF pp.187-188 (printed 170-171)

⚠️ Two qualifiers in that passage repay attention. ⚠️ **Till such time as** makes the condition explicitly temporary. ⚠️ **Legally established and recognized** names two things, not one — establishment and recognition — and the chapter does not say whether recognition means domestic or international, which is an honest gap in the material available here.

### 3. Why it cannot last

✅ The chapter supplies a reason rather than an assertion:

> This state of affairs can only continue temporarily because it is marked by uncertainty, confusion and irregularity.
>
> — Gauba, Ch.7, PDF p.188 (printed 171)

⚠️ Compare the functional argument for indivisibility at Lesson 18 — division produces **inconsistent, conflicting and ambiguous commands**. 🔑 It is the same argument applied across time rather than across levels: a legal order cannot operate with two claimants to finality, so the condition resolves itself one way or the other.

### 4. The historical illustrations, and a standing caution

✅ The chapter names three: **the authority exercised by Cromwell in England, by Napoleon in France, and the Bolshevist group in Russia after 1917**. ✅ It adds a category: **the military dictatorships of the present-day world, established after a coup d'etat, also represent de facto sovereignty until they evolve suitable means to legitimize their authority.**

⚠️ Two cautions govern how these are used.

❌ First, the three named cases are **historical illustrations in a 2009 book** and must not be converted into present-tense examples; the canonical owners flag this expressly. ⚠️ Second, the chapter's phrase **the present-day world** is relative to its own publication; ❌ no contemporary state or government is named anywhere in this file, and none should be imported into an answer on the authority of this chapter.

✅ The chapter also explains *why* a de facto sovereign is obeyed at all: **in actual practice its commands are obeyed by the people, either because of sheer force or a strong following among the people**. ⚠️ Note the disjunction — **sheer force or a strong following** — which keeps two very different cases inside one category and is worth naming in an answer.

### 5. Austin's refusal, and the counter-argument

⚠️ This is the part of the section candidates most often miss, and it carries high marks because it shows the doctrine disputing itself.

✅ **Some writers, including John Austin, refuse to draw a distinction between de jure and de facto sovereignty.** ✅ Their argument, as the chapter gives it:

| # | ✅ Step | ⚠️ What it does |
|---|---|---|
| 1 | Adjectives like **'lawful' and 'unlawful' cannot be applied to the term sovereignty** | Sovereignty is the source of lawfulness, so it cannot itself be assessed for lawfulness |
| 2 | Sovereignty **becomes meaningless without legal sanction** | Strips the factual claimant of the title |
| 3 | It would be **more logical if the terms de facto and de jure are applied only to government, not to sovereignty** | Relocates the distinction to the level where it belongs |
| 4 | **The de jure sovereignty alone is sovereignty in the real sense of the term** | Restores unity |
| 5 | **The so-called de facto sovereignty remains inconsequential until it achieves the status of de jure sovereignty** | Converts the factual claimant into a candidate rather than a sovereign |

🔑 Step 3 is the elegant move: the state/government distinction, again. ⚠️ Governments may indeed be de facto or de jure; sovereignty, which belongs to the state, cannot be.

⚠️ Observe the irony. ⚠️ Austin's test of sovereignty was behavioural — **habitual obedience from the bulk of a given society** — which looks as though it should favour the de facto holder. ⚠️ Yet here Austin is reported as insisting on legal title. ⚠️ The chapter does not comment on the tension; it is a genuine strain in the position and is best handled in an answer by noting it rather than resolving it.

### 6. Comparison: three ways to describe a post-revolutionary situation

| ⚠️ Account | ✅ Who is sovereign | ⚠️ Strength | ⚠️ Weakness |
|---|---|---|---|
| **Two-sovereign (the chapter's)** | Both, temporarily — one de jure, one de facto | Describes the situation as participants experience it | Admits two finalities, which the indivisibility argument forbids |
| **Austin's refusal** | Only the de jure holder | Preserves unity and the link between sovereignty and legal sanction | ⚠️ Strained against Austin's own obedience test; leaves the obeyed authority unnamed |
| **Relocate to government** | Sovereignty stays with the **state**; the **government** is de facto or de jure | ✅ Consistent with permanence and with the sovereignty/power distinction | ⚠️ Says nothing about what happens if no government achieves legal establishment |

⚠️ The third row is the chapter's own best resource, and it is the one to use in an answer that must choose.

### 7. Criticism and reply

⚠️ **Objection.** The de facto category is doing illegitimate work. If an authority is obeyed **because of sheer force**, calling it a sovereign of any kind dignifies coercion with a legal vocabulary; and if it is obeyed because of **a strong following among the people**, it is closer to a legitimate authority awaiting formalities. The category lumps together conquest and popular revolution.

✅ **Reply.** The breadth is deliberate and descriptive. ⚠️ The chapter's purpose in this section is not to evaluate claimants but to record that legal title and effective control can come apart; whether a given de facto authority deserves to become de jure is a question of legitimacy, which the chapter treats elsewhere — at p.180 through the erosion clause and at p.188 through the requirement that a regime **evolve suitable means to legitimize their authority**.

⚠️ **Residual.** The reply is accurate and leaves the deeper difficulty. ⚠️ If **suitable means to legitimize** are what convert de facto into de jure, then legitimacy is prior to legal title — which inverts the relation the whole classical theory assumes, where the sovereign is the source of legality. ⚠️ MacIver draws precisely that inference at p.205, holding that **the stability of the authority of government depends on its legitimacy for which it must conform to the prevalent traditions and social values**.

### 8. Using this in an answer

- ⚠️ **Syllabus relevance.** This aspect is the standard material for any stem asking who is sovereign during disorder, and it supplies the chapter's clearest internal dispute.
- ⚠️ **Probable framing.** Use the two-sovereign description, then Austin's five-step refusal, then the relocation to government as the resolution.
- ❌ **Trap.** Treating Cromwell, Napoleon and the Bolsheviks as contemporary cases, or supplying a modern instance on this chapter's authority. ✅ They are historical illustrations in a 2009 text.
- ✅ **Answer use.** The sentence **the so-called de facto sovereignty remains inconsequential until it achieves the status of de jure sovereignty** is the sharpest available statement of the legalist position.

### Revision notes

1. ✅ A revolution is **a successful overthrow of the existing regime in a state through unconstitutional means — that is not by the established procedure of replacing the existing government**. (PDF p.187)
2. ✅ **Till such time as the new sovereign is legally established and recognized, there may exist two sovereigns.**
3. ✅ **De jure** = the **legal or formal** sovereign **who has lost his real powers**; **de facto** = the **actual sovereign** who **has not yet been legally established**.
4. ✅ The condition **can only continue temporarily because it is marked by uncertainty, confusion and irregularity**.
5. ⚠️ That is the indivisibility argument applied across time rather than across levels.
6. ✅ Historical illustrations: **Cromwell in England**, **Napoleon in France**, **the Bolshevist group in Russia after 1917**.
7. ✅ **Military dictatorships... established after a coup d'etat** also represent de facto sovereignty **until they evolve suitable means to legitimize their authority**.
8. ✅ A de facto sovereign's commands are obeyed **either because of sheer force or a strong following among the people**.
9. ✅ **Some writers, including John Austin, refuse to draw a distinction** between the two.
10. ✅ Their argument: **'lawful' and 'unlawful' cannot be applied to the term sovereignty**, which **becomes meaningless without legal sanction**.
11. 🔑 **It would be more logical if the terms de facto and de jure are applied only to government, not to sovereignty.**
12. ✅ **The de jure sovereignty alone is sovereignty in the real sense of the term**; the de facto **remains inconsequential until it achieves the status of de jure sovereignty**.
13. ⚠️ Tension to note, not resolve: Austin's own test is behavioural obedience, which appears to favour the de facto holder.
14. ❌ Never convert the three historical illustrations into present-tense examples.

### Concept check

**Question:** Austin's test of sovereignty is habitual obedience, yet Austin is reported as refusing the de facto category. Explain the apparent inconsistency and state the best available reconciliation.

**Model answer:** ⚠️ The apparent inconsistency is real. ✅ Austin's definition makes sovereignty a matter of a **determinate human superior... receiv[ing] habitual obedience from the bulk of a given society**, which in a revolutionary situation is satisfied by the authority whose **commands are obeyed by the people, either because of sheer force or a strong following**. ✅ Yet the chapter reports that writers **including John Austin** deny the distinction, on the ground that **'lawful' and 'unlawful' cannot be applied to the term sovereignty** since it **becomes meaningless without legal sanction**, concluding that **the de jure sovereignty alone is sovereignty in the real sense of the term**. ⚠️ The best reconciliation is the chapter's own third step: the terms should be **applied only to government, not to sovereignty**. ✅ On that reading Austin's obedience test identifies the de facto *government*, while sovereignty remains with the state — which also preserves permanence and the sovereignty/power distinction. ⚠️ The reconciliation is not stated as Austin's own, and it should be presented as a resolution rather than as his view.

**Misconception to avoid:** ❌ Do not present de facto sovereignty as a settled, permanent category. ⚠️ The chapter is explicit that it **can only continue temporarily**, is marked by **uncertainty, confusion and irregularity**, and **remains inconsequential until it achieves the status of de jure sovereignty**.

### Lesson Mains practice

**Mains question [10]:** Examine the distinction between de jure and de facto sovereignty and the objection to it. *(Examine — answer in about 150 words.)*

**Mains model:** ✅ The distinction arises only where a regime is overthrown **through unconstitutional means — that is not by the established procedure**. ✅ Until the new authority is **legally established and recognized**, there may exist two sovereigns: the **legal or formal** one **who has lost his real powers**, and the **actual** one **who has not yet been legally established**, obeyed **either because of sheer force or a strong following among the people**. ⚠️ Illustrations: **Cromwell**, **Napoleon** and **the Bolshevist group in Russia after 1917**, the condition being temporary, **marked by uncertainty, confusion and irregularity**. ✅ The objection, from writers **including John Austin**: **'lawful' and 'unlawful' cannot be applied to the term sovereignty**, so the terms should be **applied only to government, not to sovereignty**, and **the de jure sovereignty alone is sovereignty in the real sense**. ⚠️ It is strengthened by permanence and weakened by Austin's own obedience test. 🔑 A transitional description, contested at its root.

**Rubric [10]:** the revolutionary trigger defined **2** · both categories defined with the lost-powers and not-yet-established clauses **2** · at least two historical illustrations **1** · the temporariness reason **1** · Austin's objection with at least two of its steps **3** · the honest note that the objection sits awkwardly with Austin's own test **1**.

## Lesson 22 — Chapter 7's appraisal: the mortal sovereign

Progress: 22 / 38 | Stage: Core | Part IV — aspects, and the first appraisal

━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
Book context: Core owner §4's spine, which records the chapter's balanced position that legal sovereignty remains analytically useful while political wisdom requires limiting governmental omnipotence, and §5's state/government entry; with the Advanced companion §3's step 6, read against the **A CRITICAL APPRAISAL** section spanning PDF pp.188-189 (printed 171-172).
CA search: "sovereignty constitutional government checks and safeguards omnipotence political theory 2025 2026"
CA found: ⚠️ nothing returned that bears on the chapter's appraisal as argued; no further current linkage is permitted in this file. ❌ No linkage is carried into this lesson.
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

---

### 1. What the concept achieved — stated before it is criticised

⚠️ The appraisal is a model of fair-minded assessment, and candidates should copy its order: achievement first, then cost, then remedy.

✅ The achievements are four, and each is historically specific:

| # | ✅ What sovereignty did | ⚠️ Against what |
|---|---|---|
| 1 | **Sought to free the modern state from obligations towards other social institutions** | **Particularly the authority of feudal lords and the papacy** |
| 2 | **Establish the dignity of the state in the international sphere** | The absence of standing for the new monarchies |
| 3 | **Proclaimed the monopoly of the state in deciding public policy and declaring the law that binds all citizens in unequivocal terms** | Competing law-declaring authorities |
| 4 | **Instituted the nation-state as an independent entity in the international sphere, not bound by any organization superior to itself** | Imperial and papal claims to superiority |

✅ The summary: **in short, sovereignty armed the state with supreme authority in its internal as well as external affairs.**

✅ And the chapter restates the necessity in functional terms: **the state cannot perform its function of regulating the life of its citizens unless it is equipped with supreme authority**, from which **the logic of the legal character of sovereignty postulates that it has to be absolute, permanent, universal, inalienable and indivisible**.

🔑 That sentence is the best available defence of the five characteristics, because it derives them from a *function* rather than from a definition.

### 2. The careful clarification about moral obligations

✅ The chapter then clarifies a point it has made twice before, and this is its sharpest formulation:

> When we say that the sovereign is not bound by any moral principles or international obligations except at his own will, it is only intended to emphasize that the sovereign cannot be subordinated to any other human agency for interpretation of such obligations.
>
> — Gauba, Ch.7, PDF p.188 (printed 171)

⚠️ Read this closely, because it is finer than the earlier statements. ⚠️ The claim is not that the sovereign has no moral obligations, nor even that he alone decides whether he has them. ⚠️ It is that no other **human agency** may be the authoritative **interpreter** of them. 🔑 The denial is about *jurisdiction over interpretation*, not about the existence of obligation.

⚠️ This is the most precise statement in the two chapters of what legal supremacy actually denies, and it is the single best sentence to deploy against the charge that classical sovereignty is amoral.

### 3. The turn: the mortal sovereign

✅ The appraisal then pivots with one of the chapter's strongest passages:

> But in the sphere of actual practice, all authority postulates responsibility. Sovereignty, being supreme authority, postulates highest responsibility. Theoretically, we may evolve the image of a perfect sovereign and thus justify him exercising supreme authority. But in the real world, sovereignty has to be vested in human beings who, being mortals, are as imperfect as any other human beings.
>
> — Gauba, Ch.7, PDF pp.188-189 (printed 171-172)

```text
   THE INFERENCE CHAIN
   -------------------
   all authority postulates RESPONSIBILITY
        |
   supreme authority postulates HIGHEST responsibility
        |
   but sovereignty must be vested in MORTALS, "as imperfect as
   any other human beings"
        |
   and when the authority of a MORTAL human sovereign is
   proclaimed ABSOLUTE, PERPETUAL, BEYOND ANY RESPONSIBILITY
   OR OBLIGATION, it leads to "SOCIALLY DISASTROUS CONSEQUENCES"
        |
        +--> "the most cruel and degenerate forms of AUTOCRACY,
             TYRANNY and AUTHORITARIANISM"
        +--> "UNTOLD OPPRESSION inside the state"
        +--> "MORE DESTRUCTIVE WARS outside"
```

⚠️ Three named consequences, two internal and one external. ⚠️ Note that the argument does not require any particular sovereign to be wicked; it requires only that sovereigns be human. 🔑 That is what makes it a structural criticism rather than a complaint.

### 4. The remedy, and the admission that it changes the doctrine

✅ The chapter's remedy is stated as an imperative:

> To straighten the issue, it is imperative to draw a distinction between the state and the government so that sovereignty is always regarded as an attribute of the state — conceived as an abstract idea — while the government consisting of human beings, whatever their wisdom or excellence of character, is only assigned definite powers, to be exercised through an established procedure with inbuilt checks and safeguards. This is necessary so that no organ of the government is able to become omnipotent.
>
> — Gauba, Ch.7, PDF p.189 (printed 172)

✅ And then the admission that most summaries omit and that is worth more than the remedy itself:

> This would imply a departure from the original concept of sovereignty which sought to vest sovereignty in a 'determinate person or body of persons'. This would mean a constitutional government.
>
> — Gauba, Ch.7, PDF p.189 (printed 172)

🔑 **A departure from the original concept.** ⚠️ The chapter is conceding that its own remedy is not a refinement of Bodin and Austin but a move away from them — because a sovereignty vested in an abstract state is precisely not a sovereignty vested in a **determinate person or body of persons**.

✅ The closing justification: only a constitutional government **can be expected to function in a responsible manner and deliver the goods, especially in the present age of democracy when the people are claimed to be the real sovereign.**

### 5. Example, and the limit of the example

⚠️ Consider any arrangement in which a legislature's enactments are subject to a procedure, an organ's powers are enumerated and officials may be replaced. ⚠️ On the appraisal's account this is the practical content of the remedy: definite powers, established procedure, inbuilt checks, no omnipotent organ.

❌ **Where the example stops.** ⚠️ The chapter supplies the *principle* of constitutional government and not its machinery, and this file does not supply the machinery either. ➡️ Constitutional text, institutional design, judicial review and amendment procedure belong to other owners. ⚠️ What this topic owns is the argument that gets you to constitutionalism **from** the theory of sovereignty, which is a different and more examinable thing than a description of constitutional arrangements.

### 6. Comparison: three appraisals in two chapters

| ⚠️ Where | ✅ Verdict | ⚠️ Remedy proposed |
|---|---|---|
| **Ch.7 appraisal (pp.188-189)** | The legal concept is necessary; vesting it in mortals is dangerous | **Constitutional government**: sovereignty to the abstract state, definite powers to government |
| **Ch.8 appraisal (pp.207-208)** | Pluralism rightly limits the state; but groups are unequal and corporations dominant | A **stronger base for the state sovereignty than what the pluralist theory might concede** |
| **Ch.7 conclusion on popular sovereignty (p.193)** | Popular sovereignty is the cornerstone of classical democracy; confusing right with duty opens **the floodgates of absolutism** | Again **constitutional government**, with **effective checks on the power-holders** |

🔑 Read the remedy column downward. ⚠️ Twice the answer is constitutional government and once it is a stronger state; and the two are not in tension, because a constitutionally limited state can be strong against private concentrations precisely by being accountable. ⚠️ A candidate who can say that has synthesised both chapters in one sentence.

### 7. Criticism and reply

⚠️ **Objection.** The appraisal gives up the game. If sovereignty must be relocated to an abstraction and governments confined to assigned powers, then the classical doctrine has been replaced by constitutionalism, and the five characteristics are being retained as vocabulary rather than as doctrine.

✅ **Reply.** The chapter can answer that the legal concept remains indispensable and unchanged in its own register: the state still **cannot perform its function of regulating the life of its citizens unless it is equipped with supreme authority**, and the characteristics still follow from that. ⚠️ What changes is the *location* of sovereignty, not its existence — and location was always contested, as titular, de jure and de facto sovereignty have just shown.

⚠️ **Residual.** The reply holds and the objection has already been half-conceded, in the chapter's own words: the remedy **would imply a departure from the original concept**. ⚠️ Chapter 8 then shows what the departure costs: once the determinate person or body is gone, Laski can ask where the sovereign is, and MacIver can answer that it is **not sovereignty but the law and the constitution that wear the legitimate armour of might**. ⚠️ The chapter's own remedy opens the door through which the pluralists walk.

### 8. Using this in an answer

- ⚠️ **Syllabus relevance.** The appraisal is the bridge between the two chapters and the source of the strongest evaluative material available inside this topic.
- ⚠️ **Probable framing.** Any stem inviting a judgment on classical sovereignty should use the appraisal's own order: achievements, the interpretation clarification, the mortal-sovereign argument, constitutional government, and the admitted departure.
- ❌ **Trap.** Presenting the mortal-sovereign argument as a pluralist criticism. ✅ It is the chapter's own, made before Chapter 8 begins.
- ✅ **Answer use.** The interpretation clarification in §2 is the best single defence available against the charge that classical sovereignty is amoral; the departure admission in §4 is the best single concession. Use one when defending and the other when criticising.

### Revision notes

1. ✅ Sovereignty **sought to free the modern state from obligations towards other social institutions, particularly the authority of feudal lords and the papacy**, and to **establish the dignity of the state in the international sphere**. (PDF p.188)
2. ✅ It **proclaimed the monopoly of the state in deciding public policy and declaring the law that binds all citizens in unequivocal terms**.
3. ✅ It **instituted the nation-state as an independent entity in the international sphere, not bound by any organization superior to itself**.
4. ✅ **Sovereignty armed the state with supreme authority in its internal as well as external affairs.**
5. ✅ Functional necessity: the state **cannot perform its function of regulating the life of its citizens unless it is equipped with supreme authority**, whence the five characteristics follow.
6. 🔑 The interpretation clarification: the sovereign **cannot be subordinated to any other human agency for interpretation** of moral or international obligations — a denial of interpretive jurisdiction, not of obligation.
7. ✅ **All authority postulates responsibility. Sovereignty, being supreme authority, postulates highest responsibility.**
8. ✅ But sovereignty must be **vested in human beings who, being mortals, are as imperfect as any other human beings**.
9. ✅ Absolute, perpetual, unaccountable authority leads to **the most cruel and degenerate forms of autocracy, tyranny and authoritarianism, untold oppression inside the state and more destructive wars outside**.
10. ✅ The remedy: sovereignty as **an attribute of the state — conceived as an abstract idea** — while government is **only assigned definite powers, to be exercised through an established procedure with inbuilt checks and safeguards**.
11. ✅ Purpose: **so that no organ of the government is able to become omnipotent**.
12. 🔑 The admission: this **would imply a departure from the original concept of sovereignty which sought to vest sovereignty in a 'determinate person or body of persons'**, and **would mean a constitutional government**.
13. ✅ Only such a government can **function in a responsible manner and deliver the goods**, especially **in the present age of democracy when the people are claimed to be the real sovereign**.
14. ⚠️ The remedy opens the door the pluralists walk through: remove the determinate person, and the sovereign becomes hard to find.

### Concept check

**Question:** The chapter's own remedy for the dangers of sovereignty is said to be "a departure from the original concept". Explain what exactly departs, and why the chapter nevertheless adopts it.

**Model answer:** ✅ What departs is the **location** of sovereignty. ⚠️ The original concept **sought to vest sovereignty in a 'determinate person or body of persons'** — Austin's formulation exactly. ✅ The remedy instead makes sovereignty **an attribute of the state — conceived as an abstract idea** — while the government is **only assigned definite powers, to be exercised through an established procedure with inbuilt checks and safeguards**. ⚠️ An abstract state is by definition not a determinate person or body, so the determinacy requirement is abandoned. ✅ The chapter adopts the departure because the alternative is worse: **all authority postulates responsibility**, sovereignty must be vested in **mortals** who are **as imperfect as any other human beings**, and unaccountable absolute authority produces **autocracy, tyranny and authoritarianism, untold oppression inside the state and more destructive wars outside**. ⚠️ The price is that the sovereign becomes hard to locate — the opening Laski and MacIver exploit in Chapter 8. 🔑 Determinacy traded for safety.

**Misconception to avoid:** ❌ Do not treat the mortal-sovereign argument as a pluralist criticism quoted by the author. ⚠️ It is Chapter 7's own appraisal, five pages before Chapter 8 begins, which is why it is far more damaging: the exposition and the criticism come from the same pen.

### Lesson Mains practice

**Mains question [10]:** Assess the chapter's critical appraisal of the concept of sovereignty. *(Assess — answer in about 150 words.)*

**Mains model:** ✅ The appraisal is balanced and self-critical. ⚠️ It first credits the concept with freeing the state from **the authority of feudal lords and the papacy**, establishing its **dignity in the international sphere**, proclaiming a **monopoly... in deciding public policy and declaring the law**, and instituting the nation-state as **not bound by any organization superior to itself**; and it grounds the five characteristics functionally, since the state **cannot perform its function of regulating the life of its citizens unless it is equipped with supreme authority**. ⚠️ It then turns: **all authority postulates responsibility**, yet sovereignty is vested in **mortals**, and absolute unaccountable authority yields **autocracy, tyranny and authoritarianism, untold oppression inside the state and more destructive wars outside**. ✅ The remedy is **constitutional government**, with sovereignty attributed to the abstract state and only **definite powers** to government. ⚠️ Its cost is openly admitted: **a departure from the original concept**. 🔑 Defence, diagnosis and a disclosed price.

**Rubric [10]:** at least three of the four historical achievements **2** · the functional derivation of the characteristics **1** · the responsibility-and-mortality argument **2** · all three named consequences **2** · the constitutional-government remedy with the abstract-state/definite-powers contrast **2** · the admitted departure **1**.

## Lesson 23 — Dicey's other sovereign

Progress: 23 / 38 | Stage: Core | Part V — popular sovereignty

━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
Book context: Core owner §3's definitions of **legal** and **political** sovereignty, which record that Gauba introduces political sovereignty through Dicey's distinction, and §8's comparison table; with the Advanced companion §3's step 4, read against the **WHAT IS POPULAR SOVEREIGNTY?** section opening at PDF p.189 (printed 172), which supplies the restatement of legal sovereignty, the monarchy observation and Dicey's quotation.
CA search: "Dicey legal sovereignty political sovereignty distinction constitutional theory 2025 2026"
CA found: ⚠️ nothing returned that bears on the 1885 distinction as the chapter uses it. ❌ No linkage is carried into this lesson.
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

---

### 1. The restatement that sets up the problem

⚠️ The chapter begins the popular-sovereignty section by restating legal sovereignty in its hardest form. ⚠️ This is a rhetorical choice: the harder the legal sovereign is made to look, the sharper the dilemma that follows.

✅ The restatement, in a single dense passage:

| ✅ Claim about the legal sovereign | ⚠️ Source of the claim |
|---|---|
| **Absolute and omnipotent**, functioning **solely according to his own judgment and will — right or wrong** | Absoluteness, Lesson 14 |
| Can **ignore and override** **prescriptions of divine law or natural law, principles of morality, dictates of public opinion** | Austin's repudiation of superior law, Lesson 12 |
| Vested in the **'determinate person or body of persons'** | Austin's definition, Lesson 12 |
| **Definitely organized, precise and recognized as the source of law** | Bodin's source-of-law claim, Lesson 5 |
| **Represents the will of the state**; its commands are **legally binding**; **disobedience is visited by penalties** | Austin's command theory, Lesson 12 |
| **The source of all rights in society** | Austin's rights-as-concessions, Lesson 12 |
| **Absolute, unlimited and supreme** | The characteristics, Lessons 14-19 |

🔑 Seven claims, every one already established. ⚠️ The chapter is deliberately gathering the full strength of the legal doctrine on one page before showing where it fails.

### 2. The observation about monarchy, and the dilemma

✅ Then the historical observation:

> In the political sphere, legal sovereignty closely corresponds to absolute monarchy. It is significant that Jean Bodin and John Austin — the chief exponents of legal sovereignty — had built their theories in the context of absolute monarchies. This sort of situation does not create problems of inconsistency between theory and practice.
>
> — Gauba, Ch.7, PDF p.189 (printed 172)

⚠️ This is the third time the chapter has made the monarchy point — at p.179 (**more befitting a monarch**), at p.187 (**best represented by an absolute monarchy**) and now here, with the added detail that **both** chief exponents wrote in that context. ⚠️ Repetition on this scale is the author signalling that the point is load-bearing.

✅ And the dilemma:

> However, as we move to other forms of government, we are faced with the dilemma of reconciling the idea of legal sovereignty with the actual powers of the sovereign body.
>
> — Gauba, Ch.7, PDF p.189 (printed 172)

### 3. Dicey's sentence

✅ The resolution is a quotation, and it should be memorised in substance:

> That is why A.C. Dicey (*An Introduction to the Study of the Law of Constitution*; 1885) observes: 'Behind the sovereign which the lawyer recognizes there is another sovereign to whom the legal sovereign must bow.' This other sovereign is described by Dicey as the political sovereign.
>
> — Gauba, Ch.7, PDF p.189 (printed 172)

```text
                  THE LAWYER'S SOVEREIGN
                  = the LEGAL sovereign
                    determinate, organised, recognised as the
                    source of law; commands legally binding
                              |
                              |  "must bow" to
                              v
                  ANOTHER SOVEREIGN
                  = the POLITICAL sovereign
                    the power actually behind the legal sovereign
                              |
                              |  when it "comprises all members of
                              |  a community, i.e. the people"
                              v
                  POPULAR SOVEREIGNTY
```

🔑 Note the conditional in the third step, because it is the chapter's own and is routinely dropped: popular sovereignty is a **special case** of political sovereignty, arising **when political sovereign comprises all members of a community, i.e. the people**. ⚠️ Political sovereignty is the genus; popular sovereignty is the species.

⚠️ That matters for precision. ⚠️ A political sovereign that was not the whole community — a decisive class, a dominant interest, an electorate narrower than the population — would still be a political sovereign and would not be a popular one.

### 4. What Dicey's move costs and buys

| ⚠️ | ✅ What it buys | ⚠️ What it costs |
|---|---|---|
| For the doctrine | Lets the legal theory stay exactly as Austin left it, while accounting for the obvious fact that legal sovereigns do not act as they please | Admits a **second** sovereign, which sits uneasily with **indivisibility** |
| For analysis | Supplies a vocabulary for the gap between legal competence and political reality | The political sovereign is not **determinate**, and so cannot be identified by Austin's test |
| For democracy | Gives the people a place in the theory without rewriting the legal account | ⚠️ Leaves the relation between the two sovereigns described metaphorically — **must bow** — rather than analysed |

🔑 The indivisibility strain is the sharpest point available here, and strong answers name it: the chapter has spent a page proving that **a divided sovereignty is a contradiction in terms**, and now reports with approval a doctrine of two sovereigns.

⚠️ The best defence available on the chapter's own materials is that *legal* and *political* sovereignty are not two finalities in the same register — indivisibility was asserted **in the legal sense**, and Dicey's second sovereign is not a legal authority at all. ⚠️ The chapter does not make this defence explicitly; it is an inference from the domain lock, and should be presented as such.

### 5. Example, and the limit of the example

⚠️ A legislature possesses the legal competence to enact a measure that would be rejected by an overwhelming majority of the population, and does not enact it. ⚠️ Dicey's vocabulary describes this exactly: the legal sovereign retains competence and **must bow** to the political sovereign.

❌ **Where the example stops.** ⚠️ It does not tell us what happens when the political sovereign is divided, which is the ordinary case, or how we would identify the political sovereign other than by observing what the legal sovereign declines to do. ⚠️ On that method the political sovereign is defined by its effects, which makes the claim close to unfalsifiable. ⚠️ MacIver presses exactly this at p.204 when he observes that **there is no homogeneous will of the people** and that **majority and minority opinions exist simultaneously**.

### 6. Comparison: three sovereignties, one territory

| ⚠️ Type | ✅ Who | ✅ Test | ⚠️ Weakness |
|---|---|---|---|
| **Legal** | The **determinate person or body of persons**, **recognized as the source of law** | Austin's: habitual obedience, no like superior | ⚠️ Competence without determinative force in practice |
| **Political** | **Another sovereign to whom the legal sovereign must bow** | ⚠️ None stated — identified by the legal sovereign's deference | ⚠️ Indeterminate; definable only by its effects |
| **Popular** | The political sovereign **when it comprises all members of a community, i.e. the people** | ⚠️ The people **in their corporate capacity** as **the embodiment of reason** | ⚠️ Rousseau's own concession: fits **direct democracy** in a small, uniform community |

⚠️ Candidates frequently merge rows two and three. 🔑 Keeping them apart is one of the most reliable ways to show precision on this topic.

### 7. Criticism and reply

⚠️ **Objection.** Dicey's distinction saves the legal theory by making it irrelevant. If the legal sovereign must always bow to a political sovereign, then the legal sovereign never decides anything that matters, and a theory of who may lawfully decide becomes a theory of empty competence.

✅ **Reply.** Legal competence is not empty merely because it is exercised under political constraint. ⚠️ The constraint operates on *which* enactments are made; the legal sovereign still determines what counts as an enactment, who is bound, and what follows from disobedience. ⚠️ The chapter's own functional defence applies: the state **cannot perform its function of regulating the life of its citizens unless it is equipped with supreme authority**, and that equipment is legal, not political.

⚠️ **Residual.** The reply is good and does not reach Laski's version of the objection, which the chapter prints without answer. ⚠️ Laski treats Dicey's distinction as **a proof of the absurdity of Austin's definition of sovereignty as determinate and indivisible**, and then goes further by showing that **even the idea of popular sovereignty is not workable**. ⚠️ If both the legal and the popular sovereign are unworkable as descriptions, the distinction has catalogued the problem rather than solved it.

### 8. Using this in an answer

- ⚠️ **Syllabus relevance.** The legal/political/popular triad is the most frequently required piece of apparatus in this topic after the state/government distinction.
- ⚠️ **Probable framing.** Any stem on sovereignty and democracy requires Dicey in the first paragraph; so does any stem on Laski's second front.
- ❌ **Trap.** Treating political and popular sovereignty as synonyms. ✅ Popular sovereignty is political sovereignty **when it comprises all members of a community**.
- ✅ **Answer use.** Quote Dicey's one sentence. It is short, it is exactly on point, and it carries the dated attribution — **1885** — that signals precision.

### Revision notes

1. ✅ The legal sovereign is **absolute and omnipotent**, functioning **solely according to his own judgment and will — right or wrong**. (PDF p.189)
2. ✅ He can **ignore and override** **divine law or natural law, principles of morality, dictates of public opinion**.
3. ✅ Legal sovereignty is vested in the **'determinate person or body of persons'**, **definitely organized, precise and recognized as the source of law**.
4. ✅ It **represents the will of the state**; its commands are **legally binding**; **disobedience is visited by penalties**; it is **the source of all rights in society**.
5. ✅ **In the political sphere, legal sovereignty closely corresponds to absolute monarchy**, and **both Bodin and Austin built their theories in the context of absolute monarchies**.
6. ⚠️ That is the third statement of the monarchy point, after pp.179 and 187 — the author is signalling that it is load-bearing.
7. ✅ The dilemma: as we move to other forms of government, we face **reconciling the idea of legal sovereignty with the actual powers of the sovereign body**.
8. 🔑 **Dicey** (*An Introduction to the Study of the Law of Constitution*; **1885**): **behind the sovereign which the lawyer recognizes there is another sovereign to whom the legal sovereign must bow**.
9. ✅ **This other sovereign is described by Dicey as the political sovereign.**
10. 🔑 Popular sovereignty arises **when political sovereign comprises all members of a community, i.e. the people** — a species of political sovereignty, not a synonym.
11. ⚠️ Strain: a second sovereign sits uneasily with **indivisibility**; the available defence is that the two are not finalities in the same register.
12. ⚠️ The political sovereign has no stated test and is identified by the legal sovereign's deference — close to definition by effects.
13. ⚠️ Laski uses the same distinction as **a proof of the absurdity of Austin's definition** and then denies that popular sovereignty is workable either.

### Concept check

**Question:** Dicey's distinction appears to introduce a second sovereign into a doctrine that has just insisted sovereignty is indivisible. Resolve the apparent conflict using material the chapter supplies.

**Model answer:** ⚠️ The conflict is apparent rather than real, and the resolution lies in the chapter's domain lock. ✅ Indivisibility was asserted **in the legal sense**: sovereignty must be **vested in a single body, legally competent to issue the final commands**, since division yields **inconsistent, conflicting and ambiguous commands**. ✅ Dicey's second sovereign is not a legal authority at all; it is **another sovereign to whom the legal sovereign must bow**, identified by the deference it commands rather than by legal competence. ⚠️ There is therefore one final *legal* authority and, behind it, a *political* power — two sovereigns in two registers, not two in one. ⚠️ The resolution is an inference from the chapter's own method rather than a statement it makes, and it carries a cost: the political sovereign has no test, is **not determinate**, and is knowable only through the legal sovereign's restraint. ⚠️ Laski treats precisely this as **a proof of the absurdity of Austin's definition of sovereignty as determinate and indivisible**.

**Misconception to avoid:** ❌ Do not say that Dicey's political sovereign *is* the people. ⚠️ The chapter's wording is conditional: popular sovereignty arises **when political sovereign comprises all members of a community, i.e. the people** — so a political sovereign narrower than the community is political but not popular.

### Lesson Mains practice

**Mains question [10]:** Distinguish legal, political and popular sovereignty, and explain why the distinction becomes necessary. *(Distinguish — answer in about 150 words.)*

**Mains model:** ✅ The **legal** sovereign is the **determinate person or body of persons**, **definitely organized, precise and recognized as the source of law**, whose commands are **legally binding** and whose **disobedience is visited by penalties**. ✅ The **political** sovereign is **another sovereign to whom the legal sovereign must bow**, in Dicey's formulation of **1885**. ✅ The **popular** sovereign is the political sovereign in the special case where it **comprises all members of a community, i.e. the people**, who are then **the source of all authority in the state**. ⚠️ The distinction becomes necessary because legal sovereignty **closely corresponds to absolute monarchy** — the context in which **both Bodin and Austin** wrote — so that in other forms of government there is a **dilemma of reconciling the idea of legal sovereignty with the actual powers of the sovereign body**. ⚠️ Its cost is that the political sovereign has no determinate test. 🔑 Three sovereigns, two registers, one territory.

**Rubric [10]:** legal sovereignty with at least three of its stated features **3** · Dicey's sentence with the 1885 attribution **2** · popular sovereignty stated as the conditional special case **2** · the monarchy-fit reason for the dilemma **2** · the indeterminacy cost acknowledged **1**.

## Lesson 24 — Popular sovereignty defined

Progress: 24 / 38 | Stage: Core | Part V — popular sovereignty

━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
Book context: Core owner §3's definition of **popular sovereignty** as the doctrine that the people in their corporate capacity are the source of all authority, and §9's second listed trap; with the Advanced companion §3's step 4 and §6's competing-perspectives row on popular sovereignty, read against PDF p.190 (printed 173), which supplies the full definition, the refusal of higher law and the boxed definition of **Natural Law**.
CA search: "popular sovereignty people as source of authority legitimacy democratic theory 2025 2026"
CA found: ⚠️ nothing returned that bears on the chapter's definitional paragraph; this file's single dated anchor is already carried at Lesson 2. ❌ No linkage is carried into this lesson.
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

---

### 1. The definition, unpacked clause by clause

✅ The chapter's full statement runs:

> When political sovereign comprises all members of a community, i.e. the people, the idea of its supreme legal authority is expressed as 'popular sovereignty'. In other words, the idea of popular sovereignty regards people as the source of all authority in the state. In its view, the organs of state which exercise supreme power of law-making, law-enforcement and adjudication, draw their legitimacy from the will of the people.
>
> — Gauba, Ch.7, PDF p.190 (printed 173)

| ✅ Clause | ⚠️ What it adds |
|---|---|
| **When political sovereign comprises all members of a community** | The conditional from Lesson 23 — popular sovereignty is a species, not a synonym |
| **People as the source of all authority in the state** | A claim about **origin**, not about who exercises |
| The organs of **law-making, law-enforcement and adjudication** **draw their legitimacy from the will of the people** | A claim about **legitimacy**, covering all three organs; note that *legitimacy*, not *competence*, is what is drawn |

🔑 Two different claims are doing different jobs. ⚠️ **Source of all authority** is genetic; **draw their legitimacy** is justificatory. ⚠️ Popular sovereignty is therefore the democratic analogue of Hobbes's two routes at Lesson 8 — origin and justification — with the holder changed.

### 2. The three refusals

⚠️ What makes the doctrine distinctive is as much what it declines as what it asserts. ✅ The chapter lists three refusals in a row:

```text
   IT DOES NOT RELY on any superior law --
        natural law, religious commandments or any other authority --
        to ascertain WHAT IS RIGHT OR WRONG
                          |
   IT DOES NOT LOOK for any source of "SUPERIOR REASON"
        apart from THE MIND OF THE PEOPLE
                          |
   IT REGARDS the people themselves, IN THEIR CORPORATE CAPACITY, as
        -- "the embodiment of reason"
        -- "the best judges of right and wrong"
        -- "hence the real source of supreme authority"
                          |
                          v
        "This doctrine comprises the CORNERSTONE of the CLASSICAL
         THEORY OF DEMOCRACY."
```

⚠️ The second refusal is the philosophically heaviest: there is no **superior reason** outside the people's own mind. ⚠️ That is a strong claim, and it is the one MacIver will later attack by asking whether a collective mind exists to be consulted.

⚠️ Note also the recurrence of **in their corporate capacity** — Rousseau's phrase from Lesson 10. ⚠️ Popular sovereignty is not the aggregate of individual opinions; it is the community considered as one bearer.

### 3. The boxed definition of natural law, and why it is placed here

✅ The chapter boxes a definition at exactly this point:

> **Natural Law.** A set of rules of good conduct which exist independently of conventional law. These are directly derived from nature which can be discovered through moral intuition and by the application of human faculty of reasoning. The champions of natural law regard it as superior to any other law.
>
> — Gauba, Ch.7, boxed definition, PDF p.190 (printed 173)

⚠️ The placement is argumentative. ⚠️ Popular sovereignty has just refused to rely on natural law, so the reader is told what has been refused. ⚠️ Three features of the boxed definition matter:

1. ⚠️ **Exist independently of conventional law** — it is not made, which is the medieval position from Lesson 5.
2. ⚠️ **Discovered through moral intuition and by the application of human faculty of reasoning** — it is found, by two faculties, which is what makes it available to anyone who reasons.
3. ⚠️ **Its champions regard it as superior to any other law** — the supremacy claim is attributed to its champions, not asserted by the chapter.

🔑 Put the three together and you can see why Austin had to deny that natural law is law **in the proper sense of the term**: a rule that exists independently, is discovered rather than commanded, and claims superiority is the exact negation of his three characteristics of law.

### 4. Where popular sovereignty stands relative to the other two

| ⚠️ Question | ✅ **Legal sovereignty** | ✅ **Popular sovereignty** |
|---|---|---|
| Who is it? | The **determinate person or body of persons** | **All members of a community**, in their **corporate capacity** |
| What does it supply? | Competence: commands that are **legally binding** | **Legitimacy** for the organs of **law-making, law-enforcement and adjudication** |
| What is its external reference? | None — it can **ignore and override** divine law, natural law, morality and opinion | None — it **does not rely on any superior law** and looks for no **superior reason** apart from the mind of the people |
| What is its status? | The lawyer's sovereign | **The cornerstone of the classical theory of democracy** |

🔑 Read row three carefully. ⚠️ Legal and popular sovereignty make the *same* refusal of external authority; they differ about who is the self-sufficient source. ⚠️ That is a genuinely illuminating point for an answer, because it shows popular sovereignty as a *relocation* of the classical structure rather than its rejection — which is what Lesson 10 established about Rousseau.

### 5. Example, and the limit of the example

⚠️ A constitution opens by declaring that all authority derives from the people. ⚠️ On the chapter's account that declaration is a popular-sovereignty claim: it says where authority comes from and from what the organs draw their legitimacy. ⚠️ It does not say who may enact a statute tomorrow, and it does not transfer any power to the citizenry.

❌ **Where the example stops.** ⚠️ Declarations of this kind are compatible with wildly different distributions of actual power, so the doctrine cannot by itself tell you whether a given system is one in which the people are sovereign in any operative sense. ⚠️ The chapter concedes the point itself at p.193, saying that in large, complex societies where **representative democracy is inevitable**, popular sovereignty **can only be accepted for its symbolic significance**.

### 6. Criticism and reply

⚠️ **Objection.** "The people in their corporate capacity" names nothing that can be consulted. Individuals have minds; communities do not. Treating the people as **the embodiment of reason** and **the best judges of right and wrong** substitutes a personification for an argument, and in practice whoever speaks for the corporate people exercises the authority attributed to them.

✅ **Reply.** The chapter's Rousseau anticipates this by defining the general will as the **common denominator of the real will** of all members — a construct built out of individual willing rather than a separate mind. ⚠️ On that reading the corporate capacity is not a mystical entity but a way of describing what remains when particular, momentary interests are set aside, and the traffic example at Lesson 26 shows the construction working on an ordinary case.

⚠️ **Residual.** The reply works for coordination cases and MacIver denies that it works generally. ⚠️ At p.204 he argues that **it is difficult to discover the existence of the general will in the state**, that **there are many persons in the state who differ from the prevalent will**, and that the states of the past **included vast numbers of aliens and slaves apart from the citizens whose will could not be reconciled with the so-called will of the state**. ⚠️ The chapter prints the criticism and does not rebut it.

### 7. Using this in an answer

- ⚠️ **Syllabus relevance.** Popular sovereignty is the chapter's bridge from the classical doctrine to democratic theory, and every stem about sovereignty and democracy passes through it.
- ⚠️ **Probable framing.** Define it by the two claims — source of authority, and legitimacy of the three organs — then give the three refusals, then the symbolic-significance concession.
- ❌ **Trap.** Writing that popular sovereignty means the people exercise power. ✅ The doctrine is about **source** and **legitimacy**; exercise belongs to the organs.
- ✅ **Answer use.** The phrase **cornerstone of the classical theory of democracy** is the chapter's own evaluation and is worth quoting when a stem asks about the doctrine's importance.

### Revision notes

1. ✅ Popular sovereignty arises **when political sovereign comprises all members of a community, i.e. the people**. (PDF p.190)
2. ✅ It **regards people as the source of all authority in the state**.
3. ✅ The organs of **law-making, law-enforcement and adjudication** **draw their legitimacy from the will of the people**.
4. 🔑 Two claims: **source** (genetic) and **legitimacy** (justificatory) — the democratic analogue of Hobbes's two routes.
5. ✅ Refusal one: it **does not rely on any superior law — natural law, religious commandments or any other authority — to ascertain what is right or wrong**.
6. ✅ Refusal two: it **does not look for any source of 'superior reason' apart from the mind of the people**.
7. ✅ Refusal three expressed positively: the people **in their corporate capacity** are **the embodiment of reason**, **the best judges of right and wrong**, and **hence the real source of supreme authority**.
8. ✅ **This doctrine comprises the cornerstone of the classical theory of democracy.**
9. ✅ Boxed **Natural Law**: rules of good conduct **which exist independently of conventional law**, **directly derived from nature**, **discovered through moral intuition and by the application of human faculty of reasoning**; **its champions regard it as superior to any other law**.
10. ⚠️ The box is placed here to show what popular sovereignty has just refused.
11. ⚠️ Natural law is the negation of Austin's three characteristics of law — not commanded, not from a determinate source, not sanction-backed.
12. 🔑 Legal and popular sovereignty make the **same** refusal of external authority and differ only about who is the self-sufficient source.
13. ⚠️ The chapter's own limit at p.193: in large societies with **representative democracy**, the doctrine **can only be accepted for its symbolic significance**.

### Concept check

**Question:** Show that popular sovereignty and Austinian legal sovereignty share a structural feature, and identify exactly where they diverge.

**Model answer:** ✅ The shared feature is the refusal of any external normative authority. ✅ The legal sovereign **can ignore and override any other considerations — prescriptions of divine law or natural law, principles of morality, dictates of public opinion**, and natural law is **not law in the proper sense of the term**. ✅ Popular sovereignty likewise **does not rely on any superior law — natural law, religious commandments or any other authority — to ascertain what is right or wrong**, and **does not look for any source of 'superior reason' apart from the mind of the people**. ⚠️ Both doctrines are therefore *self-sufficient*: each nominates a source that answers to nothing above it. ✅ The divergence is only in the nominee. ⚠️ Austin nominates a **determinate person or body of persons**, identified behaviourally by habitual obedience; popular sovereignty nominates **all members of a community** in their **corporate capacity**, treated as **the embodiment of reason** and **the best judges of right and wrong**. 🔑 Same structure, different occupant — which is why the chapter can present popular sovereignty inside the classical line rather than against it.

**Misconception to avoid:** ❌ Do not say that popular sovereignty subjects the state to a higher moral law. ⚠️ The chapter says the opposite: the doctrine refuses superior law and superior reason alike, and locates the final standard inside the people's own mind.

### Lesson Mains practice

**Mains question [10]:** What does the doctrine of popular sovereignty assert, and what does it refuse? *(Explain — answer in about 150 words.)*

**Mains model:** ✅ Two assertions. ✅ First, that **people** are **the source of all authority in the state**, popular sovereignty arising **when political sovereign comprises all members of a community**. ✅ Second, that the organs exercising **law-making, law-enforcement and adjudication** **draw their legitimacy from the will of the people** — a claim about justification, not about who exercises power. ✅ Three refusals. ⚠️ It **does not rely on any superior law — natural law, religious commandments or any other authority — to ascertain what is right or wrong**; it **does not look for any source of 'superior reason' apart from the mind of the people**; and it therefore treats the people **in their corporate capacity** as **the embodiment of reason** and **the best judges of right and wrong**. ✅ The chapter calls the result **the cornerstone of the classical theory of democracy**. ⚠️ Its self-confessed limit: in representative systems it survives for its **symbolic significance**. 🔑 A source, a legitimacy, and three refusals.

**Rubric [10]:** both assertions, with the three organs named **3** · all three refusals **3** · the corporate-capacity formulation with at least one of its three predicates **2** · the cornerstone evaluation **1** · the symbolic-significance limit **1**.

## Lesson 25 — The ancestry: Cicero, Marsiglio, Althusius

Progress: 25 / 38 | Stage: Core | Part V — popular sovereignty

━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
Book context: Core owner §6's thinker sequence, which names Rousseau as the chief exponent of popular sovereignty, and §4's spine; with the Advanced companion §5's Rousseau entry, read against the **HISTORICAL DEVELOPMENT** section of popular sovereignty spanning PDF pp.190-191 (printed 173-174), which supplies Cicero, the medieval reversal, Marsiglio of Padua and Althusius. ⚠️ Cicero, Marsiglio and Althusius appear in neither canonical owner and are restored here directly from the chapter.
CA search: "Marsiglio of Padua Defensor Pacis republicanism Althusius popular sovereignty scholarship 2025 2026"
CA found: ⚠️ nothing returned that bears on the chapter's medieval and early-modern paragraph. ❌ No linkage is carried into this lesson.
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

---

### 1. Why this lesson exists

⚠️ Three named thinkers in this section — **Cicero**, **Marsiglio of Padua** and **Althusius** — appear in neither of the canonical owners for this topic. ⚠️ They are restored here from the chapter itself, because without them the chapter's claim that popular sovereignty has **early indications... in ancient as well as medieval political thought** is an assertion with nothing behind it.

✅ The framing sentence: **Jean Jaques Rousseau (1712-78) is regarded the chief exponent of the doctrine of popular sovereignty. However, its early indications may be found in ancient as well as medieval political thought.**

⚠️ Note that the chapter spells the name **Jaques** here and **Jacques** at p.182; the inconsistency is the book's own and is recorded in the Source Ledger rather than silently harmonised.

### 2. The timeline

```text
   ANCIENT
   CICERO (106-43 B.C.), "an ancient Roman philosopher"
     postulated that the ULTIMATE SOURCE OF POLITICAL AUTHORITY
     may be traced to THE AGGREGATE PEOPLE OF A STATE
            |
            |  REVERSED: "when Roman Empire became very powerful,
            |  it was conceded that WILL OF THE EMPEROR was the
            |  source of validity of law"
            v
   MEDIEVAL
   "God as the source of all authority. Pope was his representative
    on earth."
            |
            |  CHALLENGED in the final phase of the medieval age
            v
   MARSIGLIO OF PADUA (1275-1343), "an Italian philosopher"
     Defensor Pacis (1324)
     -- papacy and clergy should submit TO THE WHOLE PEOPLE,
        "not only in temporal matters but also in spiritual matters"
     -- names the principle of supreme authority of the people
        "REPUBLICANISM"
            |
            v
   EARLY MODERN
   JOHANNES ALTHUSIUS (1557-1638), "a German jurist"
     sovereignty = the supreme power of performing those acts
     necessary for the MATERIAL AND SPIRITUAL WELFARE of members
     -- state is the product of CONTRACT or CONSENT
     -- sovereignty RESIDES IN ALL THE MEMBERS
     -- functions are DELEGATED to representatives
     -- "the FINAL AUTHORITY implied by sovereignty remains with
        the people themselves"
            |
            v
   ROUSSEAU (1712-78) -- the chief exponent
```

### 3. Cicero, and the reversal that followed

✅ Cicero **postulated that the ultimate source of political authority may be traced to the aggregate people of a state**. ⚠️ The phrase **aggregate people** is worth noticing, because it is *not* Rousseau's corporate capacity: an aggregate is a sum, a corporate body is a unity. ⚠️ The chapter does not comment on the difference, but it is the distinction that Lesson 26 turns on.

✅ The reversal is reported in one clause: **when Roman Empire became very powerful, it was conceded that will of the Emperor was the source of validity of law**. ⚠️ Read against Lesson 4, this is a miniature of the chapter's whole historical thesis — concentrations of power generate theories that justify them.

### 4. Marsiglio, and the sharpest claim in the section

⚠️ Marsiglio's argument is the most radical material in Chapter 7 and is almost never reproduced in summaries.

✅ The medieval orthodoxy: **temporal rulers should submit themselves to papacy not only in ecclesiastical matters but also in temporal matters.**

✅ Marsiglio's reversal in *Defensor Pacis* (**1324**): **papacy and clergy should submit themselves to the whole people not only in temporal matters but also in spiritual matters.**

🔑 The symmetry is exact and deliberate. ⚠️ The orthodoxy extended papal supremacy *from* the spiritual *into* the temporal; Marsiglio extends popular supremacy *from* the temporal *into* the spiritual.

✅ Three further specifics the chapter supplies:

| ✅ Claim | ⚠️ What it establishes |
|---|---|
| He **described the principle of supreme authority of the people as 'republicanism'** | A name for the doctrine, two centuries before Bodin named sovereignty |
| The powers of the priesthood should be **confined to the administration of the sacraments and the teaching of divine law**, and **even in these functions priests should be regulated and controlled by the people and their elected government** | Popular control reaches inside the religious sphere |
| **The priesthood itself should be elected by the people of each community rather than being appointed by Pope who was chosen by an oligarchy**; **the principle of legitimacy required that Pope himself should be elected by the whole of Christendom** | An electoral principle applied at every level, up to the papacy |

⚠️ The word **oligarchy** is the chapter's own characterisation of the body that chose the Pope, and the contrast it draws is between selection by a few and election by the whole.

### 5. Althusius, and the delegation structure

✅ Althusius **conceived of sovereignty as the supreme power of performing those acts which are necessary for the material and spiritual welfare of the members of a state**.

⚠️ That is a **purposive** definition — sovereignty defined by what it is for — which differs sharply from both Bodin's (a power of commanding) and Austin's (a relation of obedience).

✅ The structure then runs:

```text
   the state is the PRODUCT OF CONTRACT OR CONSENT of the people
                     |
   hence the state MUST exercise sovereignty to SECURE WELFARE
   of its members
                     |
   "In essence, sovereignty RESIDES IN ALL THE MEMBERS of a state
    although it may be EXERCISED BY A PARTICULAR BODY from time
    to time."
                     |
   since people cannot perform the functions directly,
   "they DELEGATE these functions to their representatives"
                     |
   "Still the FINAL AUTHORITY implied by sovereignty REMAINS WITH
    THE PEOPLE THEMSELVES."
                     |
   "the rulers in a democracy are required to function under the
    ULTIMATE CONTROL of the people"
```

🔑 Althusius solves, in the early seventeenth century, the problem Rousseau will later leave unsolved: how popular sovereignty can operate in a community too large for an **assembly of the whole people**. ⚠️ His answer is **delegation of functions with retention of final authority** — which is also, note, the **sovereignty/powers** distinction of Lesson 19 arriving two centuries early.

⚠️ The chapter does not draw this comparison. ⚠️ It is an inference, and a useful one in an answer, that Althusius is closer than Rousseau to the representative democracy the chapter later says is **inevitable**.

### 6. Comparison: four stages of one idea

| ✅ Thinker | ✅ Where authority ultimately lies | ⚠️ How it is exercised | ⚠️ Distinctive contribution |
|---|---|---|---|
| **Cicero** | The **aggregate people of a state** | ⚠️ Not specified in the chapter | The earliest statement; an **aggregate**, not a corporate body |
| **Marsiglio** | **The whole people** | Through **the people and their elected government**, including over clergy | Extends popular supremacy into **spiritual** matters; names it **republicanism** |
| **Althusius** | **All the members of a state** | By a **particular body from time to time**, through **delegated** functions | **Final authority remains with the people**; purposive definition of sovereignty |
| **Rousseau** | The people **in their corporate capacity** | **Only in an assembly of the whole people** | The **general will**; government reduced to **agent** |

⚠️ Reading the third column downward shows the problem getting harder, not easier: Marsiglio and Althusius both allow representation; Rousseau, the chief exponent, does not. 🔑 That is why the chapter must later concede that popular sovereignty fits only a small, uniform, direct democracy.

### 7. Criticism and reply

⚠️ **Objection.** Assembling four thinkers across nineteen centuries under one label is anachronistic. Cicero's **aggregate people**, Marsiglio's anti-papal **republicanism**, Althusius's welfare-purposive sovereignty and Rousseau's **general will** answer different questions in different vocabularies, and calling them stages of one doctrine imposes a unity the evidence does not support.

✅ **Reply.** The chapter claims only that **early indications may be found**, which is weaker than claiming a continuous tradition. ⚠️ And the indications are genuine: each of the four locates ultimate authority in the governed rather than the governor, and each therefore denies the premise on which Bodin and Austin build.

⚠️ **Residual.** The reply is sound and a real difficulty persists, which strong answers name. ⚠️ Cicero's aggregate and Rousseau's corporate body are not the same thing, and the difference is exactly what MacIver attacks at p.204 — an aggregate can be counted, a corporate will must be discovered, and **there is no homogeneous will of the people**. ⚠️ The chapter treats the two as stages of one idea without addressing the difference.

### 8. Using this in an answer

- ⚠️ **Syllabus relevance.** The ancestry supplies the depth that distinguishes a researched answer from a textbook one, and it is restored here precisely because the owners omit it.
- ⚠️ **Probable framing.** Use one or two of the three — Marsiglio's symmetry and Althusius's delegation are the most useful — rather than all three; a 10-mark answer has no room for a full genealogy.
- ❌ **Trap.** Attributing popular sovereignty wholly to Rousseau. ✅ He is **the chief exponent**, not the originator, in the chapter's own words.
- ✅ **Answer use.** Althusius's delegation structure is the best available answer to the objection that popular sovereignty cannot work at scale, and it comes from inside this chapter.

### Revision notes

1. ✅ **Rousseau (1712-78)** is **regarded the chief exponent** of popular sovereignty, but **early indications may be found in ancient as well as medieval political thought**. (PDF p.190)
2. ✅ **Cicero (106-43 B.C.)**, **an ancient Roman philosopher**, postulated that the **ultimate source of political authority may be traced to the aggregate people of a state**.
3. ✅ **When Roman Empire became very powerful, it was conceded that will of the Emperor was the source of validity of law.**
4. ✅ Medieval European thought **regarded God as the source of all authority**; the **Pope was his representative on earth**.
5. ✅ **Marsiglio of Padua (1275-1343)**, **an Italian philosopher**, challenged the absolute authority of the Pope **in order to restore the authority of the people**.
6. ✅ Orthodoxy: temporal rulers submit to papacy **not only in ecclesiastical matters but also in temporal matters**. ✅ Marsiglio, in *Defensor Pacis* (**1324**): papacy and clergy submit **to the whole people not only in temporal matters but also in spiritual matters**.
7. 🔑 He **described the principle of supreme authority of the people as 'republicanism'**.
8. ✅ Priestly powers confined to **the administration of the sacraments and the teaching of divine law**, and even there **regulated and controlled by the people and their elected government**.
9. ✅ The priesthood should be **elected by the people of each community** rather than appointed by a Pope **chosen by an oligarchy**; the Pope himself should be **elected by the whole of Christendom**.
10. ✅ **Johannes Althusius (1557-1638)**, **a German jurist**: sovereignty is **the supreme power of performing those acts which are necessary for the material and spiritual welfare of the members of a state**.
11. ✅ The state is **the product of contract or consent of the people**; sovereignty **resides in all the members of a state although it may be exercised by a particular body from time to time**.
12. 🔑 People **delegate these functions to their representatives**, yet **the final authority implied by sovereignty remains with the people themselves**; rulers **function under the ultimate control of the people**.
13. ⚠️ Althusius anticipates both representative democracy and the sovereignty/powers distinction; Rousseau, the chief exponent, allows neither.
14. ⚠️ Cicero's **aggregate** differs from Rousseau's **corporate capacity**, and MacIver's criticism exploits the difference.

### Concept check

**Question:** Althusius and Rousseau both hold that sovereignty belongs to the people. Identify the one structural difference between them, and explain why it matters for the chapter's later concession about representative democracy.

**Model answer:** ✅ The difference is representation. ⚠️ Rousseau holds that sovereignty **could be exercised only in an assembly of the whole people** and that **government is but an agent of the general will**, which confines the doctrine to a body small enough to assemble. ✅ Althusius, by contrast, holds that sovereignty **resides in all the members of a state although it may be exercised by a particular body from time to time**, that since people cannot perform the functions directly **they delegate these functions to their representatives**, and that nevertheless **the final authority implied by sovereignty remains with the people themselves**. ⚠️ That is a delegation structure — functions move, final authority does not — and it is the same distinction the chapter later uses to separate sovereignty from powers. ✅ It matters because the chapter concedes at p.193 that popular sovereignty suits only **a small and uniform community, preferably for 'direct democracy'**, and that in large societies where **representative democracy is inevitable** it survives for its **symbolic significance**. ⚠️ Althusius's structure is the resource the chapter does not use to meet its own objection.

**Misconception to avoid:** ❌ Do not treat Marsiglio as merely an anti-papal polemicist. ⚠️ The chapter credits him with naming the doctrine — he **described the principle of supreme authority of the people as 'republicanism'** — and with extending popular control into spiritual matters, including election of the priesthood and of the Pope himself.

### Lesson Mains practice

**Mains question [10]:** Trace the pre-Rousseauan development of the idea of popular sovereignty. *(Trace — answer in about 150 words.)*

**Mains model:** ✅ Three stages precede Rousseau. ✅ **Cicero (106-43 B.C.)** postulated that the **ultimate source of political authority may be traced to the aggregate people of a state**, reversed when the Empire grew powerful and **the will of the Emperor** became **the source of validity of law**. ✅ In the final medieval phase, **Marsiglio of Padua (1275-1343)**, in *Defensor Pacis* (**1324**), inverted the orthodoxy that rulers submit to the papacy in temporal as well as ecclesiastical matters, arguing that **papacy and clergy should submit themselves to the whole people not only in temporal matters but also in spiritual matters**, and naming the principle **'republicanism'**. ✅ In the early modern period, **Althusius (1557-1638)** made the state **the product of contract or consent**, held that sovereignty **resides in all the members**, and added that although functions are **delegated to their representatives**, **the final authority... remains with the people themselves**. 🔑 Source, inversion, delegation.

**Rubric [10]:** Cicero with the aggregate-people formulation and the imperial reversal **3** · Marsiglio with *Defensor Pacis*, the date and the temporal/spiritual inversion **3** · the naming of **republicanism** **1** · Althusius with consent, residence in all members and the delegation-with-retention structure **3**.
