## Lesson 35 — Laski I: the three-front attack

Progress: 35 / 38 | Stage: Advanced | Part VII — Laski, MacIver and the verdict

━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
Book context: Core owner §6's Laski entry, which enumerates the three fronts as historical, external/international and political-organisational; and the Advanced companion §7's enumeration, which gives them as history, law and political organisation. ⚠️ The two owners disagree, and the chapter's own pages settle it; the disagreement is resolved in §4 below and recorded in the Source Ledger. Read against **Harold J. Laski (1893-1950)** and **Attack on Absolute Sovereignty** at PDF pp.200-202 (printed 183-185).
CA search: "Laski rejection of absolute sovereignty world interdependence federal state critique 2025 2026"
CA found: ⚠️ nothing returned that bears on Laski's 1930s argument as the chapter presents it. ❌ No linkage is carried into this lesson.
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

---

### Part A — The man, and the change in his position

### 1. The works, and the warning

✅ The chapter introduces Laski as **the most ardent exponent of the pluralist theory of sovereignty** and immediately adds two cautions.

✅ First, dispersal: he is **a prolific writer and his views on this subject lie scattered in a large number of his works** — the chapter lists *A Grammar of Politics*, *An Introduction to Politics*, *Studies in Law and Politics*, *The Foundations of Sovereignty*, *Authority in the Modern State*, *The State in Theory and Practice* and *Reflections on the Revolution of Our Time*.

✅ Second, inconsistency: **though very brilliant, he has not been very consistent in his formulations. After adjusting his pluralist position between liberalism and Marxism, he ultimately emerged as an exponent of positive liberalism — a combination of liberalism and socialism.**

⚠️ Both cautions should appear in any Laski answer, because they explain why different textbooks attribute different positions to him.

### 2. The two phases, which examiners test

✅ The chapter distinguishes them sharply:

| ⚠️ Phase | ✅ Position |
|---|---|
| **The 1920s** | **Vigorous criticism of the theory of state sovereignty**; he **held that this theory would pass like the theory of the divine right of kings which had had its day**, and **thought of sovereignty as nothing more than a legal fiction and a barren concept** |
| **The 1930s** | He **began to evolve a balanced view of pluralism — identifying the points of its strength and weakness**, and **then he conceded the importance of sovereignty as an essential element of state-power**, though he **viewed the state itself as an agency for regulating class-relations in society**, while he **himself preferred a classless society** |

🔑 The later Laski **conceded the importance of sovereignty**. ⚠️ A candidate who presents him only as the man who called sovereignty **a legal fiction and a barren concept** has reported the 1920s and missed the mature position, which is the one the examined stems are usually about — they ask why he **rejected the absolute nature** of sovereignty, not why he rejected sovereignty.

⚠️ Note also the position he reaches: in his scheme of the **reorganization of the state on the basis of democratization of power**, Laski **comes out as an ardent pluralist**. ⚠️ Pluralism, for the mature Laski, is a programme of reorganisation rather than a denial.

### Part B — The three fronts

### 3. The attack, front by front

✅ The chapter's framing sentence: in *A Grammar of Politics* (**1938**), **Laski proceeds to scrutinize the theory of sovereignty from three aspects and discovers its weaknesses everywhere**.

```text
   FRONT 1 -- HISTORICAL ANALYSIS
   "historical analysis of the state REPUDIATES the idea of absolute
    sovereignty"
      - accepts "Sir Henry Maine's criticism of Austin"
      - shows how "CUSTOM AND TRADITION substantially limit the
        exercise of sovereign power"
      - these are "NOT legal checks imposed by some determinate human
        superior, nor do they operate with the express or tacit
        consent of the sovereign himself"
      - "the DEFERENCE which even omnipotent monarchs and sultans
        have to show to custom, is a POLITICAL NEED, a DICTATE OF
        PRUDENCE AND EXPEDIENCY"
      - "BESIDES these limitations in the INTERNAL sphere, sovereignty
        is ALSO subject to limitations in the EXTERNAL sphere"
      - externally, absolute sovereignty is "INCOMPATIBLE WITH THE
        INTERESTS OF HUMANITY":
          "In a creative civilization what is important is not the
           historical accident of separate States, but the SCIENTIFIC
           FACT OF WORLD-INTERDEPENDENCE. The real unit of allegiance
           is the world. The real obligation of obedience is to the
           total interest of our fellow-men."

   FRONT 2 -- AS A THEORY OF LAW
   "the theory of absolute sovereignty FAILS AS A THEORY OF LAW"
      - accepts "Dicey's distinction between legal and popular
        sovereignty as a PROOF OF THE ABSURDITY of Austin's definition
        of sovereignty as DETERMINATE and INDIVISIBLE"
      - then shows "how even the idea of POPULAR SOVEREIGNTY IS NOT
        WORKABLE":
          "everyone knows that to regard the King-in-Parliament as a
           sovereign body in the Austinian sense is absurd. No
           Parliament would dare to disfranchise the Roman Catholics
           or to prohibit the existence of trade unions. If it made
           the attempt, it would cease to be a Parliament. That is to
           say that in practice LEGALLY UNLIMITED POWER turns out to
           be POWER EXERCISED UNDER CONDITIONS FAIRLY WELL KNOWN TO
           EACH GENERATION."

   FRONT 3 -- POLITICAL ORGANISATION
   "the theory of sovereignty does not hold good in the analysis of a
    POLITICAL ORGANIZATION"
      - "Laski cites the case of the FEDERAL STATE, particularly of
        the UNITED STATES, to show that the LOCATION of sovereignty
        -- as envisaged by Austin -- is VERY DIFFICULT in the case of
        such political organizations."
```

🔑 Front 2 is the sharpest and is the one most often dropped. ⚠️ It does not say that sovereignty is immoral or impractical; it says the doctrine **fails as a theory of law**, which is an internal objection in Austin's own domain. ⚠️ Notice also that Laski does not merely replace the legal sovereign with the people: he shows that **even the idea of popular sovereignty is not workable**, so both candidates for the sovereign position fail.

### 4. ⚠️ Where the canonical owners disagree, and what the pages show

⚠️ The two owner files enumerate the three fronts differently, and the difference is substantive rather than stylistic.

| ⚠️ Source | ⚠️ Front 1 | ⚠️ Front 2 | ⚠️ Front 3 |
|---|---|---|---|
| **Core owner** | Historical — custom and tradition, following Maine | **External/international** — world-interdependence | Political-organisational — the federal state |
| **Advanced companion** | Historical — custom limits sovereigns | **Law** — law is not determinate and indivisible in practice | Political organisation — the federal state |

✅ The chapter's own text supports the **Advanced** enumeration. ⚠️ The external-sphere material appears **inside** front 1, introduced by the words **besides these limitations in the internal sphere, sovereignty is also subject to limitations in the external sphere**, and front 2 is opened by the distinct sentence **in the second place, the theory of absolute sovereignty fails as a theory of law**.

⚠️ The consequence matters for answers. ⚠️ On the Core owner's enumeration, Laski's second front is about interdependence — which is Chapter 9's theme and belongs to **Topic 12**. ⚠️ On the text, his second front is about the **internal coherence of the legal doctrine**, which belongs squarely here. ❌ No owner file is edited; the correction is disclosed and the chapter's pages govern.

🔑 In an answer, state the three fronts as **history**, **law** and **political organisation**, and fold the world-interdependence quotation into the historical front where the chapter puts it.

### 5. What each front is worth

| ⚠️ Front | ⚠️ Kind of objection | ⚠️ Does it defeat the firewall of Lesson 13? |
|---|---|---|
| **History** | Empirical — sovereigns are in fact restrained by custom | ❌ No. Austin conceded that the sovereign **habitually observes fundamental moral principles**; custom is of the same species |
| **Law** | Internal — the doctrine fails on its own ground | ✅ Yes. Determinacy and indivisibility are legal claims, and Laski attacks them legally |
| **Political organisation** | Descriptive — the sovereign cannot be located | ✅ Yes, partly. Location is a legal question, and the federal case is not answered by conceding morals |

🔑 This table is the most useful single thing a candidate can carry out of this lesson. ⚠️ It shows that Laski's own first front is his weakest and his second his strongest — and an answer that says so is doing evaluation rather than reporting.

⚠️ Note also the honesty of Laski's historical front. ⚠️ He does not claim that custom legally binds; he says expressly that the deference is **a political need, a dictate of prudence and expediency**. ⚠️ That concession is what makes front 1 vulnerable to Austin's firewall and what makes Laski a serious rather than a rhetorical critic.

### 6. Example, and the limit of the example

⚠️ A legislature possesses, on any legal test, the competence to abolish a long-established institution; it has never attempted this and would face collapse if it did. ⚠️ Laski's front 2 describes the situation exactly: **legally unlimited power turns out to be power exercised under conditions fairly well known to each generation**.

❌ **Where the example stops.** ⚠️ The conditions Laski names are political facts, and political facts change; a power constrained by conditions **fairly well known to each generation** is constrained differently in different generations. ⚠️ So the argument establishes that legal competence is not predictive of conduct, which is damaging, without establishing that the competence is unreal — and a defender of Austin may accept the first while denying the second. ⚠️ The chapter prints Laski's charge and does not rebut it, which is itself informative.

### 7. Criticism and reply

⚠️ **Objection.** Front 2 equivocates between two senses of "fails as a theory of law". If it means that Austin's definition misdescribes how legislatures behave, that is true and irrelevant, since a theory of legal validity is not a prediction of conduct. If it means that no determinate body possesses final legal competence, the federal case is needed to show it, and that is front 3 — so front 2 has no independent content.

✅ **Reply.** Front 2 has independent content because its target is **determinacy and indivisibility**, not behaviour. ⚠️ Laski uses Dicey's distinction as **a proof of the absurdity** of defining sovereignty as **determinate and indivisible**: if there is a sovereign behind the sovereign, the legal sovereign is not final, and finality was the point. ⚠️ And he closes the escape route by showing that **even the idea of popular sovereignty is not workable**, so the second sovereign cannot be made determinate either.

⚠️ **Residual.** The reply is strong and leaves one line open, which the chapter itself used at Lesson 23. ⚠️ If the legal and political sovereigns operate in different registers, then Dicey's two sovereigns are not two finalities and indivisibility is untouched. ⚠️ Laski's argument requires that they be competitors in the same register, and he asserts rather than argues this. ⚠️ The honest verdict is that front 2 wounds the doctrine's descriptive claims decisively and its conceptual claims only if the register distinction is refused.

### 8. Using this in an answer

- ⚠️ **Syllabus relevance.** Two of the nine primary anchors are Laski stems, both about his rejection of **absolute** sovereignty, and both are answered principally from this lesson.
- ⚠️ **Probable framing.** Give the two phases first — it immediately distinguishes a careful answer — then the three fronts in the chapter's order, then an evaluation of their relative strength.
- ❌ **Trap.** Quoting **a legal fiction and a barren concept** as Laski's settled view. ✅ That is the 1920s; by the 1930s he **conceded the importance of sovereignty as an essential element of state-power**.
- ✅ **Answer use.** The world-interdependence quotation is the most memorable sentence in Chapter 8 and should be used once, inside the historical front, with its three clauses intact.

### Revision notes

1. ✅ **Laski (1893-1950)** is **the most ardent exponent of the pluralist theory of sovereignty**; his views **lie scattered in a large number of his works**. (PDF p.200)
2. ✅ Works named: *A Grammar of Politics*, *An Introduction to Politics*, *Studies in Law and Politics*, *The Foundations of Sovereignty*, *Authority in the Modern State*, *The State in Theory and Practice*, *Reflections on the Revolution of Our Time*.
3. ✅ **Though very brilliant, he has not been very consistent in his formulations**; he **ultimately emerged as an exponent of positive liberalism — a combination of liberalism and socialism**.
4. ✅ **1920s:** sovereignty **would pass like the theory of the divine right of kings**; it is **nothing more than a legal fiction and a barren concept**.
5. 🔑 **1930s:** a **balanced view of pluralism — identifying the points of its strength and weakness** — and he **conceded the importance of sovereignty as an essential element of state-power**.
6. ✅ In *A Grammar of Politics* (**1938**) he **scrutinize[s] the theory of sovereignty from three aspects and discovers its weaknesses everywhere**.
7. ✅ **Front 1 — history:** accepts **Sir Henry Maine's criticism of Austin**; **custom and tradition substantially limit the exercise of sovereign power**; these are **not legal checks** and do not operate with the sovereign's **express or tacit consent**; the deference of **omnipotent monarchs and sultans** to custom is **a political need, a dictate of prudence and expediency**.
8. ✅ Still within front 1: **besides these limitations in the internal sphere, sovereignty is also subject to limitations in the external sphere**, where absolute claims are **incompatible with the interests of humanity**.
9. 🔑 **In a creative civilization what is important is not the historical accident of separate States, but the scientific fact of world-interdependence. The real unit of allegiance is the world. The real obligation of obedience is to the total interest of our fellow-men.**
10. ✅ **Front 2 — law:** the theory **fails as a theory of law**; Dicey's distinction is **a proof of the absurdity of Austin's definition of sovereignty as determinate and indivisible**; and **even the idea of popular sovereignty is not workable**.
11. 🔑 **To regard the King-in-Parliament as a sovereign body in the Austinian sense is absurd. No Parliament would dare to disfranchise the Roman Catholics or to prohibit the existence of trade unions. If it made the attempt, it would cease to be a Parliament.**
12. 🔑 **In practice legally unlimited power turns out to be power exercised under conditions fairly well known to each generation.**
13. ✅ **Front 3 — political organisation:** the **federal state, particularly of the United States**, shows that **the location of sovereignty — as envisaged by Austin — is very difficult**.
14. ⚠️ Owner disagreement, resolved by the pages: the fronts are **history**, **law** and **political organisation**; the external-sphere material sits **inside** front 1.

### Concept check

**Question:** Rank Laski's three fronts by how much damage each does to Austin's position as Lesson 13 reconstructed it, and justify the ranking.

**Model answer:** ⚠️ **Strongest: front 2, law.** ✅ It attacks **determinacy** and **indivisibility**, which are legal claims inside Austin's own domain, using Dicey's distinction as **a proof of the absurdity of Austin's definition**, and closing the escape route by showing that **even the idea of popular sovereignty is not workable**. ⚠️ Austin's firewall concedes morals, not legal structure, so this front is not blunted by it. ⚠️ **Second: front 3, political organisation.** ✅ The **federal state, particularly of the United States**, makes **the location of sovereignty — as envisaged by Austin — very difficult**; location is again a legal question, though a defender may reply that difficulty of identification is not impossibility. ⚠️ **Weakest: front 1, history.** ✅ Laski himself concedes that custom's restraint is **not** a legal check and is **a political need, a dictate of prudence and expediency** — which is precisely what Austin allowed when he admitted that the sovereign **habitually observes fundamental moral principles though not legally bound by any external authority**. 🔑 Ranked by domain: legal objections bite, political ones were already conceded.

**Misconception to avoid:** ❌ Do not state Laski's second front as the international one. ⚠️ The chapter places the external-sphere material **inside** the historical front, introduced by **besides these limitations in the internal sphere**, and opens front 2 with **in the second place, the theory of absolute sovereignty fails as a theory of law**; one canonical owner enumerates it differently, and the chapter's pages govern.

### Lesson Mains practice

**Mains question [15]:** Why did Laski reject the absolute nature of sovereignty? *(Explain — answer in about 250 words.)*

**Mains model:** ✅ Laski's rejection is of **absoluteness**, not of sovereignty: by the 1930s he had abandoned the 1920s view that it was **nothing more than a legal fiction and a barren concept** and **conceded the importance of sovereignty as an essential element of state-power**. ✅ In *A Grammar of Politics* he scrutinises the theory **from three aspects**.

✅ **Historically**, accepting **Sir Henry Maine's criticism of Austin**, he shows that **custom and tradition substantially limit the exercise of sovereign power**, though these are **not legal checks** and the deference shown by even **omnipotent monarchs and sultans** is **a political need, a dictate of prudence and expediency**. ⚠️ Within the same front he turns outward: absolute claims are **incompatible with the interests of humanity**, because **what is important is not the historical accident of separate States, but the scientific fact of world-interdependence**.

✅ **As a theory of law** it fails: Dicey's distinction is **a proof of the absurdity of Austin's definition of sovereignty as determinate and indivisible**, and popular sovereignty is no better, since **to regard the King-in-Parliament as a sovereign body in the Austinian sense is absurd** — **in practice legally unlimited power turns out to be power exercised under conditions fairly well known to each generation**.

✅ **In political organisation** it fails again: the **federal state, particularly of the United States**, makes **the location of sovereignty — as envisaged by Austin — very difficult**.

⚠️ The second front is the most damaging, since Austin had already conceded the moral and customary restraints the first names. 🔑 Absoluteness fails; sovereignty survives.

**Rubric [15]:** the two phases, with the 1930s concession stated **3** · front 1 with Maine, custom, and the prudence-and-expediency concession **3** · the world-interdependence quotation placed inside front 1 **2** · front 2 with Dicey's distinction and the determinate-and-indivisible target **3** · the King-in-Parliament or the conditions-fairly-well-known formulation **2** · front 3 with the federal state and the location problem **1** · a ranked evaluation rather than a list **1**.

## Lesson 36 — Laski II: state, government and federal authority

Progress: 36 / 38 | Stage: Advanced | Part VII — Laski, MacIver and the verdict

━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
Book context: Core owner §6's Laski entry, which records the moral argument that the state's powers are exercised by fallible officials and that because society is federal, authority must be federal; with the Advanced companion §5's Laski entry, read against **Distinction between the State and Government**, **Moral Grounds of Allegiance** and **Democratization of Power** at PDF pp.202-204 (printed 185-187).
CA search: "Laski public service corporation federal authority democratisation of economic power 2025 2026"
CA found: ⚠️ nothing returned that bears on Laski's constructive proposals as the chapter presents them. ❌ No linkage is carried into this lesson.
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

---

### Part A — State against government

### 1. Why the distinction is Laski's instrument and not merely his observation

✅ Laski **dwells on the distinction between the state and government to demonstrate the inadequacy of the theory of absolute and unlimited sovereignty**, and the demonstration runs through a factual premise:

> He points out that, in actual practice, the so-called sovereign powers of the state are claimed by the government, that is a body of persons issuing orders and enforcing obedience thereto. In the exercise of these powers the government can never be allowed to become absolute and irresponsible.
>
> — Gauba, Ch.8, PDF p.202 (printed 185)

⚠️ Compare Lesson 28: the chapter's own opening argument was the same. ⚠️ Laski's addition is the prescriptive clause — **can never be allowed to become absolute and irresponsible** — which converts a diagnosis into a demand.

✅ The supporting quotation, from *The State in Theory and Practice* (**1935**):

> It exists to carry out the purpose of the state. It is not itself the supreme coercive power; it is simply the mechanism of administration which gives effect to the purpose of that power. It is not, we are told, sovereign in the sense in which the state is sovereign; its competence is defined by such authority as the state may choose to confer upon it; and if it oversteps that authority it may, where such provision exists, be called to account.
>
> — Laski, quoted at Gauba, Ch.8, PDF p.202 (printed 185)

⚠️ Four claims in one passage: government **exists to carry out the purpose of the state**; it is **not itself the supreme coercive power**; its **competence is defined by such authority as the state may choose to confer upon it**; and if it oversteps, it **may, where such provision exists, be called to account**.

🔑 The final clause contains a quiet qualification that strong answers notice: **where such provision exists**. ⚠️ Accountability is not automatic; it depends on institutions having been created. ⚠️ That is exactly why Laski's next move is institutional.

### 2. The dangers passage

✅ Laski's statement of what goes wrong is comprehensive and does not rest on bad faith:

> Every government is composed of fallible men. They may deliberately exploit the authority they possess for their own selfish purposes. They may, with the best intentions, but quite unreasonably, mistake the private interest of a few for the well-being of the whole community. They may be ignorant of the position they confront, or be incompetent in handling it. Circumstances such as these have occurred in every political society at some period of its history.
>
> — Laski, quoted at Gauba, Ch.8, PDF p.202 (printed 185)

```text
   FOUR FAILURE MODES, ONLY ONE OF WHICH IS MALICE
   ------------------------------------------------
   [1] DELIBERATE EXPLOITATION   "for their own selfish purposes"
   [2] HONEST ERROR              "with the best intentions, but quite
                                  unreasonably, mistake the private
                                  interest of a few for the well-being
                                  of the whole community"
   [3] IGNORANCE                 "ignorant of the position they confront"
   [4] INCOMPETENCE              "incompetent in handling it"
                 |
   and the universality claim:
   "Circumstances such as these have occurred in EVERY POLITICAL
    SOCIETY at some period of its history."
```

🔑 Three of the four failure modes are compatible with good intentions. ⚠️ That is the argumentative point: a remedy that depends on selecting virtuous rulers cannot work, because most of the ways government fails are not failures of virtue.

✅ Hence the value of the distinction: **the possibility it offers of creating institutional mechanisms for changing the agents of the state, that is the government, when the latter shows itself inadequate to its responsibilities.**

⚠️ Set this beside Hobbes at Lesson 8. ⚠️ Hobbes's third premise was that any challenge to the sovereign risks dissolution. ⚠️ Laski's whole constructive proposal depends on denying it: agents can be changed without the state being destroyed.

### Part B — Allegiance, and the federal principle

### 3. The moral ground of allegiance

✅ From *The Foundations of Sovereignty* (**1921**), the chapter reports that Laski argues the state is **only one among many forms of human associations** and that, **as compared with other associations, it has no superior claims to an individual's allegiance**.

✅ The decisive formulation: **the state has no right to the allegiance of an individual save insofar as his conscience gives assent.**

✅ And the reason, which is developmental rather than merely libertarian: **to demand unquestioning obedience to the orders of the sovereign authority without giving the individual the right to judge for himself their moral adequacy is morally wrong; it stunts the growth of his moral personality.**

⚠️ **Stunts the growth of his moral personality** is the key phrase. ⚠️ The objection to unlimited obligation is not that it is inconvenient but that it damages persons — the same developmental argument that justified associational life at Lesson 31.

### 4. The public service corporation

✅ From *A Grammar of Politics* (**1938**): **the larger functions of the state postulate larger responsibility as well**, and the state **can justify its existence only as a 'public service corporation'**:

> The State differs from every other association in that it is, in the first place, an association in which membership is compulsory. It is, in the second place, essentially territorial in nature . . . The State controls the level at which men are to live as men. It is, in administrative terms, a government whose activities are shaped by the common needs of its members. To satisfy those common needs, it must control other associations to the degree that secures from them the service such needs require.
>
> — Laski, quoted at Gauba, Ch.8, PDF p.203 (printed 186)

⚠️ Read this passage carefully, because it is far less anti-statist than Laski's reputation suggests:

| ✅ Claim | ⚠️ What it concedes to the state |
|---|---|
| **Membership is compulsory** | A difference in kind from every voluntary association |
| **Essentially territorial in nature** | A difference in kind from every functional association |
| **Controls the level at which men are to live as men** | An extraordinarily broad remit |
| **Must control other associations to the degree that secures from them the service such needs require** | ✅ An express power of control over associations |

🔑 Laski, the most ardent pluralist, grants the state compulsory membership, territoriality, control over living standards and a power of control over other associations. ⚠️ What he withholds is only *unjustified* authority: **the state must justify exercise of its social authority by ensuring an effective coordination of functions of other human associations in the best public interest**, and **its claim to the allegiance of individuals will rest on the efficient performance of its functions**.

### 5. Because society is federal, authority must be federal

✅ The argument has three steps and a conclusion:

```text
  [1] "society is NOT a uniform organization of all citizens"
  [2] "Citizens organize themselves into several associations
       according to their specific interests"
  [3] "and these associations in turn organize themselves into society"
          => "society is an ASSOCIATION OF ASSOCIATIONS, or a
              FEDERATION of various interest groups"
                             |
  "This principle should form the basis of authority of the state."
                             |
  >>> "... because society is federal, AUTHORITY MUST BE FEDERAL
      ALSO."  (A Grammar of Politics; 1938)  <<<
                             |
  "the state should exercise its authority over the various interest
   groups which constitute it. But EACH INTEREST GROUP should IN TURN
   exercise its authority OVER ITS MEMBERS."
```

🔑 Note that at p.203 it is **society**, not the state, that Laski calls an **association of associations**; at p.197 the chapter applied the phrase to the **state**, and at p.200 Barker applied it to the state as well. ⚠️ The distinction is worth preserving in an answer: Laski infers the required *form of authority* from the *form of society*.

✅ The conclusion: **sovereignty in the state should be shared by many groups according to the respective value of the functions of each group**; **the state should perform its coordinating function, but has no right to omnipotence**; **the power of the state should become coordinative instead of being hierarchical, and authority should become federal instead of being absolute and indivisible**. ✅ And the chapter's evaluation: **Laski's plea to make authority federal is the cornerstone of his pluralist doctrine.**

⚠️ Observe which two of the five characteristics are named as targets: **absolute** and **indivisible**. ⚠️ Permanence, universality and inalienability are untouched. ⚠️ That is a precise and limited attack, and saying so is a mark of careful reading.

### Part C — Democratisation of power

### 6. The economic turn, and the four bases

✅ Laski's pluralism ends in an economic programme, and the chapter explains why: he is **deeply concerned about the undemocratic control of industry and politics by the economic overlords in society — a characteristic feature of capitalist civilization**.

✅ From *Reflections on the Revolution of Our Time* (**1943**):

> In modern society, the large industrial corporations are controlled by a caste of economic directors, mainly remarkable for their skill in financial manipulation, who are masters alike of their shareholders and of the consumers and are not seldom in a position to hold even the states to ransom. Their power is as massive in volume as it has largely been irresponsible in operation. We have reached a stage in historical evolution where either their power must be subordinated to the interest of the community or the interest of the community will be a tragic pseudonym for their power.
>
> — Laski, quoted at Gauba, Ch.8, PDF pp.203-204 (printed 186-187)

⚠️ The chapter then records a revealing move: **this state of affairs cannot be transformed, Laski feels, unless the vital instruments of production are owned and controlled by the community. But he immediately shifts from this socialist solution to an alternative scheme of democratization of power — a pluralist solution.**

🔑 **He immediately shifts.** ⚠️ The chapter is noting that Laski states a socialist remedy and does not adopt it, preferring a pluralist one. ⚠️ This is the clearest textual evidence of the positioning described at Lesson 35 — **adjusting his pluralist position between liberalism and Marxism**.

✅ The four bases of economic power:

| # | ✅ Base |
|---|---|
| 1 | **The supply of capital and credit** |
| 2 | **The ownership and control of land** |
| 3 | **Control of the import and export trades** |
| 4 | **Control of transport, fuel and power** |

✅ And the remedy: **socialization of these vital resources will avert concentration of economic power in society and start the process of the democratization of power.**

⚠️ Note the tension, which the chapter does not resolve. ⚠️ **Socialization** of four categories of resource is a substantial extension of state control, proposed by the theorist whose cornerstone is that authority should be **federal instead of being absolute and indivisible**. ⚠️ Pluralism here needs a strong state to disperse private power — which is precisely the position the chapter itself reaches at Lesson 38.

### 7. Criticism and reply

⚠️ **Objection.** Laski's constructive programme contradicts his critique. He attacks sovereign omnipotence and then grants the state compulsory membership, territoriality, control over **the level at which men are to live as men**, an express power to **control other associations**, and the socialisation of capital, land, trade and power. A state so equipped is more powerful than Austin's, not less.

✅ **Reply.** The two are consistent because Laski's target is the *basis* of authority, not its *extent*. ⚠️ The state may do a great deal, provided it does so as a **public service corporation** whose **claim to the allegiance of individuals will rest on the efficient performance of its functions**, with authority **coordinative instead of... hierarchical** and shared **according to the respective value of the functions of each group**. ⚠️ Extent follows function; what is denied is **omnipotence**, which is authority unrelated to function.

⚠️ **Residual.** The reply is principled and leaves the practical question unanswered. ⚠️ Who determines the **respective value of the functions of each group**, and who performs the **socialization**? ⚠️ On any realistic answer it is the state, which then holds the allocating power that federal authority was meant to disperse. ⚠️ The chapter's closing pages accept this outcome rather than resolve it, and that acceptance is Lesson 38.

### 8. Using this in an answer

- ⚠️ **Syllabus relevance.** The constructive half of Laski is what distinguishes an upper-band answer on the 20-mark Laski anchors; criticism alone will not reach it.
- ⚠️ **Probable framing.** State/government and its four failure modes; allegiance and conscience; the public service corporation; federal authority; democratisation of power. Five blocks, in that order.
- ❌ **Trap.** Presenting Laski as hostile to state power as such. ✅ He concedes compulsory membership, territoriality and a power to **control other associations**.
- ✅ **Answer use.** **Because society is federal, authority must be federal also** is the single sentence the chapter itself calls **the cornerstone of his pluralist doctrine**; it should appear in every Laski answer.

### Revision notes

1. ✅ **In actual practice, the so-called sovereign powers of the state are claimed by the government, that is a body of persons issuing orders and enforcing obedience thereto.** (PDF p.202)
2. ✅ Government **can never be allowed to become absolute and irresponsible**.
3. ✅ *The State in Theory and Practice* (**1935**): government **exists to carry out the purpose of the state**, is **not itself the supreme coercive power**, its **competence is defined by such authority as the state may choose to confer upon it**, and if it oversteps **it may, where such provision exists, be called to account**.
4. 🔑 Four failure modes: **deliberate exploitation**; honest error — **with the best intentions, but quite unreasonably, mistake the private interest of a few for the well-being of the whole community**; **ignorance**; **incompetence**. Only the first involves bad faith.
5. ✅ **Circumstances such as these have occurred in every political society at some period of its history.**
6. 🔑 The value of the distinction is **the possibility it offers of creating institutional mechanisms for changing the agents of the state... when the latter shows itself inadequate to its responsibilities**.
7. ✅ *The Foundations of Sovereignty* (**1921**): the state **has no superior claims to an individual's allegiance** and **has no right to the allegiance of an individual save insofar as his conscience gives assent**.
8. ✅ Unquestioning obedience without the right to judge moral adequacy **is morally wrong; it stunts the growth of his moral personality**.
9. ✅ *A Grammar of Politics* (**1938**): the state **can justify its existence only as a 'public service corporation'**; membership is **compulsory**; it is **essentially territorial**; it **controls the level at which men are to live as men**; and it **must control other associations to the degree that secures from them the service such needs require**.
10. ✅ Its **claim to the allegiance of individuals will rest on the efficient performance of its functions**.
11. ✅ Society is **an association of associations, or a federation of various interest groups**; hence **because society is federal, authority must be federal also**.
12. 🔑 Sovereignty should be **shared by many groups according to the respective value of the functions of each group**; power should become **coordinative instead of being hierarchical**, and authority **federal instead of being absolute and indivisible**. This plea is **the cornerstone of his pluralist doctrine**.
13. ✅ *Reflections on the Revolution of Our Time* (**1943**): corporations are controlled by **a caste of economic directors... masters alike of their shareholders and of the consumers**, able **to hold even the states to ransom**; their power is **as massive in volume as it has largely been irresponsible in operation**.
14. ✅ Four bases of economic power: **the supply of capital and credit**; **the ownership and control of land**; **control of the import and export trades**; **control of transport, fuel and power**. **Socialization** of these will **start the process of the democratization of power**.

### Concept check

**Question:** Laski attacks sovereign omnipotence yet grants the state very extensive powers. Show that this is consistent, and identify the practical question his position leaves open.

**Model answer:** ✅ It is consistent because Laski's target is the **basis** of authority rather than its **extent**. ⚠️ He grants that the state differs from every other association in that **membership is compulsory**, that it is **essentially territorial in nature**, that it **controls the level at which men are to live as men**, and that it **must control other associations to the degree that secures from them the service such needs require**. ✅ What he denies is authority unrelated to function: the state **can justify its existence only as a 'public service corporation'**, its **claim to the allegiance of individuals will rest on the efficient performance of its functions**, and sovereignty should be **shared by many groups according to the respective value of the functions of each group**, so that power becomes **coordinative instead of being hierarchical** and authority **federal instead of being absolute and indivisible**. ⚠️ The open question is who assigns functions. ⚠️ Someone must fix the **respective value** of each group's functions and carry out the **socialization** of capital, land, trade and power; on any realistic answer that body is the state, which then holds precisely the allocating authority federalism was meant to disperse.

**Misconception to avoid:** ❌ Do not infer from the conscience clause that Laski makes obedience optional. ⚠️ His claim is that the state has no right to allegiance **save insofar as his conscience gives assent**, which sets a condition on the *right*; it is paired with a state that enjoys compulsory membership, coercive capacity and a power of control over other associations.

### Lesson Mains practice

**Mains question [15]:** "Laski's pluralism is a programme of reorganisation rather than a denial of sovereignty." Examine. *(Examine — answer in about 250 words.)*

**Mains model:** ✅ The claim fits the mature Laski. ⚠️ Having in the 1920s called sovereignty **nothing more than a legal fiction and a barren concept**, by the 1930s he **conceded the importance of sovereignty as an essential element of state-power**, and it is in his scheme of **reorganization of the state on the basis of democratization of power** that he **comes out as an ardent pluralist**.

✅ The programme has three parts. ⚠️ First, accountability: since **every government is composed of fallible men** who may exploit authority, may **with the best intentions** mistake **the private interest of a few for the well-being of the whole community**, or may be **ignorant** or **incompetent**, the value of the state/government distinction is **the possibility it offers of creating institutional mechanisms for changing the agents of the state**.

⚠️ Second, justification: the state **can justify its existence only as a 'public service corporation'**, and its **claim to the allegiance of individuals will rest on the efficient performance of its functions** — consistent with granting it **compulsory** membership, territoriality and a power to **control other associations**.

⚠️ Third, form: **because society is federal, authority must be federal also**, so sovereignty should be **shared by many groups according to the respective value of the functions of each group**, and power should become **coordinative instead of being hierarchical**.

⚠️ Only **absolute** and **indivisible** are targeted. ⚠️ And note the unresolved question: whoever allocates functions and carries out **socialization** of capital, land, trade and power holds the authority federalism sought to disperse. 🔑 Reorganisation, not abolition.

**Rubric [15]:** the two phases with the 1930s concession **2** · at least three of the four failure modes **3** · the institutional-mechanisms formulation **2** · the public-service-corporation justification with at least two conceded state attributes **3** · the federal-authority principle quoted **2** · the identification of *absolute* and *indivisible* as the only targeted characteristics **1** · the democratisation programme with at least two of the four bases **1** · the unresolved allocation question **1**.

## Lesson 37 — MacIver: the supremacy of law

Progress: 37 / 38 | Stage: Advanced | Part VII — Laski, MacIver and the verdict

━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
Book context: Core owner §6's MacIver entry, which grounds his pluralism on three arguments — the undiscoverable general will, the supremacy of law, and the analogy with other associations; and the Advanced companion §7, which reports the three arguments differently. ⚠️ The owners disagree, and the chapter's own enumeration settles it; the disagreement is resolved in §3 below and recorded in the Source Ledger. Read against **Robert M. MacIver (1882-1970)**, **Supremacy of Law** and **Basis of Authority** at PDF pp.204-206 (printed 187-189).
CA search: "MacIver supremacy of law constitution legitimate armour of might state associations 2025 2026"
CA found: ⚠️ nothing returned that bears on MacIver's argument as the chapter presents it. ❌ No linkage is carried into this lesson.
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

---

### Part A — The sociological starting point

### 1. The works, and the method

✅ **MacIver (1882-1970)** developed his pluralistic theory in **two outstanding works**: ***The Modern State* (1926)** and ***The Web of Government* (1947/1965)**.

✅ His method is distinctive: he **takes a sociological view of the state and traces its evolution from primitive social structures to its modern form. In this process he rejects the theory of absolute sovereignty and vindicates the pluralist view of the state.**

⚠️ Note that MacIver is described as **an American sociologist** at p.199. ⚠️ Where Laski argues from political experience and Krabbe from jurisprudence, MacIver argues from the developmental history of social structures — which is why his criticisms are about what can be **discovered** and **located** rather than about what is just.

### 2. The thesis, stated at once

✅ The opening claim distinguishes the state by the wrong criterion and then supplies the right one:

> At the outset, MacIver identifies the state as one of several human associations. The state is distinguished from other associations because it gives expression to law, not because it is characterized by sovereignty.
>
> — Gauba, Ch.8, PDF p.204 (printed 187)

🔑 **Not sovereignty but law** is MacIver's whole position in four words. ⚠️ The state remains distinctive — nobody denies that — but its distinguishing mark is relocated. ⚠️ And the relocation is to something the state does not own, since on MacIver's account the state **does not create law**.

### Part B — The three arguments

### 3. ⚠️ What the three arguments actually are

⚠️ The two canonical owners enumerate MacIver's three arguments differently, and the chapter's own lettering settles the matter.

| ⚠️ Source | ⚠️ (a) | ⚠️ (b) | ⚠️ (c) |
|---|---|---|---|
| **Core owner** | The general will cannot be located | **Supremacy of law** | **Basis of authority analogous to other associations** |
| **The chapter's own lettering, p.204** | The **general will** cannot be discovered | The **ultimate sovereign** cannot be located even in a democracy | **Power is always exercised by a political government**, limited by a constitution it does not control |

✅ The chapter's text is explicit: **MacIver has advanced three arguments against the doctrine of sovereignty: (a)... (b)... and (c)...**, and **Supremacy of Law** and **Basis of Authority** are the *section headings* that follow the three arguments, not the arguments themselves.

⚠️ The correction matters because the Core owner's version omits argument (b) — the indeterminacy of the sovereign in a democracy — which is MacIver's most direct contribution to the location problem Laski raised. ❌ No owner file is edited; the chapter's pages govern.

✅ The three arguments, in full:

```text
  (a)  THE GENERAL WILL CANNOT BE DISCOVERED
       "the concept of the general will, as formulated by Rousseau
        (1712-78) and Green (1836-82), is sometimes invoked as the
        basis of sovereignty. But it is DIFFICULT TO DISCOVER THE
        EXISTENCE OF THE GENERAL WILL in the state."
         - "There are MANY PERSONS in the state who DIFFER from the
            prevalent will in the state."
         - "The states of the past included VAST NUMBERS OF ALIENS
            AND SLAVES apart from the citizens whose will could not
            be reconciled with the so-called will of the state."
         - "Besides, the state appears as an UPHOLDER OF CUSTOM and,
            therefore, loyalty to the state in such a case is derived
            from MEN'S REGARD FOR CUSTOM, NOT FOR THE STATE ITSELF."

  (b)  THE ULTIMATE SOVEREIGN CANNOT BE LOCATED
       "even in a democratic state, it is very difficult to locate
        the ultimate sovereign."
         - "There is NO HOMOGENEOUS WILL of the people."
         - "MAJORITY AND MINORITY OPINIONS EXIST SIMULTANEOUSLY."
         - "One government is replaced by another; WILL SEEMS TO GO
            ON CHANGING; POLICY ALSO CHANGES THEREWITH."

  (c)  POWER IS ALWAYS EXERCISED BY A POLITICAL GOVERNMENT
       "Limits to the power of government are set by the CONSTITUTION
        over which government HAS NO DIRECT CONTROL."
         - "The STABILITY of the authority of government depends on
            its LEGITIMACY for which it must CONFORM TO THE PREVALENT
            TRADITIONS AND SOCIAL VALUES."
```

🔑 Argument (a)'s third limb is the subtlest in Chapter 8. ⚠️ If obedience flows from regard for **custom**, then what looks like loyalty to the state is loyalty to something older that the state happens to uphold — so the state's apparent authority is borrowed. ⚠️ Note also the pairing of **Rousseau (1712-78) and Green (1836-82)**: the chapter attributes the general-will basis of sovereignty to both.

⚠️ Argument (c) inverts the classical order. ⚠️ For Austin the sovereign is the source of law; for MacIver the government's power is bounded by a **constitution over which government has no direct control**, and its **stability** depends on **legitimacy**, which depends on conformity to **prevalent traditions and social values**. ⚠️ Legitimacy is thus prior to authority — the inversion flagged at Lesson 21.

### Part C — The two positive doctrines

### 4. Supremacy of law

✅ The conclusion MacIver draws, in his own words:

> It is not the office but the instrument, not sovereignty but the law and the constitution that wear the legitimate armour of might. The government has power as the guardian of the constitution, as the executor of law, not in its own right. Outside the realm of law its use of force is as irreducible to principle as that of any strong man armed. To law, therefore, we must turn, and not to sovereignty as such, if we are to attain a true definition of the state.
>
> — MacIver, *The Modern State* (1926), quoted at Gauba, Ch.8, PDF p.205 (printed 188)

⚠️ Three distinctions are packed into the first sentence: **office** against **instrument**, **sovereignty** against **law and the constitution**, and might that is **legitimate** against might that is not.

🔑 The third sentence is the sharpest: **outside the realm of law its use of force is as irreducible to principle as that of any strong man armed.** ⚠️ A government acting beyond law is not exercising diminished authority; it is exercising none. ⚠️ Compare Austin, for whom the sovereign's command is law *because* he commands it; on MacIver's account the command is authoritative only if it is within law.

✅ The supporting claim about law's origin:

| ✅ MacIver's claim | ⚠️ What it denies |
|---|---|
| Law **cannot be regarded as an expression of the will of the state** | Austin's second mark of law |
| **The state does not create law** | Bodin's source-of-law reversal (Lesson 5) |
| **Law exists prior to the state** | That law presupposes a sovereign |
| The state **only grasps it and gives it a definite expression in the form of its statutes** | That legislation is creation |
| The state **only codifies law, or modifies its form; it cannot make or remake it** | Comprehensive legislative sovereignty |
| **The state can no more reconstitute at any time the law as a whole than a man can remake his body** | — |

✅ And the alliance: **MacIver agrees with Hugo Krabbe that the authority of law is greater than the authority of the state.**

⚠️ The body analogy repays comparison with Lieber's at Lesson 17. ⚠️ Lieber used an organic image to show that sovereignty **cannot be alienated**; MacIver uses one to show that law **cannot be remade**. ⚠️ Same rhetorical device, opposite conclusions about the state's reach.

### 5. Basis of authority: the corporation analogy

✅ MacIver's second positive doctrine derives the state's authority from the same source as any association's:

> Every association of any magnitude has grades of authority and control analogous to those of the state.
>
> — MacIver, *The Modern State*, quoted at Gauba, Ch.8, PDF p.205 (printed 188)

✅ The worked analogy:

```text
      A BUSINESS CORPORATION                 THE STATE
      ----------------------                 ---------
   a body of SHAREHOLDERS                 the WILL OF THE PEOPLE
   "united in the will and interest        -- "which is nothing more
    to uphold the corporation"                than the DOMINANT WILL
            |                                 or the MAJORITY WILL"
   must choose a BOARD OF DIRECTORS                |
            |                               GOVERNMENT
   "neither on the choice nor on the               |
    policy of the board are they            => "The state, accordingly,
    likely to be UNANIMOUS"                     DERIVES ITS AUTHORITY
                                                FROM THE WILL OF THE
                                                MAJORITY OF THE PEOPLE,
                                                NOT IN ITS OWN RIGHT."
```

🔑 The clause **not in its own right** is the conclusion of the whole analogy. ⚠️ And note the deflationary gloss on the people's will — **nothing more than the dominant will or the majority will** — which quietly completes argument (b): there is no mystical general will, only a majority, and a majority is a changeable thing.

✅ The analogy extends to legal status: **a corporation is recognized by law; it entails certain rights and obligations. The state is similar to other corporations in this sense also.**

✅ And then the decisive passage on the state's relation to other associations:

> The state gives them a special status defining their privileges and responsibilities: The state does not create the corporation but only regulates its legal character. . . The association, such as a professional group or a body of believers, exists apart from and prior to the state's act of recognition. The state cannot, for the most part, either make or unmake it ... The great associations are as native to the soil of society as the state itself. The state can scarcely even decide whether it will or will not recognize them.
>
> — MacIver, quoted at Gauba, Ch.8, PDF pp.205-206 (printed 188-189)

⚠️ **As native to the soil of society as the state itself** is the image to carry into an answer. ⚠️ It makes the equality claim vivid without asserting that associations are as powerful as the state.

### 6. The limits of coordination

✅ The chapter poses the hard question directly: **How can the state fulfil the function of control and coordination of human associations unless it enjoys supremacy over them? Does it imply that, while other associations cater to the partial interests of men, the state is the guardian and agent of the universal interests of men?**

✅ MacIver's answer, which the chapter calls **the essence of his pluralist position**:

| ✅ What the state does **not** do | ✅ What it does |
|---|---|
| **Does not regulate the internal affairs of other associations** | — |
| **Does not and cannot determine their purposes or, for the most part, their methods** | — |
| **Does not treat them as its own agencies or instruments** | — |
| — | **Comes into the picture only when the interests of one group encroach on another** |
| — | **Acts only in order to resolve the conflicting interests of various associations in society** |
| **Cannot impose its own will on human associations for the protection of the 'common interest'** | — |

✅ And the reason, in MacIver's words:

> [The state] stands for the common interests; but only so far as the common interest is sufficiently unified to admit of political expression, and only so far as it is sufficiently externalized to admit of legal regulation. True, it stands for the common interest; but not for the whole of the common interest. . . The common interest is no simple objective, attainable in its entirety by an inclusive authority. The partial interests of a thousand associations, cultural and economic, are also parts of the common interest.
>
> — MacIver, quoted at Gauba, Ch.8, PDF p.206 (printed 189)

🔑 Two conditions limit the state's standing for the common interest: it must be **sufficiently unified to admit of political expression** and **sufficiently externalized to admit of legal regulation**. ⚠️ Interests that are diffuse, or internal to a way of life, fall outside both — and those are precisely the interests associations serve.

✅ The further reason associations may outrank the state in loyalty: **other associations may cater to less extensive interests of men, yet they may attract their deeper loyalties because they are more intimate and more closely bound with the traditions and beliefs of groups.**

✅ And the warning: **on this account the doctrine of absolute sovereignty, if actually practised in the states of today with all their diversities of culture, would be fatal to the harmony of social life.**

### 7. Criticism and reply

⚠️ **Objection.** MacIver's position is unstable. He says the state **does not create law** and only **codifies** it, yet he also says the state **gives** associations **a special status defining their privileges and responsibilities** and **regulates its legal character**. Defining privileges and regulating legal character is creating law. If the state can do that, MacIver's deflation of legislation is overstated.

✅ **Reply.** The distinction MacIver needs is between *constituting* and *regulating*. ⚠️ The association **exists apart from and prior to the state's act of recognition**, and the state **cannot, for the most part, either make or unmake it**; what the state does is attach legal consequences to something it did not bring into being. ⚠️ That is consistent with his claim that the state **only grasps** law **and gives it a definite expression in the form of its statutes**.

⚠️ **Residual.** The reply works at the margin and strains in the middle. ⚠️ **For the most part** does real work in MacIver's sentence, and he does not say what happens in the remaining part. ⚠️ A body created by statute, dissolved by statute and defined wholly by statute is hard to describe as **native to the soil of society**, and the chapter neither notices the difficulty nor resolves it. ⚠️ MacIver's own later qualification, taken up at Lesson 38, is the nearest he comes to addressing which associations the state may properly reach.

### 8. Using this in an answer

- ⚠️ **Syllabus relevance.** MacIver supplies the strongest *legal* alternative to sovereignty in these chapters, and any stem on where sovereignty resides, or on monism against pluralism, is improved by him.
- ⚠️ **Probable framing.** Give the three arguments by the chapter's lettering, then the supremacy of law, then the corporation analogy, then the two conditions on the common interest.
- ❌ **Trap.** Listing MacIver's three arguments as general will, supremacy of law and associational analogy. ✅ The chapter's own lettering makes (b) the undiscoverable ultimate sovereign; supremacy of law and basis of authority are the section headings that follow.
- ✅ **Answer use.** **Not sovereignty but the law and the constitution that wear the legitimate armour of might** is the single most quotable line in Chapter 8 after Laski's federal-authority plea.

### Revision notes

1. ✅ **MacIver (1882-1970)**, **an American sociologist**, wrote ***The Modern State* (1926)** and ***The Web of Government* (1947/1965)**. (PDF pp.199, 204)
2. ✅ He **takes a sociological view of the state and traces its evolution from primitive social structures to its modern form**.
3. 🔑 The state is **distinguished from other associations because it gives expression to law, not because it is characterized by sovereignty**.
4. ✅ Argument (a): the **general will**, as formulated by **Rousseau (1712-78) and Green (1836-82)**, is **difficult to discover**; **many persons... differ from the prevalent will**; past states included **vast numbers of aliens and slaves**; and where the state is an **upholder of custom**, loyalty is **derived from men's regard for custom, not for the state itself**.
5. ✅ Argument (b): **even in a democratic state, it is very difficult to locate the ultimate sovereign**; **there is no homogeneous will of the people**; **majority and minority opinions exist simultaneously**; **will seems to go on changing; policy also changes therewith**.
6. ✅ Argument (c): **power in the state is always exercised by a political government**; limits are set **by the constitution over which government has no direct control**; **stability... depends on its legitimacy for which it must conform to the prevalent traditions and social values**.
7. 🔑 **It is not the office but the instrument, not sovereignty but the law and the constitution that wear the legitimate armour of might.**
8. ✅ Government **has power as the guardian of the constitution, as the executor of law, not in its own right**; **outside the realm of law its use of force is as irreducible to principle as that of any strong man armed**.
9. ✅ **The state does not create law. Law exists prior to the state**; the state **only grasps it and gives it a definite expression in the form of its statutes**; it **only codifies law, or modifies its form; it cannot make or remake it**.
10. ✅ **The state can no more reconstitute at any time the law as a whole than a man can remake his body.** ✅ **MacIver agrees with Hugo Krabbe that the authority of law is greater than the authority of the state.**
11. ✅ **Every association of any magnitude has grades of authority and control analogous to those of the state**: shareholders to the will of the people — **nothing more than the dominant will or the majority will** — and the board of directors to government.
12. 🔑 **The state... derives its authority from the will of the majority of the people, not in its own right.**
13. ✅ **The state does not create the corporation but only regulates its legal character**; associations **exist apart from and prior to the state's act of recognition**; **the great associations are as native to the soil of society as the state itself**.
14. ✅ The state **comes into the picture only when the interests of one group encroach on another**; it stands for the common interest **only so far as** it is **sufficiently unified to admit of political expression** and **sufficiently externalized to admit of legal regulation**, and **not for the whole of the common interest**, since **the partial interests of a thousand associations, cultural and economic, are also parts of the common interest**.
15. ✅ Absolute sovereignty, **if actually practised in the states of today with all their diversities of culture, would be fatal to the harmony of social life**.

### Concept check

**Question:** MacIver says the state is distinguished by law, not by sovereignty. Show how his three arguments support that relocation, and identify which of them is omitted by one of the canonical summaries.

**Model answer:** ✅ Each argument removes a candidate basis for sovereignty and leaves law standing. ⚠️ Argument (a) removes the **general will**: as formulated by **Rousseau and Green** it is **difficult to discover**, since **many persons... differ from the prevalent will**, past states contained **vast numbers of aliens and slaves**, and loyalty may be **derived from men's regard for custom, not for the state itself**. ⚠️ Argument (b) removes the determinate holder: **even in a democratic state, it is very difficult to locate the ultimate sovereign**, because **there is no homogeneous will of the people**, **majority and minority opinions exist simultaneously**, and **will seems to go on changing**. ⚠️ Argument (c) removes the government as a candidate, since its limits are set **by the constitution over which government has no direct control** and its stability depends on **legitimacy**. ✅ What survives is law: **not sovereignty but the law and the constitution... wear the legitimate armour of might**. ⚠️ Argument (b) is the one omitted by the Core owner's summary, which substitutes the supremacy-of-law and basis-of-authority headings for it; the chapter's own lettering governs.

**Misconception to avoid:** ❌ Do not say MacIver denies that the state makes statutes. ⚠️ His claim is narrower and stranger: the state **only codifies law, or modifies its form; it cannot make or remake it**, because **law exists prior to the state** and the state **only grasps it and gives it a definite expression in the form of its statutes**.

### Lesson Mains practice

**Mains question [15]:** Examine MacIver's case against the doctrine of sovereignty. *(Examine — answer in about 250 words.)*

**Mains model:** ✅ MacIver identifies the state as **one of several human associations** distinguished **because it gives expression to law, not because it is characterized by sovereignty**.

✅ His three arguments remove sovereignty's candidate bases. ⚠️ The **general will**, as formulated by **Rousseau and Green**, is **difficult to discover**: **many persons... differ from the prevalent will**, past states included **vast numbers of aliens and slaves**, and where the state is an **upholder of custom**, loyalty is **derived from men's regard for custom, not for the state itself**. ⚠️ Second, **even in a democratic state, it is very difficult to locate the ultimate sovereign**, since **there is no homogeneous will of the people** and **majority and minority opinions exist simultaneously**. ⚠️ Third, **power in the state is always exercised by a political government** whose limits are set **by the constitution over which government has no direct control**, its stability depending on **legitimacy**.

✅ What remains is law: **not the office but the instrument, not sovereignty but the law and the constitution... wear the legitimate armour of might**, and **outside the realm of law** its force is **as irreducible to principle as that of any strong man armed**. ✅ Law is not the state's creature — it **only codifies law... it cannot make or remake it** — and its authority is, like a corporation's, derived **from the will of the majority of the people, not in its own right**.

⚠️ The case is strong on *location* and weaker on legislation, since the state does define associations' **privileges and responsibilities**. 🔑 Law before sovereignty.

**Rubric [15]:** the distinguishing-by-law thesis **2** · argument (a) with at least two of its three limbs **3** · argument (b) with the homogeneity and simultaneity points **3** · argument (c) with the constitution and legitimacy clauses **2** · the legitimate-armour quotation **2** · the law-prior-to-the-state claim with the codification limit **2** · a qualification acknowledging the strain over statutory definition of associations **1**.
