# CUMULATIVE CONCEPT CHECKS

⚠️ Seven cumulative checks. ⚠️ Each requires material from at least three lessons, which is the skill an examiner is actually testing when a stem spans both chapters. ⚠️ They are ordered so that the first three consolidate Chapter 7, the next three consolidate Chapter 8, and the last joins them.

---

## Check A — the domain lock, traced through five uses

**Prompt:** The chapter's claim that objections arise when sovereignty is shifted out of the legal domain is made once and used repeatedly. Identify five places where the lock does work, and say what it settles in each.

**Worked response:** ⚠️ **One — the introduction (L1).** ✅ Sovereignty is **primarily a legal concept** and **all objections about sovereignty arise when this concept is shifted from the legal and juristic domain to other spheres such as moral, political, and sociological**. ⚠️ This predicts the shape of every later criticism. ⚠️ **Two — absoluteness (L14).** ✅ Absoluteness **is the logical outcome of its legal character, which may not hold good in the political sphere, nor would it govern the non-legal actions of the individuals and groups within the state** — the author conceding the pluralist opening in advance. ⚠️ **Three — Austin's firewall (L13).** ✅ The theory **solely dwells on the legal character of sovereignty** and **does not repudiate moral or social limitations**, which defeats the easy criticisms and leaves the structural ones. ⚠️ **Four — Dicey (L23).** ⚠️ The apparent conflict between a second sovereign and indivisibility dissolves because indivisibility was asserted **in the legal sense** while Dicey's sovereign is political. ⚠️ **Five — the pluralist method (L31).** ✅ Pluralism proceeds by **a shift in focus from the legal to the sociological character of the state**, so monism and pluralism are not strict contradictories.

**Trap:** ❌ Do not use the lock to dismiss pluralism as a category mistake. ⚠️ The chapter's own appraisal holds that the shift happens in practice — **the distinction between the state and government is soon forgotten or ignored** — so the political sphere must be addressed, not ruled out.

---

## Check B — one distinction, five jobs

**Prompt:** The state/government distinction is introduced to rescue a minor characteristic and ends by carrying the argument of both chapters. Trace it through five uses and state what each use achieves.

**Worked response:** ⚠️ **One — Locke (L9).** ✅ **Ruler or government could not possess supreme power**, because the people **came into existence before the formation of the state**; the distinction appears first as a limit on rulers. ⚠️ **Two — permanence (L15).** ✅ **Theoretically, sovereignty belongs to the state**, and **governments may be formed and dissolved according to established procedure** without affecting **the continuity of the state**; the distinction makes perpetuity statable. ⚠️ **Three — indivisibility (L19).** ✅ **Sovereignty vests in the state, power is assigned to the government**, so **unitary** and **federal** are **forms of government, not the forms of state**; the distinction dissolves the federal paradox. ⚠️ **Four — the appraisal (L22).** ✅ Sovereignty becomes **an attribute of the state — conceived as an abstract idea**, while government holds only **definite powers... with inbuilt checks and safeguards** so that **no organ of the government is able to become omnipotent**; the distinction becomes the remedy for the mortal sovereign. ⚠️ **Five — Laski (L36).** ✅ **Government is but the agent of the state**, and the value of the distinction is **the possibility it offers of creating institutional mechanisms for changing the agents of the state**; the distinction becomes an accountability device. 🔑 From limit, to rescue, to resolution, to remedy, to mechanism.

**Trap:** ❌ Do not treat the distinction as neutral bookkeeping. ⚠️ Chapter 7 itself admits that using it this way **would imply a departure from the original concept of sovereignty which sought to vest sovereignty in a 'determinate person or body of persons'**.

---

## Check C — the three sovereignties, and who fails which test

**Prompt:** Legal, political and popular sovereignty each claim a holder. Set out the test each would have to pass, and say which thinker shows each failing.

**Worked response:** ✅ **Legal sovereignty** must identify a **determinate person or body of persons**, **not in the habit of obedience to a like superior**, receiving **habitual obedience from the bulk of a given society**. ⚠️ Laski's second and third fronts show the test failing in modern conditions: treating **the King-in-Parliament as a sovereign body in the Austinian sense is absurd**, and in a **federal state, particularly of the United States**, the location **is very difficult**. ✅ **Political sovereignty** has no stated test: Dicey identifies it only as **another sovereign to whom the legal sovereign must bow**. ⚠️ MacIver shows it failing — **even in a democratic state, it is very difficult to locate the ultimate sovereign**, since **there is no homogeneous will of the people** and **majority and minority opinions exist simultaneously**. ✅ **Popular sovereignty** requires the people **in their corporate capacity** to be the **embodiment of reason**, which requires a discoverable general will. ⚠️ MacIver shows this failing too: it is **difficult to discover the existence of the general will in the state**, and loyalty may be **derived from men's regard for custom, not for the state itself**. 🔑 All three candidates fail a locatability test, which is why MacIver turns to **the law and the constitution**.

**Trap:** ❌ Do not conclude that sovereignty does not exist. ⚠️ Laski himself **conceded the importance of sovereignty as an essential element of state-power**, and the chapter holds that the state **cannot perform its function of regulating the life of its citizens unless it is equipped with supreme authority**.

---

## Check D — the consent manoeuvre, three appearances

**Prompt:** One device appears three times in Chapter 7 to absorb apparent limitations on sovereignty. Identify it, locate the three appearances, and state the cost it carries.

**Worked response:** ⚠️ The device is that **compliance by free consent is an exercise of sovereignty rather than a subtraction from it** — this session's label for a pattern the chapter uses without naming — and it restates Garner's **except by its own will**. ⚠️ **One — Grotius (L7).** ✅ Nations respect international law **not because it curtailed their sovereignty, but because they voluntarily chose to do so, of their free consent**, explicit in **treaties and conventions** or implicit in **usages and customs**. ⚠️ **Two — absoluteness (L14).** ✅ The sovereign may regard international law **by his free consent, not because he is obliged to obey the commands of any superior state or international organization**. ⚠️ **Three — universality (L16).** ✅ Immunity for **foreign envoys, ambassadors, their staff, residence and vehicles** is granted **because of its free consent**, is **a matter of international courtesy, not of compulsion**, and **can be withdrawn at any moment at the will of the sovereign**. ⚠️ The cost is that the device cannot distinguish consent from necessity: where refusal would be ruinous, the formal freedom persists and the substantive freedom does not. ⚠️ Laski states the general form — **legally unlimited power turns out to be power exercised under conditions fairly well known to each generation**.

**Trap:** ❌ Do not cite diplomatic immunity as a limitation on sovereignty. ⚠️ Revocability at will is precisely what converts it from a limitation into a demonstration of the power to permit.

---

## Check E — what pluralists concede, and why the concessions matter

**Prompt:** List what the pluralists, on the chapter's own account, concede to the state, and explain why an answer that omits the concessions is weaker rather than stronger.

**Worked response:** ✅ They concede, first, that the state's **jurisdiction is compulsory over all individuals and associations within its fold** and that it is **equipped with coercive powers**. ✅ Second, that the state retains **maintaining internal order and security**, **defence from external enemies** and **enforcement of voluntary contracts**. ✅ Third, that interference is proper where required **in the interest of public order, public safety or public morality**. ✅ Fourth, in Laski's hands, that the state is distinctive because **membership is compulsory**, that it is **essentially territorial in nature**, that it **controls the level at which men are to live as men**, and that it **must control other associations to the degree that secures from them the service such needs require**. ✅ Fifth, that pluralists **do not seek to abolish the state itself as anarchists do**. ⚠️ Omitting these weakens an answer because the pluralist conclusion is about the **burden of justification**, not about the absence of authority; an answer that denies the state authority has argued for anarchism and then attributed it to pluralists, which the chapter expressly forbids. 🔑 The concessions are what make the demand for justification weighty.

**Trap:** ❌ Do not write that pluralists oppose state regulation. ⚠️ The three permitted grounds are supplied by the pluralists themselves, and MacIver's later qualification positively requires regulation of **economic** associations.

---

## Check F — the chapter's two appraisals, and their two remedies

**Prompt:** Both chapters end in an appraisal. Set out the diagnosis and remedy of each, and show why the two remedies are complements rather than alternatives.

**Worked response:** ✅ **Chapter 7's appraisal.** ⚠️ Diagnosis: **all authority postulates responsibility**, yet sovereignty must be **vested in human beings who, being mortals, are as imperfect as any other human beings**, so absolute unaccountable authority produces **the most cruel and degenerate forms of autocracy, tyranny and authoritarianism, untold oppression inside the state and more destructive wars outside**. ✅ Remedy: **constitutional government** — sovereignty to the state **conceived as an abstract idea**, government holding only **definite powers... with inbuilt checks and safeguards**, so that **no organ of the government is able to become omnipotent**. ✅ **Chapter 8's appraisal.** ⚠️ Diagnosis: groups are unequal — essential-service unions can **disrupt normal social life** while **teachers, artists, scientists** cannot; **traders and workers** are better organised than **consumers**; and **giant corporations have, in fact, eclipsed other social groups**, so **public policy can hardly be regarded as an end-product of mutual adjustment of group interests**. ✅ Remedy: **a stronger base for the state sovereignty than what the pluralist theory might concede**. ⚠️ They are complements because they answer different dangers — the first the danger from the state, the second the danger from private concentrations — and only a state that is constitutionally limited in form and strong in function meets both.

**Trap:** ❌ Do not present the second remedy as a retraction of the first. ⚠️ The chapter never withdraws the duty of justification, the protection of cultural associations, or the requirement that authority be **coordinative instead of being hierarchical**.

---

## Check G — the whole topic in one question

**Prompt:** A stem asks simply: "Is sovereignty still a useful concept?" Construct the answer's spine from both chapters, naming the evidence at each step.

**Worked response:** ⚠️ **Step one — fix the domain.** ✅ Sovereignty is **primarily a legal concept**, and objections arise when it is shifted out of that domain. ⚠️ **Step two — establish necessity within the domain.** ✅ The state **cannot perform its function of regulating the life of its citizens unless it is equipped with supreme authority**, whence the five characteristics follow as **the logic of the legal character of sovereignty**. ⚠️ **Step three — state the political danger.** ✅ Sovereignty must be vested in **mortals**; monism applied politically yields **unlimited political obligation** and **complete subordination of one set of individuals to another set of similar individuals**. ⚠️ **Step four — record the two remedies.** ✅ Constitutional government with **inbuilt checks and safeguards**; and the pluralist programme — the state as **an association of associations** that coordinates **not by imposing its own will**, with authority **federal instead of being absolute and indivisible**. ⚠️ **Step five — state the limits of the second remedy.** ✅ Groups are unequal and **giant corporations have... eclipsed other social groups**, so the chapter demands **a stronger base for the state sovereignty**. ⚠️ **Step six — verdict.** 🔑 Sovereignty remains indispensable as a statement of legal finality and inadequate as a theory of political authority, and the workable position joins constitutional limitation of form with functional strength of substance.

**Trap:** ❌ Do not answer the stem by choosing a side. ⚠️ Both chapters are structured as concession-and-qualification, and an answer that mirrors that structure is both more accurate and better rewarded.

---

# ORIGINAL 10-, 15- AND 20-MARK MAINS MODEL PRACTICE

⚠️ Six original questions — two at each weight — distinct from the thirty-eight lesson-local questions above and from every stem in the PYQ section. ⚠️ Ceilings observed: **10-mark models within 150 words; 15- and 20-mark models within 250 words.** ⚠️ Each rubric is quantified and unique.

---

## Question 1 [10 marks]

**Question:** "Sovereignty is absolute in law and conditional in politics." Examine. *(Examine — answer in about 150 words.)*

**Model:** ✅ In law, sovereignty **cannot be limited or restricted by any superior power or authority**, and the sovereign is **not obliged to consult or seek the approval of any other individual or group** on whether a decision is **right or wrong** — absoluteness being **the logical outcome of its legal character**. ⚠️ In politics it is conditional, because the power **may not be interpreted as an arbitrary power**: due regard must be paid to **reason**, **conscience**, a **sense of justice**, **prevalent customs**, **social values** and **the common interest**, failing which **the legitimacy and authority of the sovereign power will be rapidly eroded and sovereignty may eventually be lost**. ⚠️ The two claims coexist because the conditions carry no legal sanction. ⚠️ The cost is that legitimacy, not legality, then explains obedience — the opening Laski exploits when he calls legal omnipotence **a purely formal concept often invalid in fact**. 🔑 Unlimited in form, contingent in fact.

**Rubric [10]:** the legal limb with the no-superior formulation and the no-consultation clause **2** · the explicit refusal of arbitrariness **1** · at least four of the six named factors **2** · the erosion clause quoted or closely reported **2** · the explanation that the conditions carry no legal sanction **2** · the Laski consequence named **1**.

---

## Question 2 [10 marks]

**Question:** Why are pluralists not anarchists? *(Explain — answer in about 150 words.)*

**Model:** ✅ Because they curtail the state rather than remove it. ✅ The chapter states that the pluralist theory **seeks to curtail or limit the absolute authority of the state as against the theory of state-sovereignty** and **does not seek to abolish the state itself as anarchists do**. ✅ Three functions are expressly retained: **maintaining internal order and security**, **defence from external enemies**, and **enforcement of voluntary contracts**, the last qualified **unless a contract is deemed to be void on moral grounds**. ✅ Positively, the state is assigned a role no anarchist could accept — it **co-ordinates the activities of these associations in society**, resolving conflicting claims **by harmonizing and coordinating their several interests** to secure the **common interest**, a role **essential for the maintenance of order in society**. ⚠️ Laski goes further, conceding compulsory membership, territoriality and a power to **control other associations**. 🔑 Limited authority, not absent authority.

**Rubric [10]:** the curtail-not-abolish formulation with the explicit anarchist contrast **3** · all three retained functions **3** · the moral-grounds qualification on contracts **1** · the coordinating role stated with the harmonising method **2** · one Laski concession used as reinforcement **1**.

---

## Question 3 [15 marks]

**Question:** "The five characteristics of sovereignty are true of a concept and false of a country." Critically examine. *(Critically examine — answer in about 250 words.)*

**Model:** ⚠️ The statement overstates, but its direction is the chapter's own. ✅ The five — **absoluteness, permanence, universality, inalienability and indivisibility** — are introduced **viewed as a purely legal concept** and derived from the state's need for **supreme authority**: a power limited by a superior is not sovereign, one held for a term implies a grantor, one with zones it may not reach is not all-comprehensive, one that can be conveyed destroys the state, and one that is shared yields **inconsistent, conflicting and ambiguous commands**.

⚠️ The chapter then concedes much of the statement. ✅ Absoluteness **may not hold good in the political sphere, nor would it govern the non-legal actions of the individuals and groups within the state**. ✅ The characteristics are **best represented by an absolute monarchy**, and in a **democratic, federal, pluralist and constitutional government** it becomes **extremely difficult to discover the seat or real character of sovereignty**.

⚠️ But *false* is too strong, and two tests show why. ✅ Altering the division of powers leaves **sovereignty... intact so long as the state maintains its independent existence**; and internationally the state remains **one entity — single and indivisible — whatever its form of government**. ⚠️ Both are observable facts about actual countries, so the characteristics are not merely conceptual.

🔑 The accurate formulation is that the five are true of every state as a legal order and unhelpful as a description of where power sits — which is exactly why Chapter 7 ends in constitutionalism and Chapter 8 in pluralism.

**Rubric [15]:** all five named, with the governing *purely legal concept* clause **3** · at least three characteristics derived rather than listed **3** · the political-sphere and non-legal-actions concession **2** · the monarchy-benchmark and seat-of-sovereignty concession **2** · both tests — alteration of powers and the international single entity **3** · a graded verdict that refuses the word *false* with a reason **2**.

---

## Question 4 [15 marks]

**Question:** Assess the claim that pluralism replaces one monopoly with several. *(Assess — answer in about 250 words.)*

**Model:** ⚠️ The claim identifies a real risk that the chapter itself raises and then answers in two different ways.

✅ Pluralism's programme invites the charge. ⚠️ It holds that **the management and control of society should be shared by several associations in proportion to their contribution to the social good**, that associations should serve as **countervailing powers in the state**, and, in Lindsay's version, that they should be **allowed to legislate for themselves**; Laski adds that sovereignty should be **shared by many groups according to the respective value of the functions of each group**.

⚠️ The chapter's own critique confirms the danger. ✅ Some groups **might become unduly powerful** and **dictate unjust terms on society**: essential-service unions can **disrupt normal social life** while **teachers, artists, scientists** cannot; **traders and workers** are **more organized and vocal** than **consumers**; and **giant corporations have, in fact, eclipsed other social groups**.

✅ Two answers are nonetheless available inside the theory. ⚠️ **Barker** already required the state to **preserve the equality of associations before law** and to **protect individuals from the possible tyranny of the group**. ⚠️ **MacIver's** later qualification distinguishes associations serving **emotional and cultural interests**, which **cannot be regulated**, from those serving **economic interests**, which **have to be regulated to serve the common interest**.

⚠️ Neither answer is sufficient without the chapter's final move: **the responsibility for protecting the common interest devolves on the state**, which must **curb the powers and influence of the more powerful organs**. 🔑 Pluralism avoids several monopolies only by retaining one strong arbiter.

**Rubric [15]:** at least two programmatic features that invite the charge, with attribution **3** · all three of the chapter's dangers, with the essential-services contrast named **4** · Barker's protective and equality duties **3** · MacIver's two-type qualification **3** · the closing recognition that a strong arbiter is required **2**.

---

## Question 5 [20 marks]

**Question:** "Both chapters diagnose the same disease and prescribe opposite medicines." Critically evaluate this reading of the relation between the concept of sovereignty and the pluralist theory. *(Critically evaluate — answer in about 250 words.)*

**Model:** ✅ The diagnosis is shared. ⚠️ Chapter 7: **all authority postulates responsibility** while sovereignty must be **vested in human beings who, being mortals, are as imperfect as any other human beings**, so that absolute unaccountable authority yields **autocracy, tyranny and authoritarianism, untold oppression inside the state and more destructive wars outside**. ⚠️ Chapter 8, in political form: **the state is represented by government which claims sovereignty on behalf of the state**, by men **who may be wise or foolish, benevolent or selfish, virtuous or vicious — but in no case perfect, divine and infallible**, implying **complete subordination of one set of individuals to another set of similar individuals**.

⚠️ The medicines differ. ✅ Chapter 7 prescribes **constitutional government** — sovereignty to the state **conceived as an abstract idea**, government holding only **definite powers... with inbuilt checks and safeguards** so that **no organ of the government is able to become omnipotent**. ✅ Chapter 8 prescribes dispersal — the state as an **association of associations**, coordinating **not by imposing its own will**, authority becoming **coordinative instead of being hierarchical** and **federal instead of being absolute and indivisible**.

❌ But *opposite* is wrong. ⚠️ Chapter 8's appraisal finds dispersal insufficient, because **giant corporations have... eclipsed other social groups** and **public policy can hardly be regarded as an end-product of mutual adjustment of group interests**, so it demands **a stronger base for the state sovereignty than what the pluralist theory might concede**.

🔑 They address different dangers — the state's power and private power — and a state limited in form yet strong in function takes both.

**Rubric [20]:** Chapter 7's diagnosis with the mortality premise and at least two named consequences **4** · Chapter 8's diagnosis with the representation substitution and the similar-individuals conclusion **4** · the constitutional remedy with the abstract-state and definite-powers contrast **3** · the pluralist remedy with at least two of its formulations **3** · the refutation of *opposite*, using Chapter 8's own three dangers **3** · the **stronger base** sentence quoted **2** · a synthesis naming the two distinct dangers **1**.

---

## Question 6 [20 marks]

**Question:** Examine whether the classical theory of sovereignty can survive the objection that its sovereign cannot be located. *(Examine — answer in about 250 words.)*

**Model:** ⚠️ The objection is made from inside the tradition before it is made from outside.

✅ The classical requirement is strict: a **determinate human superior, not in the habit of obedience to a like superior**, receiving **habitual obedience from the bulk of a given society**, **definitely organized, precise and recognized as the source of law**. ⚠️ Chapter 7 itself then concedes that in a **democratic, federal, pluralist and constitutional government** it becomes **extremely difficult to discover the seat or real character of sovereignty**.

⚠️ The objection is pressed from three directions. ✅ **Laski** holds that treating **the King-in-Parliament as a sovereign body in the Austinian sense is absurd**, and that in the **federal state, particularly of the United States**, the **location of sovereignty... is very difficult**. ✅ **MacIver** adds that **even in a democratic state, it is very difficult to locate the ultimate sovereign**, since **there is no homogeneous will of the people** and **majority and minority opinions exist simultaneously**. ⚠️ **Dicey's** political sovereign has no determinate test and is identified only by the legal sovereign's deference.

✅ Two defences survive. ⚠️ First, difficulty of location is not non-existence: the federal paradox is resolved by distinguishing **sovereignty**, which **vests in the state**, from **power**, **assigned to the government**, so that **unitary** and **federal** are **forms of government, not the forms of state**. ⚠️ Second, internationally the state remains **one entity — single and indivisible**.

🔑 The theory survives as an account of legal finality and fails as an account of where authority sits — the division of labour both chapters end by recommending.

**Rubric [20]:** the determinacy requirement stated with at least three of its elements **4** · Chapter 7's own seat-of-sovereignty concession with its four adjectives **3** · Laski's two instances **3** · MacIver's indeterminacy argument with both supporting clauses **3** · the indeterminacy of Dicey's political sovereign **2** · the sovereignty/power defence with the forms-of-government reclassification **3** · the international single-entity defence **1** · a verdict that separates legal finality from locatability **1**.

---

# REMEDIATION

⚠️ Sixty errors, grouped by where they arise. ⚠️ Each row states the error as a candidate would actually commit it, and the correction in the source's own terms wherever the source supplies them.

## Set 1 — the concept and its domain (Lessons 1-4)

| # | ❌ Error | ✅ Correction |
|---|---|---|
| 1 | ❌ "Sovereignty means the state can do anything." | ✅ It is **primarily a legal concept**, denoting supremacy **in the legal sphere**; the power **may not be interpreted as an arbitrary power** |
| 2 | ❌ "Garner said the state cannot be bound." | ✅ **Cannot be legally bound *except by its own will*** or limited by any other power than itself — the exception is the whole formula |
| 3 | ❌ "Sovereignty is about power." | ✅ It is about **final legal competence**; **power emanates from sovereignty** and is **assigned to the government** |
| 4 | ❌ "The four powers are the same thing said four ways." | ✅ Four distinct activities: **declare law**, **issue commands**, **take political decisions**, i.e. **determining public goals and their priorities**, and **command physical force to punish** |
| 5 | ❌ "Internal sovereignty means authority inside the country." | ✅ Supremacy over **all individuals and associations** — the second noun is where the examinable dispute lies |
| 6 | ❌ "Sovereign equality means states are actually equal." | ✅ **Theoretically**, each is equal **in international law**, **regardless of its population, area or economic wealth** |
| 7 | ❌ "The UN Charter limits sovereignty." | ✅ The chapter cites it the other way — the UN **is based on the principle of the sovereign equality of all its Members** and reserves a **'domestic jurisdiction'** |
| 8 | ❌ "The six restraints limit sovereignty." | ✅ They are conditions of legitimacy; neglect leaves authority **rapidly eroded** and sovereignty liable to be **eventually lost**, which is political, not legal |
| 9 | ❌ "Aristotle had an early theory of sovereignty." | ✅ He **had not given the idea**, because **the power of the ruler or ruling body was limited by the law which existed above them** |
| 10 | ❌ "The Middle Ages lacked sovereignty because rulers were weak." | ✅ Because authority was structurally limited — **by the rights of the feudal lords** below and **the claims of the Pope to superior authority** above |

## Set 2 — the classical line (Lessons 5-13)

| # | ❌ Error | ✅ Correction |
|---|---|---|
| 11 | ❌ "Bodin required a monarch." | ✅ The authority may reside **in one person or several**; what is excluded is division, not plurality |
| 12 | ❌ "Bodin's sovereign was above everything." | ✅ **Above law but not above duty and moral responsibility**, with **fundamental laws** and the natural-law protection of property surviving |
| 13 | ❌ "Bodin invented sovereignty out of nothing." | ✅ He reversed a position: law had been **part of the universal and eternal order**, **discovered by a study of custom and precedent**, since **the idea of the state as a source of law was alien to medieval thought** |
| 14 | ❌ "Grotius limited sovereignty by international law." | ✅ Nations respect it **not because it curtailed their sovereignty, but because they voluntarily chose to do so, of their free consent** |
| 15 | ❌ "Grotius based international law on consent." | ⚠️ On **two** foundations — natural law or **the dictate of right reason**, *and* a **'voluntary' law of nations** |
| 16 | ❌ "Hobbes's sovereign rests on force." | ✅ On **legitimacy**: the sovereign is **the product of the will of the people** and holds authority **for its functional value** |
| 17 | ❌ "Hobbes's people surrendered their rights to a ruler who then owed them nothing." | ⚠️ True as to irrevocability; ✅ but what was surrendered is glossed as **the right of the stronger to oppress the weaker** |
| 18 | ❌ "Locke made the government sovereign under conditions." | ✅ **Ruler or government could not possess supreme power**; supreme power stayed with **the people which came into existence before the formation of the state** |
| 19 | ❌ "Rousseau weakened sovereignty." | ✅ He relocated it; it remains **absolute and unlimited**, accepted **unconditionally in the interests of their freedom** |
| 20 | ❌ "In a democracy the elected government is sovereign." | ✅ **Government is but an agent of the general will**, and **a government cannot logically claim to hold sovereignty** |
| 21 | ❌ "Bentham limited sovereignty by law." | ✅ **Not limited by law, but... subject to moral limitations**, plus **the possibility of resistance** |
| 22 | ❌ "Bentham gave no standard for judging legislation." | ✅ **Useful legislation with the object of promoting the greatest happiness of the greatest numbers** |
| 23 | ❌ "Austin defined sovereignty as whoever is obeyed." | ✅ A **determinate human superior, *not in the habit of obedience to a like superior*** — the second clause is the test of independence |
| 24 | ❌ "Austin required universal obedience." | ✅ **Habitual obedience from the bulk of a given society** — two deliberate hedges |
| 25 | ❌ "For Austin, custom is a kind of law." | ✅ The three marks are **peculiar to the positive law of the state**, not found in **natural law, custom or religious commandments** |
| 26 | ❌ "Austin ignored morality." | ✅ He **does not repudiate moral or social limitations** and **admits that sovereign authority habitually observes fundamental moral principles** |
| 27 | ❌ "Austin glorified the state." | ✅ He **does not declare the state as a 'perfect embodiment of reason' as the idealist theory had maintained** |
| 28 | ❌ "Austin denied the sovereign any responsibility." | ✅ He **does not envisage an irresponsible sovereign**, only denies that the sovereign can be **formally made responsible to any authority similar to himself** |
| 29 | ❌ "Austin allowed subjects rights against the state." | ✅ Rights are **concessions granted by the sovereign**; **the subjects could have no legal right against the state** |
| 30 | ❌ "Monism means one strong ruler." | ✅ It means **a single sovereign in the state — a person or a body of persons**, and **a monistic view of law, state and sovereignty** |

## Set 3 — the characteristics and aspects (Lessons 14-23)

| # | ❌ Error | ✅ Correction |
|---|---|---|
| 31 | ❌ Listing the five without their governing clause. | ✅ They are described **viewed as a purely legal concept** |
| 32 | ❌ "Absoluteness is assumed." | ✅ It is **the logical outcome of its legal character** |
| 33 | ❌ "Permanence means sovereignty is eternal." | ✅ It **should not be interpreted to mean that sovereignty is eternal**; it ends with **the suzerainty of another state** |
| 34 | ❌ "Governments changing shows sovereignty changing." | ✅ **Governments may be formed and dissolved according to established procedure, but the continuity of the state will not be affected** |
| 35 | ❌ "Diplomatic immunity limits sovereignty." | ✅ Granted **because of its free consent**, it is **international courtesy, not... compulsion**, and **can be withdrawn at any moment at the will of the sovereign** |
| 36 | ❌ "Multinational corporations are outside state jurisdiction." | ✅ They are **also subject to the sovereignty of the respective states** — though Chapter 8 records their political ascendancy separately |
| 37 | ❌ Confusing inalienability with indivisibility. | 🔑 **Inalienability forbids moving sovereignty; indivisibility forbids sharing it** |
| 38 | ❌ "Cession transfers sovereignty." | ✅ **The sovereignty of a state over any area is not transferred but replaced by another state** |
| 39 | ❌ "Federalism proves divided sovereignty." | ✅ **Sovereignty vests in the state, power is assigned to the government**; **unitary** and **federal** are **forms of government, not the forms of state** |
| 40 | ❌ Ignoring the federal objection's strength. | ✅ It is supported by **Lowell**'s **two sovereigns** claim and by the **Supreme Court of America**'s formulation — concede both before answering |
| 41 | ❌ "India's three Lists divide sovereignty." | ✅ Powers are distributed; India has a **single constitution** and **residuary subjects... assigned to the Union**. ➡️ Constitutional detail belongs to the Polity owners |
| 42 | ❌ "Titular sovereignty is a weaker species of sovereignty." | ✅ The title and the thing have separated: the ruler is **only a nominal or titular head of state, without any real powers** |
| 43 | ❌ "De facto sovereignty is a settled category." | ✅ It **can only continue temporarily**, being marked by **uncertainty, confusion and irregularity** |
| 44 | ❌ Using Cromwell, Napoleon or the Bolsheviks as present-day cases. | ❌ They are historical illustrations in a **2009** text; ❌ no contemporary instance is supplied here |
| 45 | ❌ "Everyone accepts the de jure/de facto distinction." | ✅ Writers **including John Austin** refuse it; the terms should be **applied only to government, not to sovereignty** |
| 46 | ❌ "Political sovereignty and popular sovereignty are the same." | ✅ Popular sovereignty arises **when political sovereign comprises all members of a community, i.e. the people** — a species, not a synonym |

## Set 4 — popular sovereignty (Lessons 24-27)

| # | ❌ Error | ✅ Correction |
|---|---|---|
| 47 | ❌ "Popular sovereignty means the people exercise power." | ✅ It concerns **source** and **legitimacy**; the organs of **law-making, law-enforcement and adjudication** **draw their legitimacy from the will of the people** |
| 48 | ❌ "Popular sovereignty subjects the state to higher moral law." | ✅ It **does not rely on any superior law** and **does not look for any source of 'superior reason' apart from the mind of the people** |
| 49 | ❌ "Rousseau invented popular sovereignty." | ✅ He is **the chief exponent**; **early indications may be found in ancient as well as medieval political thought** |
| 50 | ❌ Omitting Marsiglio and Althusius entirely. | ✅ **Marsiglio** named the principle **'republicanism'** in *Defensor Pacis* (**1324**); **Althusius** held that **the final authority implied by sovereignty remains with the people themselves** despite delegation |
| 51 | ❌ "The general will is the majority's will." | ✅ It is the **common denominator of the real will** of **all members of the community** |
| 52 | ❌ "The general will is the sum of what people want." | ✅ The **actual will** **differs from individual to individual**; only the **real will** is **common to all members** |
| 53 | ❌ Quoting "forced to be free" without its derivation. | ✅ Acting on actual will is **against his own real interest, or against his true 'freedom'**, so restraint does not deprive him of freedom **in the real sense of the term** |
| 54 | ❌ "Popular sovereignty works in any democracy." | ✅ It suits **a small and uniform community, preferably for 'direct democracy'**; in large societies it survives **for its symbolic significance** |
| 55 | ❌ "The chapter endorses Rousseau without reservation." | ✅ He **has not drawn a clear distinction between 'will' and 'reason'**, so the doctrine **enters the realm of idealist theory which is often invoked in support of absolutism** — hence **a note of caution** |

## Set 5 — pluralism and the verdict (Lessons 28-38)

| # | ❌ Error | ✅ Correction |
|---|---|---|
| 56 | ❌ "Chapter 8 rejects the classical theory." | ✅ It opens by granting that the theory **gave the best description of the legal character of sovereignty** |
| 57 | ❌ "Pluralists deny that the state has authority." | ✅ They concede **compulsory** jurisdiction and **coercive powers**, and deny only that these **automatically establish the superior authority of the state** |
| 58 | ❌ "Pluralists speak with one voice." | ⚠️ Duguit grounds law in **social solidarity**, Krabbe in **a sense of right of the majority**; Lindsay accepts **real personality** and Barker **rejects the concept** |
| 59 | ❌ "Laski called sovereignty a legal fiction." | ⚠️ In the **1920s**; by the **1930s** he **conceded the importance of sovereignty as an essential element of state-power** |
| 60 | ❌ Ending an answer on pluralism with Laski and MacIver. | ✅ The chapter's last word corrects both: groups are unequal, **giant corporations have... eclipsed other social groups**, and the case calls for **a stronger base for the state sovereignty than what the pluralist theory might concede** |

# MASTER COMPARISON, CAUSAL AND ARGUMENT MAPS

## Map 1 — the eight thinkers, on five axes

| ✅ Thinker | ✅ Locus of sovereignty | ✅ Test or criterion | ✅ Limits admitted | ✅ Status of government | 🔑 Exam cue |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| **Bodin** (1530-96) | The commanding authority, **one person or several** | Is he the **source of law**? | **Duty and moral responsibility**; **fundamental laws**; property and consent to taxation | Above ordinary law | Early-modern state-building |
| **Grotius** (1583-1645) | The state, internally and externally | Independence **from foreign control** | **Natural law**; the **voluntary law of nations** by free consent | — | The external face completed |
| **Hobbes** (1588-1679) | The **created sovereign** | Was he created by the people's will, and does he deliver **order, peace and security**? | ⚠️ None legally; the surrender is **complete, final and irrevocable** | Dominated by sovereign authority | Security-first legitimacy |
| **Locke** (1632-1704) | **The people**, who pre-exist the state | Are **life, liberty and property** protected? | Natural rights **retained by the people** | **Could not possess supreme power** | Liberal limitation |
| **Rousseau** (1712-78) | The people **in corporate capacity**, via the **general will** | Does the general will reign? | ⚠️ None external; absolute **in the interests of their freedom** | **Agent** only; **cannot logically claim** sovereignty | Popular sovereignty |
| **Bentham** (1748-1832) | The legal sovereign, utility-tested | Does legislation promote **the greatest happiness of the greatest numbers**? | **Moral limitations**; the **possibility of resistance** | Must justify by useful legislation | The utility check |
| **Austin** (1790-1859) | A **determinate human superior** | **Habitual obedience from the bulk**, and **not... obedient to a like superior** | ❌ No legal superior; ✅ moral principles **habitually observed** | Law is sovereign command | Pure legal monism |
| **Dicey** (1885) | Two: the lawyer's sovereign, and **another sovereign to whom the legal sovereign must bow** | ⚠️ None stated for the second | — | — | Legal / political / popular |

## Map 2 — the pluralist roster, and what each denies

```text
   FORERUNNERS
   Durkheim (1858-1917)  Gierke (1841-1913)  Maitland (1850-1906)  Cole (1889-1959)
                              |
                 GIERKE-MAITLAND: groups have REAL PERSONALITY AND WILL;
                 the state's role in law-making is PRINCIPAL but
                 "BY NO MEANS EXCLUSIVE"
                              |
   =========================== EXPONENTS ===========================
                              |
   DUGUIT (1859-1928)    denies law is COMMAND
      laws = "the conditions of SOCIAL SOLIDARITY"; obligatory because
      "their observance is crucial to the functioning of human society";
      "PUBLIC SERVICE rather than sovereignty is the essential
      characteristic of the state"
                              |
   KRABBE (1857-1936)    denies sovereignty belongs to the STATE
      law is "INDEPENDENT OF AND SUPERIOR TO the state";
      "SOVEREIGNTY IS AN ATTRIBUTE OF LAW, NOT OF THE STATE";
      the state is "essentially a LEGAL COMMUNITY"
                              |
   LINDSAY (1879-1952)   denies the state's LAW-MAKING MONOPOLY
      smaller associations are "more homogeneous", attract "deeper
      loyalties", may be "more effective agencies of social
      coordination than the state itself"; should "legislate for
      themselves"
                              |
   BARKER (1874-1960)    denies REAL PERSONALITY, keeps the conclusion
      state = "an association of associations, a community of
      communities"; must "preserve the EQUALITY OF ASSOCIATIONS BEFORE
      LAW" and "PROTECT INDIVIDUALS FROM THE POSSIBLE TYRANNY OF THE
      GROUP"
                              |
   LASKI (1893-1950)     denies ABSOLUTENESS and INDIVISIBILITY
      three fronts: history | law | political organisation
      "because society is federal, AUTHORITY MUST BE FEDERAL ALSO"
                              |
   MacIVER (1882-1970)   denies that SOVEREIGNTY is the state's mark
      three arguments: general will undiscoverable | ultimate sovereign
      unlocatable | power always exercised by a constitutionally
      limited government
      "not sovereignty but THE LAW AND THE CONSTITUTION... wear the
      legitimate armour of might"
```

```text
  [1] The state must regulate the life of its citizens
            => it needs SUPREME AUTHORITY
                        |
  [2] Supreme authority, stated legally, must be

                        |

      "best represented by an ABSOLUTE MONARCHY"
                        |
  [4] In democratic / federal / pluralist / constitutional government
      the SEAT of sovereignty is "extremely difficult to discover"
                        |
          +-------------+--------------+
          |                            |
  [5a] CHAPTER 7's route        [5b] CHAPTER 8's route
  sovereignty is vested in      the state is represented by
  MORTALS => tyranny risk       GOVERNMENT => unlimited obligation
          |                            |
  [6a] REMEDY: constitutional    [6b] REMEDY: society is associational;
  government; sovereignty to     the state coordinates, justifies,
  the ABSTRACT STATE; definite   and shares authority FEDERALLY
  powers + inbuilt checks                |
          |                     [7b] BUT groups are UNEQUAL;
          |                     corporations have ECLIPSED others;
          |                     policy is NOT mutual adjustment
          |                            |
          |                     [8b] => "a STRONGER BASE for the state
          |                     sovereignty than what the pluralist
          |                     theory might concede"
          +-------------+--------------+
                        |
  [9] SYNTHESIS: constitutionally limited in FORM,
      strong enough in FUNCTION to resist private concentrations
```

## Map 4 — monism against pluralism, five axes

| ⚠️ Axis | ✅ **Monism** | ✅ **Pluralism** | ✅ Named authority on each side |
|---|---|---|---|
| **Locus of sovereignty** | **A single sovereign in the state — a person or a body of persons** | No morally omnipotent single association; the state is **one of several human associations** | Austin · MacIver |
| **Divisibility** | **A divided sovereignty is a contradiction in terms** | Authority should be **federal instead of being absolute and indivisible** | Bodin/Austin · Laski |
| **Source of obligation** | **Command** from a determinate source, **backed by sanctions** | The state has no right to allegiance **save insofar as his conscience gives assent**; limits set by **the purpose the state sought to fulfil and the judgment made by men of that purpose** | Austin · Laski |
| **Status of associations** | Subject to **universal** jurisdiction over **all individuals, groups, areas and things** | They **embody important social values prior to and independent of their authorization or approval by the state** | Gauba, Ch.7 · Gould and Kolb |
| **Account of law** | Law is the **expression of the command of the sovereign** | Law is **the product of prevailing social conditions**; **the state does not create law** | Austin · sociology, Duguit, Krabbe, MacIver |

## Map 5 — objection and reply, the eight that matter

| # | ⚠️ Objection | ✅ Reply | ⚠️ Residual |
|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | Sovereignty is a bare formality | Compulsory territorial jurisdiction plus external independence make it substantive | Formal equality ignores capacity |
| 2 | Absolute power with six "restraints" is unlimited power decorated | The restraints are conditions of **legitimacy**, and legitimacy makes obedience cheap | Then legitimacy, not legality, does the political work |
| 3 | Bodin's exceptions prove his sovereign is limited | They follow from the structure of a **perpetual** office and the natural-law status of property | No principle limits what may be called "fundamental" |
| 4 | Hobbes's irrevocable surrender destroys consent | Irrevocability is what is bought, since a revocable surrender leaves the threat standing | The deliverable includes the risk of tyranny |
| 5 | Federalism refutes indivisibility | **Sovereignty vests in the state, power is assigned to the government** | The seat of the single sovereignty is then hard to find |
| 6 | The general will licenses coercion | It is the **common denominator of the real will**, not a ruler's view | Will and reason are not clearly distinguished |
| 7 | Pluralism's associations are a social fact that proves nothing political | The inference is about the **burden of justification**, not about legal competence | Then Bentham and constitutionalism already conceded it |
| 8 | Pluralism replaces one monopoly with several | Barker's equality and protection duties; MacIver's two-type rule | Both require a strong arbiter, which is the chapter's conclusion |

## Map 6 — two words that decide most answers

```text
         "ABSOLUTE"                         "SOVEREIGNTY"
              |                                   |
   NOT "may do anything"               NOT "power" or "influence"
   NOT "unconditioned in fact"         NOT the government
              |                                   |
   = NOT SUBORDINATE to any            = FINAL LEGAL COMPETENCE,
     superior human authority            vested in the STATE
              |                                   |
   admits: regard for morals,          admits: powers assigned to
   custom, opinion, international        organs; forms of government;
   law -- all BY FREE CONSENT           delegation; federal division
              |                                   |
   fails when: shifted into the        fails when: asked to say WHO,
   political or social sphere            in a democratic / federal /
                                         pluralist / constitutional
                                         state, actually decides
```
